Day convolution for algebraic patterns
Thomas Blom, Félix Loubaton and Jaco Ruit

Abstract
. We characterize the exponentiable objects for a wide range of structures prevalent in ∞ \infty -categorical algebra, extending the construction of Day convolution to more general structures than ∞ \infty -operads. More precisely, we give a criterion that is both necessary and sufficient for many of these structures encountered in practice, such as (equivariant) ∞ \infty -operads and virtual double ∞ \infty -categories. We work within the framework of algebraic patterns of Chu–Haugseng that describe these structures in terms of weak Segal fibrations. As part of the proof, we give a new description of weak Segal fibrations in terms of generalized Segal spaces on certain “tree” categories. We also define the “underlying graph” of a weak Segal fibration, extending the notion of the underlying ∞ \infty -category for ∞ \infty -operads, and explicitly describe the underlying graph of exponential objects in weak Segal fibrations.



1.
Introduction

One of the cornerstones of modern higher algebra is the Day convolution symmetric monoidal structure on functor categories.
Given a symmetric monoidal category
(\mathscr{C},\otimes)
, Day
[
15
]
constructed a
convolution
monoidal structure
\circledast
on
\mathrm{Fun}(\mathscr{C},\mathrm{Set})
given by the coend formula

(F\circledast G)(c)=\intop\nolimits^{(c_{1},c_{2})\in\mathscr{C}\times\mathscr{C}}F(c_{1})\times G(c_{2})\times\mathrm{Hom}(c_{1}\otimes c_{2},c).

Glasman
[
17
]
considered a higher-categorical analogue of Day’s convolution monoidal structure, which was subsequently generalized to the setting of
\infty
-operads by Lurie
[
25
, §2.2.6]
and Hinich
[
21
, §2.8]
.

Given two
\infty
-operads
\mathscr{P}
and
\mathscr{Q}
, their
Day convolution
is, if it exists, an
\infty
-operad
[\mathscr{P},\mathscr{Q}]
characterized by the universal property

\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{Op}_{\infty}}(\mathscr{O}\times\mathscr{P},\mathscr{Q})\simeq\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{Op}_{\infty}}(\mathscr{O},[\mathscr{P},\mathscr{Q}]);

that is, it is an
internal hom-object
or
exponential object
in the
\infty
-category
\mathrm{Op}_{\infty}
of
\infty
-operads.
Given the importance of Day convolution in higher algebra, it is natural to wonder whether such internal hom-objects also exist for other operad-like structures, such as equivariant
\infty
-operads and virtual double
\infty
-categories.
In this paper, we characterize the
exponentiable objects
for many such operad-like structures; that is, the objects
\mathscr{P}
for which the functor
-\times\mathscr{P}
admits a right adjoint.

Our main result (
A
) is such a characterization in the context of
algebraic patterns
.
The theory of algebraic patterns, introduced by Chu–Haugseng
[
12
]
, provides a very general framework for talking about operad-like structures. An algebraic pattern is an
\infty
-category
\mathscr{O}
equipped with a factorization system
(\mathscr{O}^{\mathrm{int}},\mathscr{O}^{\mathrm{act}})
of
inert
and
active
morphisms and a full subcategory
\mathscr{O}^{\mathrm{el}}\subset\mathscr{O}^{\mathrm{int}}
of
elementary
objects. Lurie’s definition of an
\infty
-operad was generalized to any algebraic pattern in
[
12
]
by considering certain functors over
\mathscr{O}
called
weak Segal fibrations
.

Depending on the choice of algebraic pattern
\mathscr{O}
, weak Segal fibrations can describe, among others, the following operad-like structures:

•

(generalized)
\infty
-operads, as introduced by Lurie
[
25
]
,

•

non-symmetric
\infty
-operads,

•

equivariant
\infty
-operads
[
28
]
,

•

virtual double
\infty
-categories (cf.
[
16
]
, where they are called generalized non-symmetric
\infty
-operads).

Throughout this paper, and the rest of this introduction, we have decided to change the name
weak Segal fibration
to
algebrad
.
The reason is that in this paper, we will give several equivalent definitions of weak Segal fibrations/algebrads.
In these other contexts they are not necessarily a type of fibration (see e.g.
D
below), so the original terminology seemed less suitable to us.
We chose the term
algebrad
since it reflects that this notion is a generalization of an
operad
.

1.1.
A Conduché criterion for weak Segal fibrations

In
A
, we describe a criterion for detecting exponentiable objects in the
\infty
-category
\mathrm{Algad}(\mathscr{O})
of
\mathscr{O}
-algebrads
(i.e. weak Segal fibrations over
\mathscr{O}
). It is similar to the
Conduché criterion
for detecting exponentiable objects
p\colon\mathscr{C}\to\mathscr{B}
in
\mathrm{Cat}_{\infty/\mathscr{B}}
, which we now briefly recall.
Given a morphism
f
in a category
\mathscr{C}
and any factorization
p(f)\simeq g\circ h
of
p(f)
in
\mathscr{B}
, viewed as a functor
[2]\to\mathscr{B}
, we may form the
\infty
-category

\mathrm{Fact}(f\mid g\circ h)\coloneqq\mathrm{Fun}_{/\mathscr{B}}([2],\mathscr{C})\times_{\mathrm{Fun}_{/\mathscr{B}}([1],\mathscr{C})}\{f\}

of factorizations of
f
lying over the factorization
p(f)=g\circ h
. The Conduché criterion (see
[
25
, Proposition B.3.2]
or
[
4
, Lemma 2.2.8]
) now states that
p\colon\mathscr{C}\to\mathscr{B}
is exponentiable in
\mathrm{Cat}_{\infty/\mathscr{B}}
if and only if for any such
f
,
g
and
h
, the
\infty
-category
\mathrm{Fact}(f\mid g\circ h)
is weakly contractible.

Our main result is that an object
\mathscr{P}\to\mathscr{O}
in
\mathrm{Algad}(\mathscr{O})
is exponentiable if the Conduché criterion holds for a specific class of factorizations in
\mathscr{O}
.

Theorem A
.

Let
\mathscr{O}
be an algebraic pattern and
\mathscr{P}\to\mathscr{O}
an algebrad. Then
\mathscr{P}
is exponentiable, as an object in
\mathrm{Algad}(\mathscr{O})
, if the following condition is satisfied:

(1)

for any composable pair of active morphisms

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces e}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}

in
\mathscr{O}
such that
e
is an elementary object, and any lift
f
of
g\circ h
, the
\infty
-category
\mathrm{Fact}(f\mid g\circ h)
is weakly contractible.

In particular, if
\mathscr{P}\to\mathscr{O}
is exponentiable in
\mathrm{Cat}_{\infty/\mathscr{O}}
, then it is also exponentiable in
\mathrm{Algad}(\mathscr{O})
.
For
\infty
-operads,
A
was also obtained by Hinich
[
21
, §2.8]
, where he calls
\infty
-operads satisfying the condition
Item

1

flat
.
However, even in this case our proof is completely different and in particular does not rely on the model-categorical arguments from
[
25
, Appendix B]
; see
Section

1.4
for a detailed comparison between our proof and previous proof strategies.

A natural follow-up question is whether this criterion is also necessary; that is, whether
A
can be upgraded to an “if and only if” statement.
Under a condition on
\mathscr{O}
called
robustness
(see
Section

7.2
), our methods will show that this is indeed the case.

Theorem B
.

Let
\mathscr{O}
be a robust algebraic pattern. Then any exponentiable object in
\mathrm{Algad}(\mathscr{O})
satisfies condition
Item

1
from
A
.

In
Section

7.4
, we show that there exist robust algebraic patterns
\mathscr{O}
such that
\mathrm{Algad}(\mathscr{O})
is equivalent to the
\infty
-categories of

•

\infty
-operads,

•

equivariant
\infty
-operads, and

•

virtual double
\infty
-categories.

For all of these
\infty
-categories, we then obtain a complete characterization of their exponentiable objects, which will be discussed in
Section

5.5
and
Section

8.5
. We could not find a proof of similar characterizations in the current literature; see
Section

1.4
for a thorough overview of what was known before.

Remark 1.1
.

A
also provides a criterion for when a morphism
\mathscr{Q}\to\mathscr{P}
in
\mathrm{Algad}(\mathscr{O})
is exponentiable.
To see this, note that by a proof similar to
[
5
, Corollary 4.1.17]
, the category
\mathscr{P}
admits an algebraic pattern structure such that
\mathrm{Algad}(\mathscr{O})_{/\mathscr{P}}\simeq\mathrm{Algad}(\mathscr{P})
.
From this it follows that
\mathscr{Q}\to\mathscr{P}
is exponentiable in
\mathrm{Algad}(\mathscr{O})
if and only if it is exponentiable as an object in
\mathrm{Algad}(\mathscr{P})
, to which we can apply
A
.
In fact, in our proof of
A
we directly characterize the exponentiable morphisms in
\mathrm{Algad}(\mathscr{O})
, and not just the exponentiable objects.

1.2.
The underlying graph of an exponential object

An important feature of Day convolution for symmetric monoidal
\infty
-categories is that the underlying
\infty
-category of the
\infty
-operad
[\mathscr{C}^{\otimes},\mathscr{D}^{\otimes}]
is simply the functor category
\mathrm{Fun}(\mathscr{C},\mathscr{D})
.
This fact generalizes to algebrads.

To any algebrad
\mathscr{P}\to\mathscr{O}
over an algebraic pattern
\mathscr{O}
, one can assign its
underlying graph
, which is a functor
\Gamma\mathscr{P}\colon\mathscr{O}^{\mathrm{el}}\to\mathrm{Cat}_{\infty}
generalizing the notion of
underlying
\infty
-category
for
\infty
-operads.
The
\infty
-category
\mathrm{Fun}(\mathscr{O}^{\mathrm{el}},\mathrm{Cat}_{\infty})
is cartesian closed, and it turns out exponential objects in
\mathrm{Algad}(\mathscr{O})
are compatible with the internal hom
[-,-]
of
\mathrm{Fun}(\mathscr{O}^{\mathrm{el}},\mathrm{Cat}_{\infty})
.
More precisely, we show the following.

Theorem C
.

Let
\mathscr{O}
be an algebraic pattern and let
\mathscr{P}
be exponentiable in
\mathrm{Algad}(\mathscr{O})
.
Then for any algebrad
\mathscr{Q}\to\mathscr{O}
, the canonical comparison map

\Gamma[\mathscr{P},\mathscr{Q}]\to[\Gamma\mathscr{P},\Gamma\mathscr{Q}]

is an equivalence.

1.3.
Algebrads as complete Segal presheaves on the tree category

Our proof of
A
and
B
uses an alternative description of the
\infty
-category
\mathrm{Algad}(\mathscr{O})
of
\mathscr{O}
-algebrads which is of independent interest.
Namely, we show that to any algebraic pattern, one can associate a certain
\infty
-category of
layered trees

\Omega[\mathscr{O}]
such that
\mathrm{Algad}(\mathscr{O})
is equivalent to the
\infty
-category of
complete Segal
presheaves on
\Omega[\mathscr{O}]
.
1
1

1

Our choice of terminology and notation is based on the theory of dendroidal sets, introduced in
[
27
]
as a model for
\infty
-operads. However, applying our construction to the category
\mathbb{F}_{*}
of finite pointed sets yields the category
\Omega[\mathbb{F}_{*}]
of
layered
trees, which admits a map to the Moerdijk–Weiss tree category
\Omega
but is not equivalent to it.

The objects in
\Omega[\mathscr{O}]
are strings
t_{0}\rightsquigarrow\cdots\rightsquigarrow t_{n}
of active morphisms in
\mathscr{O}
such that
t_{n}
is elementary.
A morphism between two such strings
t_{0}\rightsquigarrow\cdots\rightsquigarrow t_{n}
and
s_{0}\rightsquigarrow\cdots\rightsquigarrow s_{m}
consists of a map
\phi\colon[n]\to[m]
in
\Delta
together with a diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{t_{0}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{t_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{t_{n-1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{t_{n}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{s_{\phi(0)}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{s_{\phi(1)}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{s_{\phi(n-1)}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{s_{\phi(n)}}}

in
\mathscr{O}
whose vertical morphisms are inert.
A presheaf on
\Omega[\mathscr{O}]
is called
Segal
if for any string
t_{0}\rightsquigarrow\cdots\rightsquigarrow t_{n}
, the Segal map

X(t_{0}\rightsquigarrow\cdots\rightsquigarrow t_{n})\to X(t_{0}\rightsquigarrow t_{1})\times_{X(t_{1})}\cdots\times_{X(t_{n-1})}X(t_{n-1}\rightsquigarrow t_{n})

is an equivalence. We say that such an
X
is
complete
if for any elementary object
e
in
\mathscr{O}
, the Segal space

[n]\mapsto X(\underbrace{e=\cdots=e}_{n\text{--times}})

is complete.
We write
\mathrm{CSeg}(\Omega[\mathscr{O}])\subseteq\mathrm{PSh}(\Omega[\mathscr{O}])
for the full subcategory of complete Segal presheaves.

Theorem D
.

Let
\mathscr{O}
be an algebraic pattern.
Then there is a natural equivalence

\mathrm{Algad}(\mathscr{O})\simeq\mathrm{CSeg}(\Omega[\mathscr{O}]).

Concretely, the equivalence
\mathrm{Algad}(\mathscr{O})\to\mathrm{CSeg}(\Omega[\mathscr{O}])
is given by a
nerve
construction with respect to a certain functor
\Omega[\mathscr{O}]\to\mathrm{Algad}(\mathscr{O})
; see
Section

4.4
for details.
Similar equivalences were studied by Barwick
[
7
]
in the context of operator categories and by Kern
[
23
]
for a special class of algebraic patterns called
combinatorial
algebraic patterns.

In practice, we will demonstrate
D
by passing through an auxiliary gadget. Using Juran’s double
\infty
-categorical perspective on factorization systems
[
22
]
, we may equivalently view every algebraic pattern
\mathscr{O}
as a particular double
\infty
-category
\mathscr{O}^{\mathrm{dbl}}
with a distinguished class of elementary objects. This intermediate double
\infty
-categorical point of view on algebraic patterns will turn out to be convenient to simplify arguments and formulate statements throughout the paper. For instance, we may construct the tree
\infty
-category
\Omega[\mathscr{O}]
by unstraightening the underlying functor
\Delta^{\mathrm{op}}\to\mathrm{Cat}
of
\mathscr{O}^{\mathrm{dbl}}
.

The
\infty
-category
\Omega[\mathscr{O}]
behaves quite similarly to the simplex category
\Delta
.
This will allow us to prove
A
and
B
by a strategy similar to that of Ayala–Francis
[
4
, Lemma 2.2.8]
.

In fact, we will demonstrate that
\Omega[\mathscr{O}]^{\mathrm{op}}
can again be given the structure of an algebraic pattern. The complete Segal presheaves on
\Omega[\mathscr{O}]
are precisely the Segal objects for this pattern, in the sense of Chu–Haugseng
[
12
]
, that admit an additional univalence condition. In particular, this gives a way to iterate the tree construction (see
Section

3.1
) and to produce a tower of (non-full) inclusions

\mathrm{Algad}(\mathscr{O})\to\mathrm{Algad}(\Omega[\mathscr{O}]^{\mathrm{op}})\to\mathrm{Algad}({}^{2}[\mathscr{O}]^{\mathrm{op}})\to\dotsb.

1.4.
Relation to other results

Exponentiability in the
\infty
-category of
\infty
-operads was studied by Lurie
[
25
, §2.2.6]
and Hinich
[
21
, §2.8]
, who proved
A
in this setting.
This was subsequently generalized by Nardin–Shah
[
28
, §3]
, who proved a version of
A
in the setting of
G
-
\infty
-operads.
In
[
13
]
, Chu–Haugseng prove a version of Day convolution for
cartesian
algebraic patterns
\mathscr{O}
when the source is an
\mathscr{O}
-monoidal
\infty
-category and the target is the
\infty
-category of spaces.
Our
A
generalizes all these cases.
Moreover, to our knowledge there is no place in the literature that establishes
necessary
conditions for exponentiability (our
B
).
We will now compare our proof strategy to that of Lurie, Hinich and Nardin–Shah and explain why the same strategy cannot be used to prove a result at the level of generality of
A
.

The proof strategies of Nardin–Shah and Hinich are essentially the same as that of Lurie: they verify that the conditions of
[
25
, Theorem B.4.2]
are satisfied.
Roughly, this goes as follows:
Given a map of
\infty
-operads
f\colon\mathscr{Q}\to\mathscr{P}
, one obtains a pullback functor

f^{*}\colon\mathrm{Cocart}^{\mathrm{int}}(\mathscr{P})\to\mathrm{Cocart}^{\mathrm{int}}(\mathscr{Q})

where
\mathrm{Cocart}^{\mathrm{int}}(-)
denotes the sub-
\infty
-category of
(\mathrm{Cat}_{\infty})_{/-}
spanned by functors that admit cocartesian lifts of inerts, and maps between them that preserve these.
One easily observes that
f^{*}
takes
\infty
-operads over
\mathscr{P}
to
\infty
-operads over
\mathscr{Q}
.
Moreover, the functor
f^{*}
admits a right adjoint
f_{*}\colon\mathrm{Cocart}^{\mathrm{int}}(\mathscr{Q})\to\mathrm{Cocart}^{\mathrm{int}}(\mathscr{P})
if and only if
\mathscr{Q}^{\mathrm{act}}\to\mathscr{P}^{\mathrm{act}}
is exponentiable (cf.
Section

9.2
).
The exponentiability of
f\colon\mathscr{Q}\to\mathscr{P}
is then proved by verifying that this right adjoint
f_{*}
takes
\infty
-operads over
\mathscr{Q}
to
\infty
-operads over
\mathscr{P}
.

Observe that this strategy can only work if
\mathscr{Q}^{\mathrm{act}}\to\mathscr{P}^{\mathrm{act}}
is exponentiable, because otherwise the right adjoint
f_{*}\colon\mathrm{Cocart}^{\mathrm{int}}(\mathscr{Q})\to\mathrm{Cocart}^{\mathrm{int}}(\mathscr{P})
does not exist.
However, our main result
A
only requires the Conduché criterion for pairs of maps
x\rightsquigarrow y\rightsquigarrow e
that end with an elementary object, not for all maps in
\mathscr{P}^{\mathrm{act}}
.
In the case of
\infty
-operads, these conditions are equivalent by
[
21
, Lemma 2.8.2]
, but Hinich’s argument does not go through for most other algebraic patterns.
In
Section

9.3
, we give an explicit counterexample of a map between virtual double
\infty
-categories
\mathscr{Q}\to\mathscr{P}
that is exponentiable but for which
\mathscr{Q}^{\mathrm{act}}\to\mathscr{P}^{\mathrm{act}}
is not exponentiable in
\mathrm{Cat}
.
In particular, the proof strategy of Lurie, Nardin–Shah and Hinich cannot be used to characterize all exponentiable morphisms between virtual double
\infty
-categories.
(It is also worth pointing out that condition (5) of
[
25
, Theorem B.4.2]
does not hold for most algebraic patterns.)

Instead, our strategy is to write
\mathrm{Algad}(\mathscr{O})
as the localization
\mathrm{CSeg}(\Omega[\mathscr{O}])
of a presheaf
\infty
-category (
D
).
Since any morphism
f\colon X\to Y
in a presheaf
\infty
-category is exponentiable, we lose the requirement that
\mathscr{Q}^{\mathrm{act}}\to\mathscr{P}^{\mathrm{act}}
is exponentiable this way.
We then prove
A
by studying when the right adjoint
f_{*}\colon\mathrm{PSh}(\Omega[\mathscr{O}])_{/X}\to\mathrm{PSh}(\Omega[\mathscr{O}])_{/Y}
preserves complete Segal objects.

Finally, let us mention that similar results exist in the strict categorical setting. Day introduced
promonoidal symmetric monoidal categories
in
[
15
]
. These structures were characterized by Pisani as operads that satisfy a 1-categorical version of condition
Item

1
, and moreover, Pisani showed these are precisely the exponentiable operads, thereby proving a version of
B
for ordinary operads
[
30
]
. In the setting of ordinary virtual double categories, it was recently shown by Arkor
[
1
]
that all pseudo double categories are exponentiable as virtual double categories. More generally, a characterization of all exponentiable virtual double categories was recently announced by Thompson
[
34
]
.
The
\infty
-categorical versions of these statements are a consequence of
A
and
B
, see also
Section

9.1
.
We suspect that versions of
A
and
B
in the strict setting can be deduced by restricting to truncated objects in
\mathrm{Seg}(\Omega[\mathscr{O}])
or
\mathrm{CSeg}(\Omega[\mathscr{O}])
, but we will not pursue this here.

1.5.
Outline of the paper

In
Section

2
, we recall some basic facts on algebraic patterns and discuss our main examples.
We then reformulate this theory in terms of double
\infty
-categories, using Juran’s
[
22
]
equivalence between
\infty
-categories equipped with a factorization system and factorization double
\infty
-categories.
In
Section

3
, we define the category of trees
\Omega[\mathscr{O}]
for any algebraic pattern and study its basic properties.
Here we also define robustness for algebraic patterns and show that most of our examples satisfy this property.
Combining the results from
Section

2
and
Section

3
, we establish
D
in
Section

4
.
We then provide sufficient conditions for the exponentiability of objects in
\mathrm{CSeg}(\Omega[\mathscr{O}])
in
Section

5
, exploiting the simplicial nature of the category
\Omega[\mathscr{O}]
.
Using
D
, we translate this characterization back to the setting of algebrads over
\mathscr{O}
and establish
A
.
In
Section

6
, we study the underlying graphs of our exponential objects and prove
C
.
After that, we shift our attention to proving
B
. As a preliminary step, we introduce
robust
algebraic patterns in
Section

7
. We then demonstrate
B
in
Section

8
. We work out condition (CC) for various examples of patterns in
Sections

5.5
and
8.5
.
We conclude the paper with the short
Section

9
where we discuss a few examples of exponentiable
\mathscr{O}
-algebrads.
In particular, we show that to any
\infty
-category
\mathscr{C}
one can associate its
virtual
cospan double
\infty
-category
\mathbb{C}\mathrm{ospan}(\mathscr{C})
(even if
\mathscr{C}
does not admit pushouts) and that
\mathbb{C}\mathrm{ospan}(\mathscr{C})
is always an exponentiable object in the category of virtual double
\infty
-categories.

1.6.
Future work

In a planned future work, we aim to use the results of this paper to prove a universal property of mapping out of virtual double
\infty
-categories of cospans.
The goal is to establish the same property as was obtained by Dawson–Paré–Pronk in the strict setting
[
14
]
. Our strategy involves exponentiating double
\infty
-categories by virtual cospan double
\infty
-categories. As a stepping stone, we already show in
Section

9.4
that virtual cospan double
\infty
-categories are indeed exponentiable.

The latter two authors are developing the basic aspects of category theory for algebrads in a sequel to this work. The Day convolution of algebrads proven here, can be used to define presheaf algebrads. One may then show a suitable version of the Yoneda lemma, and develop a theory of Kan extensions and cocompletions for algebrads. When specializing the theory to the pattern describing operads, the operadic Kan extensions of Lurie
[
25
, §3.1]
will be recovered.

Conventions

Throughout the rest of this article, we will make use of the following conventions:

(1)

From now on, we will drop the ‘
\infty
’ symbol from our notation. For example, we will refer to
\infty
-categories as categories, to
\infty
-operads as operads and to
(\infty,n)
-categories as
n
-categories.

(2)

The category of spaces or (
\infty
-)groupoids is denoted by
\mathscr{S}
.

(3)

If
\mathscr{C}
is a category, then we will write
\mathrm{PSh}(\mathscr{C})\coloneqq\mathrm{Fun}(\mathscr{C}^{\mathrm{op}},\mathscr{S})
for the category of presheaves on
\mathscr{C}
.

(4)

If
\mathscr{C}
is a category, then we will write
\mathrm{Ar}(\mathscr{C})\coloneqq\mathrm{Fun}([1],\mathscr{C})
for the category of arrows in
\mathscr{C}
.

(5)

If
x
and
y
are objects of a category
\mathscr{C}
, then we write
\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathscr{C}}(x,y)
or
\mathrm{Hom}(x,y)
for the space of maps from
x
to
y
.

(6)

If
\mathscr{C}
is a category that admits cartesian products and
c
is an object in
\mathscr{C}
such that
c\times-
admits a right adjoint, then we will denote this right adjoint by
[c,-]
and call it an
internal hom-object
or
exponential object
.

(7)

Given a sequence
t=(t_{0}\to\cdots\to t_{n})
of composable maps in a category, we will write
t_{i-1}
for the
i
-th object of this sequence. Given
0\leq i\leq j\leq n
, we will write
t_{i,j}
for the subsequence
(t_{i}\to\cdots\to t_{j})
, and we write
t_{\leq i}
for
(t_{0}\to\cdots\to t_{i})
and
t_{\geq j}
for
(t_{j}\to\cdots\to t_{n})
.

(8)

Suppose that we are given a commutative square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d}

in a category
\mathscr{C}
. If
\mathscr{C}
has pullbacks, then the unique map
a\to b\times_{d}c
will be called the
gap
map associated to the square. Dually, if
\mathscr{C}
has pushouts, then the unique map
b\cup_{a}c\to d
will be called the
cogap
map associated to the square.

2.
Algebrads

We commence by discussing the notions that play a key role throughout this article.

2.1.
Recollections on algebraic patterns

We briefly recall the theory of algebraic patterns. For details, the reader is referred to
[
12
,
5
]
.

Definition 2.1
.

An
algebraic pattern
is a category
\mathscr{O}
equipped with a factorization system
(\mathscr{O}^{\mathrm{int}},\mathscr{O}^{\mathrm{act}})
and a full subcategory
\mathscr{O}^{\mathrm{el}}
of
\mathscr{O}^{\mathrm{int}}
. We will write
\mathrm{AlgPatt}
for the category of algebraic patterns.

Notation 2.2
.

We will use the following notation and terminology:

•

The morphisms in
\mathscr{O}^{\mathrm{int}}
and
\mathscr{O}^{\mathrm{act}}
will be called
inert
and
active
and often be denoted by
\rightarrowtail
and
\rightsquigarrow
, respectively.

•

The objects in
\mathscr{O}^{\mathrm{el}}
are called
elementary
.

•

Given an object
x
in
\mathscr{O}
, we will write
\mathscr{O}^{\mathrm{el}}_{x/}
for the fiber product
\mathscr{O}^{\mathrm{el}}\times_{\mathscr{O}}\mathscr{O}_{x/}
.

•

The full subcategories of
\mathrm{Ar}(\mathscr{O})
spanned by the inert and active morphisms will be denoted
\mathrm{Ar}_{\mathrm{int}}(\mathscr{O})
and
\mathrm{Ar}_{\mathrm{act}}(\mathscr{O})
, respectively.

Definition 2.3

(Chu–Haugseng)
.

Let
\mathscr{O}
be an algebraic pattern. A functor
p\colon\mathscr{P}\to\mathscr{O}
is called an
\mathscr{O}
-algebrad
, or
algebrad
for short, if the following conditions hold:

(1)

p
has cocartesian lifts of inert morphisms in
\mathscr{O}
,

(2)

for all
x\in\mathscr{O}
, the canonical functor

\mathscr{P}_{x}\to\lim_{(x\rightarrowtail e)\in\mathscr{O}^{\mathrm{el}}_{x/}}\mathscr{P}_{e}

is an equivalence,

(3)

for any
x,y
in
\mathscr{O}
, any
\overline{x}\in\mathscr{P}_{x}
and any
\overline{y}\in\mathscr{P}_{y}
, the square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathscr{P}}(\overline{x},\overline{y})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\lim\limits_{(\phi\colon y\rightarrowtail e)\in\mathscr{O}^{\mathrm{el}}_{y/}}\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathscr{P}}(\overline{x},\phi_{!}\overline{y})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathscr{O}}(x,y)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\lim\limits_{(\phi\colon y\rightarrowtail e)\in\mathscr{O}^{\mathrm{el}}_{y/}}\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathscr{O}}(x,e)}}

is a pullback square.

Remark 2.4
.

In the work of Chu and Haugseng, algebrads were originally introduced as
weak Segal fibrations

[
12
, Definition 9.6]
. However, in what follows we will give several equivalent definitions of algebraic patterns and of algebrads. In these other contexts, it seemed to us that the original terminology was less suitable. We therefore chose to modify it, and the term
algebrad
seemed to us to be an appropriate choice, as it reflects that this notion is a generalization of an
operad
.

Definition 2.5
.

If
\mathscr{P}\to\mathscr{O}
is an algebrad, then
\mathscr{P}
inherits the structure of an algebraic pattern whose inert morphisms are the cocartesian lifts of inert morphisms of
\mathscr{O}
. The active morphisms (resp. elementary objects) are the ones lying over active morphisms (resp. elementary objects) of
\mathscr{O}
. This was explained in
[
12
, Lemma 9.10]
. We write

\mathrm{Algad}(\mathscr{O})\subset\mathrm{AlgPatt}_{/\mathscr{O}}

for the full subcategory spanned by the
\mathscr{O}
-algebrads in this sense.

Example 2.6
.

We have the following examples of algebrads:

•

Operads
, as introduced by Lurie
[
25
]
, are precisely the algebrads for the algebraic pattern structure on
\mathbb{F}_{*}
with the inerts and actives as in
[
25
, §2.1.1]
and elementaries given by
\{\left<1\right>\}
. We will denote this algebraic pattern by
\mathbb{F}_{*}^{\flat}
.

•

If we add the additional elementary
\left<0\right>
to
\mathbb{F}_{*}
, then we obtain a pattern which we will denote by
\mathbb{F}_{*}^{\natural}
. The algebrads for this pattern are
generalized operads

[
25
, §2.3.2]
.

•

The category
\Delta^{\mathrm{op}}
can be given the structure of an algebraic pattern where the inert morphisms are the inclusions of convex subsets, the actives are the morphisms that preserve the minimal and maximal elements, and the only elementary object is
[1]
. Algebrads for this pattern are
non-symmetric operads
(see
[
16
, Definition 3.1.3]
and
[
25
, Definition 4.1.3.2]
).
This algebraic pattern structure will be denoted
\Delta^{\mathrm{op},\flat}
.

•

Choosing the elementaries of
\Delta^{\mathrm{op}}
to be
\{[0],[1]\}
instead, its algebrads are known as
virtual double categories
(called
generalized non-symmetric operads
in
[
16
]
).
This pattern structure will be denoted
\Delta^{\mathrm{op},\natural}
to distinguish it from the previous one.

•

Let
\mathbb{F}_{G}
be the category of finite
G
-sets for
G
a finite group. The (2,1)-category
\mathrm{Span}(\mathbb{F}_{G})
of spans in
\mathbb{F}_{G}
admits the structure of an algebraic pattern whose inert and active maps are of the form

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}

and

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}

respectively, where the
\cong
-marked maps are isomorphisms. Its elementary objects are the transitive
G
-sets, i.e. the
G
-orbits
. Then as remarked in
[
5
, Observation 5.2.12]
, the algebrads for this pattern deserve to be called
G
-operads
. We will write
\mathrm{Span}(\mathbb{F}_{G})^{\flat}
for this pattern structure.

•

We will also consider the pattern structure
\mathrm{Span}(\mathbb{F})^{\natural}
on
\mathrm{Span}(\mathbb{F})
with the same actives and inerts as above (taking
G
to be trivial), and where the elementary objects are the finite sets of cardinality at most
1
.
We will see in
Section

8.5
below that the inclusion
\mathbb{F}_{*}\hookrightarrow\mathrm{Span}(\mathbb{F})
induces an equivalence
\mathrm{Algad}(\mathbb{F}_{*}^{\natural})\simeq\mathrm{Algad}(\mathrm{Span}(\mathbb{F})^{\natural})
, so algebrads for this pattern also describe generalized operads. (This also follows by applying
[
5
, Theorem A]
to
\mathbb{F}_{*}^{\natural}\hookrightarrow\mathrm{Span}(\mathbb{F})^{\natural}
.)

Remark 2.7
.

If every object in
\mathscr{O}
is elementary, then
\mathscr{P}\to\mathscr{O}
is an
\mathscr{O}
-algebrad precisely if it admits cocartesian lifts of inerts.
In this case
\mathrm{Algad}(\mathscr{O})
is equivalent to the subcategory
\mathrm{Cocart}^{\mathrm{int}}(\mathscr{O})\hookrightarrow\mathrm{Cat}_{/\mathscr{O}}
whose objects are the functors
\mathscr{C}\to\mathscr{O}
that admit cocartesian lifts over
\mathscr{O}^{\mathrm{int}}
and whose morphisms are those functors that preserve cocartesian lifts over
\mathscr{O}^{\mathrm{int}}
.

We also recall the following notion from
[
12
]
:

Definition 2.8
.

Let
\mathscr{C}
be a limit complete category. A functor
\mathscr{P}:\mathscr{O}\to\mathscr{C}

is called a
Segal
\mathscr{O}
-object
in
\mathscr{C}
if the canonical map

\mathscr{P}(x)\to\lim_{(x\rightarrowtail e)\in\mathscr{O}^{\mathrm{el}}_{x/}}\mathscr{P}(e)

is an equivalence. We will write
\mathrm{Seg}(\mathscr{O},\mathscr{C})\subset\mathrm{Fun}(\mathscr{O},\mathscr{C})
for the full subcategory spanned by the Segal
\mathscr{O}
-objects.
If
\mathscr{C}=\mathrm{Cat}
, then Segal
\mathscr{O}
-objects are referred to as
Segal
\mathscr{O}
-categories
.

It follows from
[
12
, Remark 9.11]
that the Grothendieck construction
\mathrm{Fun}(\mathscr{O},\mathrm{Cat})\to\mathrm{Cat}_{/\mathscr{O}}
restricts to a functor

\mathrm{Seg}(\mathscr{O},\mathrm{Cat})\to\mathrm{Algad}(\mathscr{O})
that selects the (non-full) subcategory spanned by the algebrads
\mathscr{P}\to\mathscr{O}
that are cocartesian fibrations, and maps between algebrads that preserve cocartesian arrows.

Example 2.9
.

We have the following examples of Segal categories:

(1)

Symmetric monoidal categories
are the Segal categories for the pattern
\mathbb{F}_{*}
.

(2)

Monoidal categories
are the Segal categories for the pattern
\Delta^{\mathrm{op},\flat}
.

(3)

Double categories
are the Segal categories for the pattern
\Delta^{\mathrm{op},\natural}
; see also
Section

2.2
.

(4)

Symmetric monoidal
G
-categories
are the Segal categories for the pattern
\mathrm{Span}(\mathbb{F}_{G})^{\flat}
; see
[
28
, §2.3]
and
[
5
, §5.2]
.

2.2.
Algebraic patterns as double categories

In this subsection, we will introduce a double categorical perspective on the previous theory.

Definition 2.10
.

A
double category
is a functor
\mathscr{P}\colon\Delta^{\mathrm{op}}\to\mathrm{Cat}
such that for any
n
, the functor

\mathscr{P}_{n}\to\mathscr{P}_{1}\times_{\mathscr{P}_{0}}\dotsb\times_{\mathscr{P}_{0}}\mathscr{P}_{1}

is an equivalence.
A double category
\mathscr{P}
will be called
complete
if moreover the functor

\mathscr{P}_{0}\to\mathscr{P}_{3}\times_{\mathscr{P}_{\{0\leq 2\}}\times\mathscr{P}_{\{1\leq 3\}}}(\mathscr{P}_{0}\times\mathscr{P}_{0})

is an equivalence.
The objects and morphisms of
\mathscr{P}_{0}
are called the
objects
and
vertical morphisms
of
\mathscr{P}
. The objects and morphisms of
\mathscr{P}_{1}
are referred to as the
horizontal morphisms
and
2-cells
of
\mathscr{P}
.
The full subcategory of
\mathrm{Fun}(\Delta^{\mathrm{op}},\mathrm{Cat})
of double categories will be denoted
\mathrm{DblCat}
.

Example 2.11
.

Let
\mathscr{C}
be a category. The
double category
\mathrm{Sq}(\mathscr{C})
of squares in
\mathscr{C}
is defined by

\mathrm{Sq}(\mathscr{C})_{n}\coloneqq\mathrm{Fun}([n],\mathscr{C}).

We recall the following definition of Juran
[
22
]
:

Definition 2.12

(Juran)
.

A complete double category
\mathscr{P}
is called a
factorization double category
if the target map
t\colon\mathscr{P}_{1}\to\mathscr{P}_{0}
is a left fibration. In other words, if every solid diagram as pictured below

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{y^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces z}

can be extended to the dashed 2-cell in an essentially unique way.

Remark 2.13
.

We have swapped the roles of the vertical and the horizontal morphisms with respect to the definition of a factorization double category given in
[
22
]
.
This convention will be important in
Section

3
when we define the forest and tree categories of an algebraic pattern.

Remark 2.14
.

If
\mathscr{P}
is a factorization double category, then the Segal condition implies that for every
n\geq 1
, the target map
\mathscr{P}_{n}\to\mathscr{P}_{0}
induced by
\{n\}\hookrightarrow[n]
is a left fibration.
In particular, for any
t=(t_{0}\to t_{1}\to\cdots\to t_{n})
in
\mathscr{P}_{n}
, the target projection
(\mathscr{P}_{n})_{t/}\to(\mathscr{P}_{0})_{t_{n}/}
is an equivalence.

The name for these structures is inspired by the following result.

Construction 2.15
.

Let
(\mathscr{C},\mathscr{C}_{L},\mathscr{C}_{R})
be a factorization system. We denote by
\mathrm{Sq}_{L,R}(\mathscr{C})
the sub double category of
\mathrm{Sq}(\mathscr{C})
whose vertical arrows are contained in
\mathscr{C}_{L}
and whose horizontal arrows are contained in
\mathscr{C}_{R}
.

Proposition 2.16

(Juran)
.

The construction
(\mathscr{C},\mathscr{C}_{L},\mathscr{C}_{R})\mapsto\mathrm{Sq}_{L,R}(\mathscr{C})
defines a fully faithful functor
\mathrm{Sq}_{L,R}\colon\mathrm{Fact}\to\mathrm{DblCat}
, where
\mathrm{Fact}
is the category of factorization systems.
Its essential image consists precisely of the factorization double categories.

Proof.

This is
[
22
, Theorem 3.19]
.
∎

This motivates the following definition.

Definition 2.17
.

A
double pattern

\mathscr{O}
is a factorization double category together with a full subcategory
\mathscr{O}_{0}^{\mathrm{el}}\subset\mathscr{O}_{0}
, called the
elementaries
. In this case, the vertical arrows of
\mathscr{O}
are called
inert
and denoted
\rightarrowtail
, and the horizontal arrows are called
active
and denoted
\rightsquigarrow
.

For any
n\geq 0
, we define

\mathscr{O}_{n}^{\mathrm{el}}
as the full subcategory of
\mathscr{O}_{n}
fitting in the pullback

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathscr{O}_{n}^{\mathrm{el}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathscr{O}_{n}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathscr{O}_{0}^{\mathrm{el}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathscr{O}_{0}},}

where the vertical maps are induced by the inclusion
\{n\}\hookrightarrow[n]
.
The pair
(\mathscr{O}_{n},\mathscr{O}_{n}^{\mathrm{el}})
defines an algebraic pattern where every map is inert.

Remark 2.18
.

It follows immediately that for any
t
in
\mathscr{O}_{n}
, the equivalence
(\mathscr{O}_{n})_{t/}\to(\mathscr{O}_{0})_{t_{n}/}
from
Section

2.2
restricts to an equivalence
(\mathscr{O}_{n}^{\mathrm{el}})_{t/}=(\mathscr{O}_{0}^{\mathrm{el}})_{t_{n}/}
.

The following is a direct consequence of
Section

2.2
:

Corollary 2.19
.

The functor
\mathrm{Sq}_{L,R}
induces an equivalence between the category
\mathrm{AlgPatt}
of algebraic patterns and the category
\mathrm{DblPatt}
of double patterns. ∎

Notation 2.20
.

We will denote this equivalence by

(-)^{\mathrm{dbl}}:\mathrm{AlgPatt}\simeq\mathrm{DblPatt}:(-)^{\mathrm{alg}}.

2.3.
Algebrads in the double categorical context

Let us start with defining the analogue of algebrads in the setting of double patterns.

Definition 2.21
.

Let
\mathscr{O}
be a double pattern. A functor
p\colon\mathscr{P}\to\mathscr{O}
is called an
\mathscr{O}
-algebrad
, or
algebrad
for short, if

(1)

for every
n\geq 0
, the functor
p_{n}\colon\mathscr{P}_{n}\to\mathscr{O}_{n}
is a left fibration,

(2)

for any
t
in
\mathscr{O}_{0}
, the morphism

(\mathscr{P}_{0})_{t}\to\lim_{(t\rightarrowtail s)\in(\mathscr{O}_{0})^{\mathrm{el}}_{t/}}(\mathscr{P}_{0})_{s}

is an equivalence,

(3)

for any
t
in
\mathscr{O}_{1}
, the square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(\mathscr{P}_{1})_{t}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\lim\limits_{(t\rightarrowtail s)\in(\mathscr{O}_{1})^{\mathrm{el}}_{t/}}(\mathscr{P}_{1})_{s}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(\mathscr{P}_{0})_{t_{0}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\lim\limits_{(t\rightarrowtail s)\in(\mathscr{O}_{1})^{\mathrm{el}}_{t/}}(\mathscr{P}_{0})_{s_{0}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d_{1}}

is cartesian.

Remark 2.22
.

To see how this square is constructed, let
P_{n}\colon\mathscr{O}_{n}\to\mathscr{S}
denote the functors classifying
p_{n}\colon\mathscr{P}_{n}\to\mathscr{O}_{n}
.
The commutative square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathscr{P}_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathscr{P}_{0}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathscr{O}_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathscr{O}_{0}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d_{1}}

then induces maps
(\mathscr{P}_{1})_{t}=P_{1}(t)\to P_{0}(d_{1}(t))=(\mathscr{P}_{0})_{t_{0}}
that are natural in
t\in\mathscr{O}_{1}
.
The horizontal maps in the square of the condition
(3)
of
Section

2.3
are obtained by restricting and right Kan extending along
\mathscr{O}^{\mathrm{el}}_{1}\hookrightarrow\mathscr{O}_{1}
.

Definition 2.23
.

If
\mathscr{P}\to\mathscr{O}
is an algebrad, then
\mathscr{P}
inherits the structure of a double pattern where
\mathscr{P}^{\mathrm{el}}
consists of the objects lying over an elementary in
\mathscr{O}
. We write

\mathrm{Algad}(\mathscr{O})\subset\mathrm{DblPatt}_{/\mathscr{O}}

for the full subcategory spanned by the
\mathscr{O}
-algebrads in this sense.

We will now compare algebrads for double patterns and algebrads for algebraic patterns.

Lemma 2.24
.

Let
p\colon\mathscr{P}\to\mathscr{O}
be an algebrad. Then for every
n\geq 1
and
t
in
\mathscr{O}_{n}
, the square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(\mathscr{P}_{n})_{t}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\lim\limits_{(t\rightarrowtail s)\in(\mathscr{O}_{n})^{\mathrm{el}}_{t/}}(\mathscr{P}_{n})_{s}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(\mathscr{P}_{n-1})_{t_{\leq n-1}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\lim\limits_{(t\rightarrowtail s)\in(\mathscr{O}_{n})^{\mathrm{el}}_{t/}}(\mathscr{P}_{n-1})_{s_{\leq n-1}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d_{n}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d_{n}}

is a pullback square. Here, we denote by
t_{\leq n-1}
the image of an element
t\in\mathscr{P}_{n}
under the morphism
d_{n}\colon\mathscr{P}_{n}\to\mathscr{P}_{n-1}
.

Proof.

Consider the squares

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(\mathscr{P}_{n})_{t}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\lim\limits_{(t\rightarrowtail s)\in(\mathscr{O}_{n})^{\mathrm{el}}_{t/}}(\mathscr{P}_{n})_{s}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\lim\limits_{(t\rightarrowtail s)\in(\mathscr{O}_{n})^{\mathrm{el}}_{t/}}(\mathscr{P}_{1})_{s_{\geq n-1}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(\mathscr{P}_{n-1})_{t_{\leq n-1}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\lim\limits_{(t\rightarrowtail s)\in(\mathscr{O}_{n})^{\mathrm{el}}_{t/}}(\mathscr{P}_{n-1})_{s_{\leq n-1}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\lim\limits_{(t\rightarrowtail s)\in(\mathscr{O}_{n})^{\mathrm{el}}_{t/}}(\mathscr{P}_{0})_{s_{n-1}}}.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d_{n}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d_{n}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}

The right square is cartesian since
\mathscr{P}\to\mathscr{O}
is a functor of double categories, so it suffices to show that the outer rectangle is cartesian. Observe that this is also the outer rectangle in

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(\mathscr{P}_{n})_{t}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(\mathscr{P}_{1})_{t_{\geq n-1}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\lim\limits_{(t\rightarrowtail s)\in(\mathscr{O}_{n})^{\mathrm{el}}_{t/}}(\mathscr{P}_{1})_{s_{\geq n-1}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(\mathscr{P}_{n-1})_{t_{\leq n-1}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(\mathscr{P}_{0})_{t_{n-1}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\lim\limits_{(t\rightarrowtail s)\in(\mathscr{O}_{n})^{\mathrm{el}}_{t/}}(\mathscr{P}_{0})_{s_{n-1}}}.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d_{n}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d_{n}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}

The left square is cartesian since
\mathscr{P}
is a double category, while the right square is cartesian since
\mathscr{P}\to\mathscr{O}
is an algebrad.
∎

Notation 2.25
.

Given an algebraic pattern
\mathscr{O}
and a map
f\colon x\to y
in
\mathscr{O}
, we will write
f^{\mathrm{int}}
and
f^{\mathrm{act}}
for the inert and active parts of its inert-active factorization. Given a functor
\mathscr{P}\to\mathscr{O}
and
\overline{x}\in\mathscr{P}_{x}
,
\overline{y}\in\mathscr{P}_{y}
, we write

\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathscr{P}}^{f}(\overline{x},\overline{y})\coloneqq\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathscr{P}}(\overline{x},\overline{y})\times_{\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathscr{O}}(x,y)}\{f\}.

Lemma 2.26
.

Let
\mathscr{O}
be an algebraic pattern and
\mathscr{P}\to\mathscr{O}
a functor satisfying the conditions
(1)
and
(2)
of
Section

2.1
. Then
\mathscr{P}\to\mathscr{O}
is an algebrad if and only if for any active morphism
f\colon x\to y
in
\mathscr{O}
and any
\overline{x}\in\mathscr{P}_{x}
and
\overline{y}\in\mathscr{P}_{y}
, the map

(1)

\mathrm{Hom}^{f}_{\mathscr{P}}(\overline{x},\overline{y})\to\lim_{\phi\in\mathscr{O}^{\mathrm{el}}_{y/}}\mathrm{Hom}^{(\phi f)^{\mathrm{act}}}_{\mathscr{P}}((\phi f)^{\mathrm{int}}_{!}\overline{x},\phi_{!}\overline{y})

is an equivalence.

Proof.

We need to verify that for any
x,y
in
\mathscr{O}
, any
\overline{x}\in\mathscr{P}_{x}
and any
\overline{y}\in\mathscr{P}_{y}
, the square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathscr{P}}(\overline{x},\overline{y})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\lim\limits_{(\phi\colon y\rightarrowtail e)\in\mathscr{O}^{\mathrm{el}}_{y/}}\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathscr{P}}(\overline{x},\phi_{!}\overline{y})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathscr{O}}(x,y)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\lim\limits_{(\phi\colon y\rightarrowtail e)\in\mathscr{O}^{\mathrm{el}}_{y/}}\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathscr{O}}(x,e)}}

is cartesian.
By
[
12
, Remark 9.7]
, the map between vertical fibers over an active morphism
f\colon x\rightsquigarrow y
may be identified with the map
Equation

1
.
This shows that the map
Equation

1
is an equivalence if
\mathscr{P}\to\mathscr{O}
is an algebrad.
For the converse, we need to show that the map of fibers

\mathrm{Hom}^{g}_{\mathscr{P}}(\overline{x},\overline{y})\to\lim_{(\phi\colon y\rightarrowtail e)\in\mathscr{O}^{\mathrm{el}}_{y/}}\mathrm{Hom}^{\phi g}_{\mathscr{P}}(\overline{x},\phi_{!}\overline{y})

is an equivalence for any
g\colon x\to y
in
\mathscr{O}
, any
\overline{x}\in\mathscr{P}_{x}
and any
\overline{y}\in\mathscr{P}_{y}
.
By factoring
g=f\circ j
, where
f
is active and
j
inert, and using cocartesian transport along inerts, we may identify this map with

\mathrm{Hom}^{f}_{\mathscr{P}}(j_{!}\overline{x},\overline{y})\to\lim_{\phi\in\mathscr{O}^{\mathrm{el}}_{y/}}\mathrm{Hom}^{\phi f}_{\mathscr{P}}(j_{!}\overline{x},\phi_{!}\overline{y})\simeq\lim_{\phi\in\mathscr{O}^{\mathrm{el}}_{y/}}\mathrm{Hom}^{(\phi f)^{\mathrm{act}}}((\phi fj)^{\mathrm{int}}_{!}\overline{x},\phi_{!}\overline{y}).

Since
f
is active, this map is an equivalence by assumption.
∎

Proposition 2.27
.

Suppose that
\mathscr{O}
is an algebraic pattern. Then the equivalence
(-)^{\mathrm{dbl}}\colon\mathrm{AlgPatt}_{/\mathscr{O}}\to\mathrm{DblPatt}_{/\mathscr{O}^{\mathrm{dbl}}}
restricts to an equivalence

\mathrm{Algad}(\mathscr{O})\to\mathrm{Algad}(\mathscr{O}^{\mathrm{dbl}}).

Proof.

Let
p\colon\mathscr{P}\to\mathscr{O}
in
\mathrm{AlgPatt}_{/\mathscr{O}}
be given. By
[
22
, Lemma 4.2]
, the functor
p^{\mathrm{dbl}}\colon\mathscr{P}^{\mathrm{dbl}}\to\mathscr{O}^{\mathrm{dbl}}
is pointwise a left fibration if and only if
\mathscr{P}\to\mathscr{O}
is a cocartesian fibration over
\mathscr{O}^{\mathrm{int}}
and all morphisms in
\mathscr{P}^{\mathrm{int}}
are cocartesian.
Since
\mathscr{O}^{\mathrm{dbl}}_{0}=\mathscr{O}^{\mathrm{int}}
, we see that the conditions
(2)
of
Section

2.1
and
Section

2.3
are equivalent.

Now let
f\colon x\rightsquigarrow y
be an active morphism in
\mathscr{O}
. Using that
(\mathscr{O}^{\mathrm{dbl}}_{1})^{\mathrm{el}}_{f/}\simeq\mathscr{O}^{\mathrm{el}}_{y/}
by
Section

2.2
, we can rewrite the square of the condition
(3)
of
Section

2.3
as

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(\mathscr{P}^{\mathrm{dbl}}_{1})_{f}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\lim\limits_{(\phi\colon y\rightarrowtail e)\in\mathscr{O}^{\mathrm{el}}_{y/}}(\mathscr{P}^{\mathrm{dbl}}_{1})_{\phi_{*}f}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(\mathscr{P}^{\mathrm{dbl}}_{0})_{x}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\lim\limits_{(y\rightarrowtail e)\in\mathscr{O}^{\mathrm{el}}_{y/}}(\mathscr{P}^{\mathrm{dbl}}_{0})_{(\phi_{*}f)_{0}}}.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d_{1}}

Note that the top horizontal map is fibered via the target projection over

(\mathscr{P}^{\mathrm{dbl}}_{0})_{y}\simeq\lim\limits_{(\phi\colon y\rightarrowtail e)\in\mathscr{O}^{\mathrm{el}}_{y/}}(\mathscr{P}^{\mathrm{dbl}}_{0})_{e}.

Taking fibers of the vertical maps in this square and over
(\mathscr{P}^{\mathrm{dbl}}_{0})_{y}
, it follows that this square is cartesian precisely if the map

\mathrm{Hom}^{f}_{\mathscr{P}}(\overline{y},\overline{x})\to\lim_{\phi\in\mathscr{O}^{\mathrm{el}}_{y/}}\mathrm{Hom}^{(\phi f)^{\mathrm{act}}}_{\mathscr{P}}((\phi f)^{\mathrm{int}}_{!}\bar{y},\phi_{!}\bar{x})

is an equivalence for every
\overline{x}\in\mathscr{P}_{x}
and
\overline{y}\in\mathscr{P}_{y}
.
The result now follows from
Section

2.3
.
∎

Convention 2.28
.

On account of
Section

2.2
, the notions of algebraic patterns and double patterns coincide. Moreover,
Section

2.3
asserts that the notions of algebrads for double patterns and algebraic patterns coincide as well. This justifies referring to double patterns as simply
algebraic patterns
as well. We will do so in the remainder of this article. When it is necessary, we will explicitly state if we are thinking of an algebraic pattern as a factorization system or as a factorization double category.

3.
The forest and tree categories of an algebraic pattern

In this section, we associate a certain category of
(layered)

forests
and
trees
to any algebraic pattern
\mathscr{O}
. We also study the categories of presheaves on these forest and tree categories, and the precise relation between these presheaf categories.
The results we obtain will be used in
Section

4
, where we show that
\mathscr{O}
-algebrads can be modelled as certain
complete Segal
presheaves on these forest and tree categories.

From now on, in light of the equivalence between algebraic patterns and double patterns from
Section

2.2
, we will not distinguish between
\mathscr{O}
when viewed as an algebraic pattern or as a double pattern anymore.
In particular, if
\mathscr{O}
is an algebraic pattern, we will simply write
\mathscr{O}_{n}
for
\mathscr{O}^{\mathrm{dbl}}_{n}
.

Definition 3.1
.

Let
\mathscr{O}
be an algebraic pattern. The
\mathscr{O}
-forest category
is defined as

\textstyle\Phi[\mathscr{O}]\coloneqq(\intop\nolimits_{\Delta^{\mathrm{op}}}\mathscr{O})^{\mathrm{op}},

the Grothendieck construction of
\mathscr{O}
.
If

{t}\coloneqq t_{0}\rightsquigarrow t_{1}\rightsquigarrow\dotsb\rightsquigarrow t_{n}

is an object of
\mathscr{O}_{n}
, then we will write
\langle n;{t}\rangle
for the corresponding object in
\Phi[\mathscr{O}]
.
The objects of
\Phi[\mathscr{O}]
will be called
forests
.

The
\mathscr{O}
-tree category
is the full subcategory

\textstyle\Omega[\mathscr{O}]\subset\Phi[\mathscr{O}]

spanned by objects of the shape
\langle n;t\rangle
such that
t\in\mathscr{O}^{\mathrm{el}}_{n}
, i.e. so that
t_{n}
is an elementary object of
\mathscr{O}
. These are called
trees
. When viewed as elements of the subcategory
\Omega[\mathscr{O}]
, the trees will be denoted by
[n;t]\coloneqq\langle n;t\rangle
.
If
n=0
, then
[0;t]
is called a
root
, and if
n=1
, then
[1;t]
is called a
corolla
.

Remark 3.2
.

It would be better to call the objects of
\Phi[\mathscr{O}]
and
\Omega[\mathscr{O}]

layered
forests and
layered
trees, respectively, as we will see in
Section

3.1
and
Section

3.1
below.
However, to avoid cluttering the text and since we don’t use non-layered trees throughout this paper, we have decided to drop the adjective
layered
everywhere.

Remark 3.3
.

We note that the forest category
\Phi[\mathscr{O}]
only depends on the inert-active factorization system on
\mathscr{O}
, while
\Omega[\mathscr{O}]
also depends on the collection of elementary objects.

3.1.
Examples

We now describe the categories
\Phi[\mathscr{O}]
and
\Omega[\mathscr{O}]
in a few examples, which also motivate the names “(layered) forest category” and “(layered) tree category”.
Observe that since
\Phi[\mathscr{O}]\to\Delta
is a cartesian fibration, a map
\langle n;t\rangle\to\langle m;s\rangle
consists of a map
\phi\colon[n]\to[m]
in
\Delta
together with a map

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{s_{\phi(0)}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{s_{\phi(1)}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{s_{\phi(n-1)}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{s_{\phi(n)}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{t_{0}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{t_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{t_{n-1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{t_{n}}}

in
\mathscr{O}_{n}
(beware of the variance!).
We will write
\underline{n}
for the set
\{1,\cdots,n\}
with
n
elements and
\langle n\rangle
for the pointed set
\{*,1,\ldots,n\}
.

Example 3.4

(
\mathscr{O}=\mathbb{F}_{*}
)
.

Let
\mathbb{F}_{*}
be the category of finite pointed sets, with one of the algebraic pattern structures from
Section

2.1
. The category
\mathbb{F}_{*}^{\mathrm{act}}
is equivalent to the category of finite sets, so we may identify the objects of
\Phi[\mathbb{F}_{*}]
with sequences
X_{0}\to\cdots\to X_{n}
of maps between finite sets. Such a sequence of
n
maps can be visualized as a
forest with
n
layers
. For example, the forest

corresponds to a sequence of maps
\underline{7}\to\underline{5}\to\underline{2}
, where the edges correspond to the elements of the sets.

Note that inert maps
\langle k\rangle\to\langle l\rangle
can be identified with injections
\underline{l}\to\underline{k}
. This allows us to identify the maps of forests
\langle n;t\rangle\to\langle m;s\rangle
over
\phi\colon[m]\to[n]
with diagrams

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{t_{0}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{t_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{t_{n-1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{t_{n}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{s_{\phi(0)}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{s_{\phi(1)}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{s_{\phi(n-1)}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{s_{\phi(n)}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}

of finite sets such that the vertical maps are injective and every square is cartesian.
It follows that
\Phi[\mathbb{F}_{*}]
is precisely the category
\Delta{F}
defined in
[
7
]
, see also
[
11
, Definition 2.1]
.
The category
\Omega[\mathbb{F}_{*}^{\flat}]
is the full subcategory of
\Phi[\mathbb{F}_{*}]
spanned by the trees, which is denoted
\Delta{F}^{1}
in
[
11
]
.
If we take the pattern structure
\mathbb{F}_{*}^{\natural}
where the set
\langle 0\rangle
is also elementary, then we obtain a slightly larger full subcategory
\Omega[\mathbb{F}_{*}^{\natural}]\subset\Phi[\mathbb{F}_{*}]
which also contains the forests of the form
\langle n;\langle 0\rangle=\cdots=\langle 0\rangle\rangle
.

Remark 3.5
.

Let us warn the reader that
\Omega[\mathbb{F}_{*}^{\flat}]=\Delta^{1}{F}
does not agree with the category
\Omega
of trees introduced by Weiss–Moerdijk
[
27
]
, which is used to define dendroidal sets and spaces.
However, it is shown in
[
11
]
that there is a functor
\Omega[\mathbb{F}_{*}^{\flat}]\to\Omega
inducing an equivalence between (complete) Segal presheaves on the categories
\Omega[\mathbb{F}_{*}^{\flat}]
and
\Omega
.

Example 3.6

(
\mathscr{O}=\mathrm{Span}(\mathbb{F})
)
.

By taking
G
to be the trivial group in
Section

2.1
,
\mathrm{Span}(\mathbb{F})
has a pattern structure where the active morphisms are the forward maps and the inerts are the backward maps.
In particular, since
\mathrm{Span}(\mathbb{F})^{\mathrm{act}}=\mathbb{F}=\mathbb{F}^{\mathrm{act}}_{*}
, the objects of
\Phi[\mathrm{Span}(\mathbb{F})]
agree with those of
\Phi[\mathbb{F}_{*}]
.
However, the category
\Phi[\mathrm{Span}(\mathbb{F})]
has more maps:
a map of forests
f\colon\langle n;t\rangle\to\langle m;s\rangle
over
\phi\colon[m]\to[n]
is a diagram

(2)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{t_{0}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{t_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{t_{n-1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{t_{n}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{s_{\phi(0)}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{s_{\phi(1)}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{s_{\phi(n-1)}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{s_{\phi(n)}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}

of finite sets where every square is cartesian. This means that the images of two trees under
f
are allowed to overlap in the forest
\langle m;s\rangle
, which is not allowed in
\Phi[\mathbb{F}_{*}]
.
Observe that if
t_{n}=\underline{1}
or
t_{n}=\underline{0}
, then every vertical map in
Equation

3
is injective.
In particular, the tree categories
\Omega[\mathrm{Span}(\mathbb{F})^{\flat}]
and
\Omega[\mathbb{F}_{*}^{\flat}]
are equivalent, and similarly for
\Omega[\mathrm{Span}(\mathbb{F})^{\natural}]
and
\Omega[\mathbb{F}_{*}^{\natural}]
.

Example 3.7

(
\mathscr{O}=\Delta^{\mathrm{op},\flat}
)
.

In
Section

2.1
, we described a pattern structure on
\Delta^{\mathrm{op}}
where
[1]
is the only elementary object.
The tree category for this pattern can be identified with a certain category of “planar trees with layers”, in analogy with
Section

3.1
.
The objects of the forest category for this algebraic pattern can be viewed as ordered collections of such planar trees.

Example 3.8

(
\mathscr{O}=\Delta^{\mathrm{op},\natural}
)
.

The algebraic pattern
\Delta^{\mathrm{op},\natural}
has the same actives and inerts as
\Delta^{\mathrm{op},\flat}
, but its elementaries are both
[0]
and
[1]
. Algebrads for this pattern are (virtual) double categories, which suggests a different visual representation of the objects of
\Phi[\Delta^{\mathrm{op}}]
, namely as pasting diagrams.
For example, the trees
[1;[2]\leftarrow[1]]
and
[1;[0]\leftarrow[1]]
can be pictured as

\hbox to118.83pt{\vbox to41.97pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 59.4166pt\lower-21.20137pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{}{}{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{}{{

More generally, trees and forests for
\Delta^{\mathrm{op},\natural}
correspond to the pasting diagrams appearing in the classical definition of (ordinary) virtual double categories.

Example 3.9

(
\mathscr{O}=\mathrm{Span}(\mathbb{F}_{G})
)
.

Similarly to
Section

3.1
, the objects of the forest category
\Phi[\mathrm{Span}(\mathbb{F}_{G})]
may be identified with sequences of maps between finite
G
-sets.
A map of forests
f\colon\langle n;t\rangle\to\langle m;s\rangle
over
\phi\colon[m]\to[n]
is then a diagram

(3)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{t_{0}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{t_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{t_{n-1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{t_{n}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{s_{\phi(0)}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{s_{\phi(1)}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{s_{\phi(n-1)}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{s_{\phi(n)}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}

of finite
G
-sets where every square is cartesian.
In fact,
\Phi[\mathrm{Span}(\mathbb{F}_{G})]
is equivalent to the category
\mathrm{Fun}(BG,\Phi[\mathrm{Span}(\mathbb{F})])
of (layered) forests with a
G
-action.
The category
\Omega[\mathrm{Span}(\mathbb{F}_{G})]
is then the full subcategory spanned by those forests
X_{0}\to\cdots\to X_{n}
for which
G
acts transitively on its trees; that is,
G
acts transitively on
X_{n}
.
These categories are layered versions of the categories of
G
-forests and
G
-trees studied by Pereira–Bonventre
[
29
,
9
,
10
]
.

We will now discuss an example of a different flavour:
it turns out that the tree category
\Omega[\mathscr{O}]^{\mathrm{op}}
of an algebraic pattern
\mathscr{O}
admits an algebraic pattern structure which, in a sense, intertwines the algebraic pattern structure of
\Delta^{\mathrm{op}}
and
\mathscr{O}
.
This will allow us to iterate the tree construction.

Definition 3.10
.

Let
f\colon[m;s]\to[n;t]
be a map in
\Omega[\mathscr{O}]
with underlying map
\phi\colon[m]\to[n]
in
\Delta
. Then
f
is called
inert
if
\phi
is inert. We call
f

active
if
\phi
is active and for each
i\leq m
, the inert morphism
s_{\phi(i)}\rightarrowtail t_{i}
is an equivalence, or equivalently, if
s_{m}\rightarrowtail t_{n}
is an equivalence.

Example 3.11

(The iterated tree construction)
.

Let
\mathscr{O}
be an algebraic pattern. We will see in
Section

3.2
below that the inert and active maps form a factorization system on
\Omega[\mathscr{O}]
. We therefore obtain a pattern structure on
\Omega[\mathscr{O}]^{\mathrm{op}}
by taking as elementary objects the roots and corollas (i.e. trees
[n;t]
with
n\leq 1
), which we denote by
\Omega[\mathscr{O}]^{\mathrm{op},\natural}
.

One can then iterate the tree construction. We define
{}^{n}[\mathscr{O}]
inductively by setting
{}^{1}[\mathscr{O}]\coloneqq\Omega[\mathscr{O}]
, and

{}^{n+1}[\mathscr{O}]\coloneqq\Omega\big[{}^{n}[\mathscr{O}]^{\mathrm{op},\natural}\big].

In particular,
{}^{1}[*]^{\mathrm{op},\natural}
is the pattern
\Delta^{\mathrm{op},\natural}
, where
\ast
is the terminal algebraic pattern.
We recently learned that Clémence Chanavat has an (unpublished) proof of the fact that the pattern
{}^{n}[*]^{\mathrm{op},\natural}
can be used to describe the (strict) virtual
n
-uple categories considered by Arkor in
[
2
]
.

3.2.
The slice categories of the forest category

As preparation for what follows, we need a better understanding of the slices
\Phi[\mathscr{O}]_{/[n;t]}
of the forest category
\Phi[\mathscr{O}]
. We introduce the following subcategories:

Definition 3.12
.

Let
0\leq i\leq n
be an integer.
We will write
\Delta^{\circ i}_{/[n]}
for the full subcategory of
\Delta_{/[n]}
spanned by the maps
\phi\colon[m]\to[n]
so that
\phi(m)=i
. If
\langle n;t\rangle
is a forest, then we define the full subcategory
\Phi^{\circ i}[\mathscr{O}]_{/\langle n;t\rangle}
by the pullback

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Phi^{\circ i}[\mathscr{O}]_{/\langle n;t\rangle}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Phi[\mathscr{O}]_{/\langle n;t\rangle}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta^{\circ i}_{/[n]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta_{/[n]}.}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}

Proposition 3.13
.

Let
\mathscr{O}
be an algebraic pattern,
\langle n;t\rangle\in\Phi[\mathscr{O}]
a forest and
0\leq i\leq n
an integer.
Then there exists an equivalence

\Delta_{/[n]}^{\circ i}\times(\mathscr{O}_{t_{i}/}^{\mathrm{int}})^{\mathrm{op}}\simeq\Delta_{/[n]}^{\circ i}\times(\mathscr{O}_{i}^{\mathrm{op}})_{/t_{\leq i}}\xrightarrow{\simeq}\Phi^{\circ i}[\mathscr{O}]_{/\langle n;t\rangle}

that carries a pair
(\phi\colon[k]\to[i],g\colon t_{\leq i}\to s)
to the composite

\langle k;\phi^{*}s\rangle\to\langle i;s\rangle\to\langle i;t_{\leq i}\rangle\to\langle n;t\rangle.

To prove the above, we will make use of the following basic results on cartesian fibrations:

Lemma 3.14
.

Let
p\colon E\to\mathscr{C}
be a cartesian fibration. Then for every
e\in E
, the induced functor
p_{/e}\colon E_{/e}\to\mathscr{C}_{/p(e)}
is a cartesian fibration as well. For any morphism
x\xrightarrow{f}y\xrightarrow{g}p(e)
, the associated structure map
(E_{/e})_{y}\to(E_{/e})_{x}
of
p_{/e}
is given by the functor

(E_{y})_{/g^{*}e}\to(E_{x})_{/f^{*}g^{*}e}
induced by
f^{*}\colon E_{y}\to E_{x}
.

Proof.

This follows from
[
24
, Proposition 2.4.3.1]
and the explicit description of the cartesian morphisms given there.
∎

Lemma 3.15
.

Let
\mathscr{C}
be a category with a final object
\ast
. If
p\colon E\to\mathscr{C}
is a cartesian fibration so that the transport maps
E_{\ast}\to E_{c}
are equivalences for every
x\in\mathscr{C}
, then there exists an equivalence
E\simeq\mathscr{C}\times E_{\ast}
over
\mathscr{C}
.

Proof.

Let
F\colon\mathscr{C}^{\mathrm{op}}\to\mathrm{Cat}
be the functor classifying
p\colon E\to\mathscr{C}
. The conditions on
p
imply that
F
is left Kan extended from its restriction to
*
, hence that it is the constant functor with value
F(*)=E_{*}
.
∎

Proof of
Section

3.2
.

On account of
Section

3.2
, the projection
\Phi[\mathscr{O}]_{/\langle n;t\rangle}\to\Delta_{/[n]}
is again a cartesian fibration. Let
\phi\colon[m]\to[n]
be a map so that
\phi(m)=i
. Then
\phi
factors uniquely through the inclusion
\{0\leq\dotsb\leq i\}\colon[i]\hookrightarrow[n]
via a map
\psi\colon[m]\to[i]
. Thus
\Delta^{\circ i}_{/[n]}
has a final object given by the canonical inclusion
\{0\leq\dotsc\leq i\}\colon[i]\hookrightarrow[n]
. The transport functor along
\psi
of the cartesian fibration
\Phi[\mathscr{O}]_{/\langle n;t\rangle}\to\Delta_{/[n]}
fits in a commutative triangle

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\mathscr{O}_{i})_{t_{\leq i}/}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\mathscr{O}_{m})_{\psi^{*}t_{\leq i}/}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\mathscr{O}_{0})_{t_{i}/}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi^{*}}

after applying
(-)^{\mathrm{op}}
.
The slanted arrows are equivalences by
Section

2.2
since
\mathscr{O}
is a factorization double category, so the result follows from
Section

3.2
.
∎

Corollary 3.16
.

Let
0\leq i\leq n
be an integer.
There is a natural pullback square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\mathscr{O}^{\mathrm{op}}_{0})_{/t_{i}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Phi[\mathscr{O}]_{/\langle n;t\rangle}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\{\phi\}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta_{/[n]}}

for
\phi\in\Delta^{\circ i}_{/[n]}
. ∎

Remark 3.17
.

The equivalence of above can be described on points as follows. For a map
f\colon t_{i}\to x
, the corresponding morphism
\langle m;s\rangle\to\langle n;t\rangle
corresponds to
\phi^{*}t\to s
in
\mathscr{O}_{m}
obtained by completing the diagram below with the dashed factorizations:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces t_{\phi(0)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces t_{\phi(1)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dotsb}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces t_{\phi(m-1)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces t_{\phi(m)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dotsb}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s_{m-1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s_{m}.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

Remark 3.18
.

One can also describe the mapping spaces in
\Phi[\mathscr{O}]
over a given map
\phi\colon[m]\to[n]
in
\Delta
. Since
\Phi[\mathscr{O}]\to\Delta
is cartesian, the space
\mathrm{Hom}^{\phi}_{\Phi[\mathscr{O}]}(\langle m;s\rangle,\langle n;t\rangle)
of maps
\langle m;s\rangle\to\langle n;t\rangle
over
\phi
agrees with the space
\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathscr{O}_{m}}(\phi^{*}t,s)
.
When
m=1
, this mapping space is given by

\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathscr{O}_{1}}(\phi^{*}t,s)\simeq\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathscr{O}_{0}}(t_{\phi(1)},s_{1})\times_{(\mathscr{O}^{\mathrm{act}}_{/s_{1}})^{\simeq}}\{s_{0}\rightsquigarrow s_{1}\}.

There is a similar formula for general
m
, which can either be described iteratively (using the Segal condition of
\mathscr{O}
) or by replacing
\mathscr{O}^{\mathrm{act}}_{/s_{1}}
with the category of strings of
m
active morphisms that end with
s_{m}
.

Finally, we are now ready to prove that the inert and active maps from
Section

3.1
form the factorization system on
\Omega[\mathscr{O}]
used in
Section

3.1
.

Lemma 3.19
.

The active and inert morphisms of
\Omega[\mathscr{O}]
form a factorization system.

Proof.

We have to show that the composition functor

\mathrm{Ar}^{\mathrm{act}}(\Omega[\mathscr{O}])\times_{\Omega[\mathscr{O}]}\mathrm{Ar}^{\mathrm{int}}(\Omega[\mathscr{O}])\to\mathrm{Ar}(\Omega[\mathscr{O}])

has contractible fibers on account of
[
6
, Proposition 3]
. If
f:[m;s]\to[n;t]
is a map between trees, then one readily verifies that the fiber above
f
is given by the full subcategory

\mathrm{Fact}(f)\subset(\Omega[\mathscr{O}]_{/[n;t]})_{f/}

that corresponds to the triangles

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[m;s]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[k;v]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[n;t]}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{\prime\prime}}

so that
f^{\prime}
is active and
f^{\prime\prime}
is inert. Let
\phi
be the underlying map of
f
. Then
f
is contained in
\Omega[\mathscr{O}]^{\circ i}_{/[n;t]}
with
i:=\phi(m)
, and this automatically implies that
f^{\prime\prime}\in\Omega[\mathscr{O}]^{\circ i}_{/[n;t]}
as well since
f^{\prime}
is active. On account of
Section

3.2
, we may identify
\mathrm{Fact}(f)
with the full subcategory of
(\Delta_{/[n]})_{\phi/}\times((\mathscr{O}^{\mathrm{el},\mathrm{op}})_{/t_{i}})_{\psi/}
spanned by the pairs of triangles

\left(\hbox to107.58pt{\vbox to42.14pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 53.79057pt\lower-19.56944pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{}{}{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{{}}{{}}{{}}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{}{{

\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\right)

so that
\phi^{\prime}
is active and
\phi^{\prime\prime}
is inert.
Here
\psi
is induced by
f
. Thus
\mathrm{Fact}(f)
is a contractible category.
∎

3.3.
Comparing presheaves on the forest and tree categories

Let
i\colon\Omega[\mathscr{O}]\to\Phi[\mathscr{O}]
be the inclusion.
Since
i
is fully faithful, right Kan extension along
i
gives an adjunction

i^{*}:\mathrm{PSh}(\Phi[\mathscr{O}])\rightleftarrows\mathrm{PSh}(\Omega[\mathscr{O}]):i_{*},

such that
i_{*}
is fully faithful.
The goal of this section is to identify the image of
i_{*}
.

Proposition 3.20
.

Let
\mathscr{O}
be an algebraic pattern and
X
a presheaf on
\Phi[\mathscr{O}]
. Then
X
lies in the image of
i_{*}\colon\mathrm{PSh}(\Omega[\mathscr{O}])\to\mathrm{PSh}(\Phi[\mathscr{O}])
if and only

•

for every object
x
in
\mathscr{O}
, the map

X(\langle 0;x\rangle)\to\lim_{e\in\mathscr{O}^{\mathrm{el}}_{x/}}X(\langle 0;e\rangle)

is an equivalence, and

•

for every forest
\langle n;t\rangle
with
n\geq 1
, the square

(4)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{X(\langle n;t\rangle)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\lim\limits_{(t\rightarrowtail s)\in(\mathscr{O}_{n})^{\mathrm{el}}_{t/}}X(\langle n;s\rangle)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{X(\langle n-1;t_{\leq n-1}\rangle)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\lim\limits_{(t\rightarrowtail s)\in(\mathscr{O}_{n})^{\mathrm{el}}_{t/}}X(\langle n-1;s_{\leq n-1}\rangle)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d_{n}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d_{n}}

is cartesian.

We will use the following lemma.

Lemma 3.21
.

Let
\mathscr{C}
be a category and let
p\colon\mathscr{C}\to[1]
be a functor with fibers
\mathscr{C}_{0}
and
\mathscr{C}_{1}
over
0
and
1
, respectively. Then for any complete category
\mathscr{E}
and any diagram
F\colon\mathscr{C}\to\mathscr{E}
, the square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\lim_{c\in\mathscr{C}}F(c)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\lim_{d\in\mathscr{C}_{1}}F(d)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\lim_{c\in\mathscr{C}_{0}}F(c)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\lim_{c\in\mathscr{C}_{0}}\lim_{d\in(\mathscr{C}_{1})_{c/}}F(d)}}

is cartesian.

Proof.

Let us write
\Gamma(\mathscr{C})=\mathrm{Fun}_{/[1]}([1],\mathscr{C})
for the category of sections of
p
.
By
[
4
, Lemma 5.1.1]
, we obtain an equivalence

\mathscr{C}_{0}\cup_{\Gamma(\mathscr{C})}\Gamma(\mathscr{C})\times[1]\cup_{\Gamma(\mathscr{C})}\mathscr{C}_{1}\simeq\mathscr{C}.

In particular, the limit of
F\colon\mathscr{C}\to\mathscr{E}
decomposes as an iterated pullback

\lim_{c\in\mathscr{C}}F(c)\simeq\lim_{c\in\mathscr{C}_{0}}F(c)\times_{\lim_{\gamma\in\Gamma(\mathscr{C})}F(\gamma(0))}\lim_{(\gamma,i)\in\Gamma(\mathscr{C})\times[1]}F(\gamma(i))\times_{\lim_{\gamma\in\Gamma(\mathscr{C})}F(\gamma(1))}\lim_{d\in\mathscr{C}_{1}}F(d).

Since
\Gamma(\mathscr{C})\times\{0\}\hookrightarrow\Gamma(\mathscr{C})\times[1]
is initial, we can write this as a single pullback

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\lim_{c\in\mathscr{C}}F(c)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\lim_{d\in\mathscr{C}_{1}}F(d)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\lim_{c\in\mathscr{C}_{0}}F(c)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\lim_{\gamma\in\Gamma(\mathscr{C})}F(\gamma(1))}.}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}

Finally, to see that the bottom right limit agrees with
\lim_{c\in\mathscr{C}_{0}}\lim_{d\in(\mathscr{C}_{1})_{c/}}F(d)
, note that limits over
\Gamma(\mathscr{C})
can be computed by first right Kan extending along
\Gamma(\mathscr{C})\to\mathscr{C}_{0}
and then taking the limit over
\mathscr{C}_{0}
.
Since
\Gamma(\mathscr{C})\to\mathscr{C}_{0}
is a cartesian fibration whose unstraightening is the functor
c\mapsto(\mathscr{C}_{1})_{c/}
, it follows from the dual of
[
24
, Proposition 4.3.3.10]
that the right Kan extension of
\Gamma(\mathscr{C})\to\mathscr{S};\gamma\mapsto F(\gamma(1))
along
\Gamma(\mathscr{C})\to\mathscr{C}_{0}
is given by
c\mapsto\lim_{\gamma\in(\mathscr{C}_{1})_{c/}}F(\gamma(1))
.
This concludes the proof.
∎

Let
f\colon\langle m;s\rangle\to\langle n;t\rangle
be a map in
\Phi[\mathscr{O}]
and
\phi\colon[m]\to[n]
the underlying map in
\Delta
.
We say that
f

reaches
n
if
\phi(m)=n
. Note that for a triangle

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\langle m;s\rangle}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\langle l;u\rangle}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\langle n;t\rangle,}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}

if
f
reaches
n
, then so does
g
.
In particular, we obtain a functor

\Omega[\mathscr{O}]_{/\langle n;t\rangle}\to[1];\quad(f\colon\langle m;s\rangle\to\langle n;t\rangle)\mapsto\begin{cases}1&\text{if }f\text{ reaches n,}\\
0&\text{otherwise.}\end{cases}

Let us write
(\Omega[\mathscr{O}]_{/\langle n;t\rangle})_{0}
and
(\Omega[\mathscr{O}]_{/\langle n;t\rangle})_{1}
for the fibers of this functor over
0
and
1
, respectively.
We will prove
Section

3.3
by applying
Section

3.3
to this functor.
This requires the following two lemmas.

Lemma 3.22
.

Let
\mathscr{O}
be an algebraic pattern and
\langle n;t\rangle\in\Phi[\mathscr{O}]
a forest. Then the inclusion

\mathscr{O}_{t_{n}/}^{\mathrm{el}}\simeq(\mathscr{O}_{n}^{\mathrm{el}})_{t/}\to(\Omega[\mathscr{O}]_{/\langle n;t\rangle})_{1}^{\mathrm{op}}

is initial.

Proof.

By
Section

3.2
, this inclusion is equivalent to the inclusion

\mathscr{O}_{t_{n}/}^{\mathrm{el}}\hookrightarrow(\Delta^{\circ n}_{/[n]})^{\mathrm{op}}\times\mathscr{O}_{t_{n}/}^{\mathrm{el}}\simeq(\Omega[\mathscr{O}]_{/\langle n;t\rangle})_{1}^{\mathrm{op}}

that takes
f\in\mathscr{O}_{t_{n}/}^{\mathrm{el}}
to
(\mathrm{id}_{[n]},f)
.
Since
\mathrm{id}_{[n]}
is terminal in
\Delta^{\circ n}_{/[n]}
, this functor is a left adjoint and hence initial.
∎

Lemma 3.23
.

Let
\mathscr{O}
be an algebraic pattern,
\langle n;t\rangle\in\Phi[\mathscr{O}]
a forest and
\langle n;s\rangle\to\langle n;t\rangle
a map of forests whose underlying map in
\Delta
is
\mathrm{id}_{[n]}
.
Then the canonical map

\Omega[\mathscr{O}]_{/\langle n-1;s_{\leq n-1}\rangle}\to((\Omega[\mathscr{O}]_{/\langle n;t\rangle})_{0})_{/\langle n;s\rangle}

is an equivalence.

Proof.

Note that
((\Omega[\mathscr{O}]_{/\langle n;t\rangle})_{0})_{/\langle n;s\rangle}
is the full subcategory of
(\Omega[\mathscr{O}]_{/\langle n;t\rangle})_{/\langle n;s\rangle}\simeq\Omega[\mathscr{O}]_{/\langle n;s\rangle}
spanned by those maps
\langle m;u\rangle\to\langle n;s\rangle
that don’t reach
n
.
This is canonically equivalent to
\Omega[\mathscr{O}]_{/\langle n-1;s_{\leq n-1}\rangle}
.
∎

Proof of
Section

3.3
.

If
n=0
, then
\mathscr{O}_{x/}^{\mathrm{el}}\to(\Omega[\mathscr{O}]_{/\langle 0;x\rangle})^{\mathrm{op}}
is initial by
Section

3.3
.
The result therefore follows if we can show that the square
Equation

4
is cartesian for every forest
\langle n;t\rangle
with
n\geq 1
if and only if the map

X(\langle n;t\rangle)\to\lim_{[m;s]\in(\Omega[\mathscr{O}]_{/\langle n;t\rangle})^{\mathrm{op}}}X(\langle m;s\rangle)

is an equivalence for every forest
\langle n;t\rangle
with
n\geq 1
.
We will show this by induction on
n
.
Combining
Section

3.3
,
Section

3.3
and
Section

3.3
, it follows that this map is an equivalence precisely if the square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{X(\langle n;t\rangle)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\lim\limits_{(t\rightarrowtail s)\in(\mathscr{O}_{n})^{\mathrm{el}}_{t/}}X(\langle n;s\rangle)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\lim\limits_{[m;u]\in(\Omega[\mathscr{O}]_{/\langle n-1;t_{\leq n-1}\rangle})^{\mathrm{op}}}X(\langle m;u\rangle)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\lim\limits_{(t\rightarrowtail s)\in(\mathscr{O}_{n})^{\mathrm{el}}_{t/}}\left(\lim\limits_{[m;u]\in(\Omega[\mathscr{O}]_{/\langle n-1;s_{\leq n-1}\rangle})^{\mathrm{op}}}X(\langle m;u\rangle)\right)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d_{n}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d_{n}}

is cartesian.
By the induction hypothesis, the bottom map is equivalent to
X(\langle n-1;t_{\leq n-1}\rangle)\to\lim_{t\rightarrowtail s\in(\mathscr{O}_{n})^{\mathrm{el}}_{t/}}X(\langle n-1;s_{n-1}\rangle)
and hence this square is equivalent to the square
Equation

4
, concluding the proof.
∎

The conditions of
Section

3.3
are reminiscent of the definition of an algebrad given in
Section

2.3
.
However, in the definition of an algebrad one only needs to consider cartesian squares for
n=1
.
We will now show that, under a mild condition on the presheaf
X
, one only needs to consider the squares in
Section

3.3
where
n=1
.

Proposition 3.24
.

Let
\mathscr{O}
be an algebraic pattern and
X
a presheaf on
\Phi[\mathscr{O}]
such that for any forest
\langle n;t\rangle
, the canonical map

X(\langle n;t\rangle)\to X(\langle 1;t_{0,1}\rangle)\times_{X(\langle 0;t_{1}\rangle)}\cdots\times_{X(\langle 0;t_{n-1}\rangle}X(\langle 1;t_{n-1,n}\rangle)

is an equivalence.
Then
X
lies in the image of
i_{*}
if and only if for every object
x
in
\mathscr{O}
, the map
X(\langle 0;x\rangle)\to\lim_{e\in\mathscr{O}^{\mathrm{el}}_{x/}}X(\langle 0;e\rangle)
is an equivalence and for any forest
\langle 1;t\rangle
of length 1, the square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{X(\langle 1;t\rangle)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\lim\limits_{(t\rightarrowtail s)\in(\mathscr{O}_{1})^{\mathrm{el}}_{t/}}}X(\langle 1;s\rangle)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{X(\langle 0;t_{0}\rangle)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\lim\limits_{(t\rightarrowtail s)\in(\mathscr{O}_{1})^{\mathrm{el}}_{t/}}}X(\langle 0;s_{0}\rangle)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d_{1}}

is cartesian.

Proof.

A proof similar to
Section

2.3
shows that this implies that for any forest
\langle n;t\rangle
with
n\geq 1
, the square
Equation

4
from
Section

3.3
is cartesian.
∎

In case
\mathscr{O}
is sound in the sense of
[
5
, Definition 3.3.4]
, one can simplify the conditions further.

Proposition 3.25
.

Suppose
\mathscr{O}
is sound and let
X
be a presheaf on
\Phi[\mathscr{O}]
. Then
X
lies in the image of
i_{*}
if and only if for every forest
\langle n;t\rangle
, the map

X(\langle n;t\rangle)\to\lim\limits_{(t\rightarrowtail s)\in(\mathscr{O}_{n})^{\mathrm{el}}_{t/}}X(\langle n;s\rangle)

is an equivalence.
If
X
satisfies the condition from
Section

3.3
, then this only needs to hold for forests of length
\leq 1
.

Proof.

We will show that these conditions are equivalent to those of
Section

3.3
.
The case
n=0
gives the first condition of
Section

3.3
.
We now show by induction on
k\geq 1
that

X(\langle n;t\rangle)\to\lim\limits_{(t\rightarrowtail s)\in(\mathscr{O}_{n})^{\mathrm{el}}_{t/}}X(\langle n;s\rangle)

is an equivalence for forests of length
n\leq k
precisely if the square
Equation

4
from
Section

3.3
is cartesian for all forests of length
n\leq k
.
Observe that by the induction hypothesis, the bottom map of the square
Equation

4
can be rewritten as

X(\langle n-1;t_{\leq n-1}\rangle)\to\operatorname{lim}_{s\in(\mathscr{O}_{n})^{\mathrm{el}}_{t/}}\operatorname{lim}_{u\in(\mathscr{O}_{n-1})^{\mathrm{el}}_{s_{\leq n-1}/}}X(\langle n-1;u\rangle).

By
[
5
, Observation 3.3.6]
and the equivalences
(\mathscr{O}_{n})^{\mathrm{el}}_{t/}\simeq\mathscr{O}^{\mathrm{el}}_{t_{n}/}
and
(\mathscr{O}_{n-1})^{\mathrm{el}}_{s_{\leq n-1}/}\simeq\mathscr{O}^{\mathrm{el}}_{s_{n-1}/}
, soundness of
\mathscr{O}
implies that this map agrees with

X(\langle n-1;t_{\leq n-1}\rangle)\to\lim_{s\in(\mathscr{O}_{n-1})^{\mathrm{el}}_{t_{\leq n-1}/}}X(\langle n-1;s\rangle),

which is an equivalence by the induction hypothesis.
In particular, the square
Equation

4
from
Section

3.3
is cartesian precisely if the top map is an equivalence, which finishes the induction.
It is clear that if
X
satisfies the conditions from
Section

3.3
, then it suffices to consider forests of length
\leq 1
.
∎

4.
Algebrads as Segal presheaves on the tree category

In this section, we will present yet another perspective on algebrads, namely as certain “complete Segal” presheaves on the tree category
\Omega[\mathscr{O}]
.
In the context of
operator categories
, a similar equivalence was exhibited by Barwick
[
7
]
.
For algebraic patterns, a similar equivalence was studied by Kern
[
23
]
as well.
We fix the following terminology:

Definition 4.1
.

Presheaves on
\Omega[\mathscr{O}]
are called
\Omega[\mathscr{O}]
-spaces
.

4.1.
Segal presheaves

We introduce Segal conditions for
\Omega[\mathscr{O}]
-spaces by making use of the simplicial nature of
\Omega[\mathscr{O}]
. We will see at the end of this section that this terminology matches up with the previous terminology of Segal objects (
Section

2.1
) applied to the pattern
\Omega[\mathscr{O}]^{\mathrm{op},\natural}
of
Section

3.1
.

Notation 4.2
.

The projection
\Omega[\mathscr{O}]\to\Delta
will be denoted by
p
, and the presheaf on
\Omega[\mathscr{O}]
represented by a tree
[n;t]
will be denoted by
[n;t]
.
More generally, let
\langle n;t\rangle
be a forest. Then we will consider the
