\Omega[\mathscr{O}]
-space

[n;t]\coloneqq i^{*}\langle n;t\rangle

that is obtained by restricting the presheaf represented by
\langle n;t\rangle
along the inclusion
i\colon\Omega[\mathscr{O}]\to\Phi[\mathscr{O}]
. Note that this leads to no ambiguity in notation when
t_{n}\in\mathscr{O}^{\mathrm{el}}
. We note that there is a natural projection
[n;t]\to p^{*}[n]
obtained by applying
i^{*}
to
\langle n;t\rangle\to q^{*}[n]
, where
q
denotes the projection
\Phi[\mathscr{O}]\to\Delta
.

Construction 4.3
.

Let
Y
be a simplicial space. Then we consider the functor

(-)\boxtimes_{Y}(-)\colon\mathrm{PSh}(\Delta)_{/Y}\times\mathrm{PSh}(\Omega[\mathscr{O}])_{/p^{*}Y}\to\mathrm{PSh}(\Omega[\mathscr{O}])_{/p^{*}Y}^{\times 2}\xrightarrow{\times_{p^{*}Y}}\mathrm{PSh}(\Omega[\mathscr{O}]).

Since
\mathrm{PSh}(\Omega[\mathscr{O}])
is locally cartesian closed, this preserves colimits in both variables.
In practice, we will use this construction when
Y=[n]
, so that we obtain a colimit preserving functor

(-)\boxtimes_{[n]}[n;t]\colon\mathrm{PSh}(\Delta)_{/[n]}\to\mathrm{PSh}(\Omega[\mathscr{O}])

for every forest
\langle n;t\rangle
.

We will make extensive use of the following
forest decomposition formulas
:

Lemma 4.4
.

Let
\langle n;t\rangle
be a forest, and suppose that
X
is a simplicial space over
[n]
. Then we have the following formulas:

•

if
n=0
, then the canonical map

\mathop{colim}\displaylimits_{e\in(\mathscr{O}^{\mathrm{el},\mathrm{op}})_{/t}}X\boxtimes_{[0]}[0;e]\to X\boxtimes_{[0]}[0;t]

is an equivalence, and

•

in the case that
n\geq 1
, the commutative square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathop{colim}\displaylimits_{s\in(\mathscr{O}^{\mathrm{el},\mathrm{op}}_{n})_{/t}}X_{\leq n-1}\boxtimes_{[n-1]}[n-1;s_{\leq n-1}]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathop{colim}\displaylimits_{s\in(\mathscr{O}^{\mathrm{el},\mathrm{op}}_{n})_{/t}}X\boxtimes_{[n]}[n;s]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{\leq n-1}\boxtimes_{[n-1]}[n-1;t_{\leq n-1}]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X\boxtimes_{[n]}[n;t]}

is a pushout square, where
X_{\leq n-1}\coloneqq d_{n}^{*}X
.

Proof.

Recall that the functor
X\boxtimes_{[n]}(-)
preserves colimits and that
X_{\leq n-1}\boxtimes_{[n-1]}[n-1;-]=X\boxtimes_{[n]}[n-1;-]
by pullback pasting. Consequently, if the formulas hold for the absolute case that
X=[n]
when there are no box-products, then they will hold for general
X
. To check this absolute case, we can apply
\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{PSh}(\Omega[\mathscr{O}])}(-,Y)
, where
Y
is an arbitrary
\Omega[\mathscr{O}]
-space, to the diagrams in both bullet points. In this case, we recover the formulas for
i_{*}Y
of
Section

3.3
.
∎

Remark 4.5
.

If
\mathscr{O}
is sound, then it follows from
Section

3.3
that the canonical map

\mathop{colim}\displaylimits_{s\in(\mathscr{O}^{\mathrm{el},\mathrm{op}}_{n})_{/t}}X\boxtimes_{[n]}[n;s]\to X\boxtimes_{[n]}[n;t]

is an equivalence for every forest
\left<n;t\right>
and simplicial space
X
over
[n]
.

Lemma 4.6
.

Let
\langle n;t\rangle
be a forest. For every map
\phi\colon[m]\to[n]
, there is a natural equivalence

[m]\boxtimes_{[n]}[n;t]\simeq[m;\phi^{*}t].

If
X\to[n]
is a map between simplicial spaces, then we have a colimit expression

\mathop{colim}\displaylimits_{(\phi\colon[k]\to[n])\in(\Delta_{/[n]})_{/X}}[k;\phi^{*}{t}]\xrightarrow{\simeq}X\boxtimes_{[n]}[n;t].

Proof.

Let
[k;s]
be a tree. Then there is a natural pullback square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{PSh}(\Omega[\mathscr{O}])}([k;s],[m]\boxtimes_{[n]}[n;t])}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Hom}_{\Phi[\mathscr{O}]}(\left<k;s\right>,\langle n;t\rangle)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Hom}_{\Delta}([k],[m])}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Hom}_{\Delta}([k],[n]),}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi\circ(-)}

thus
[m]\boxtimes_{[n]}[n;t]\to[n;t]
must be represented by the cartesian transport of
\langle n;t\rangle
along
\phi
with respect to the projection
\Phi[\mathscr{O}]\to\Delta
. The second assertion now follows immediately.
∎

Definition 4.7
.

We recall that we have the following special morphisms between simplicial spaces:

(i)

for
n\geq 0
, a
spine inclusion

\mathrm{Sp}[n]\coloneqq[1]\cup_{[0]}\dotsb\cup_{[0]}[1]\to[n]
,

(ii)

the
generating completeness extension

J\to[0]
, where
J
is the simplicial set defined by the pushout square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[1]}\sqcup[1]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[3]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[0]}\sqcup[0]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces J.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\{0\leq 2\}\sqcup\{1\leq 3\}}

Thanks to
Section

4.1
, these induce the following classes of maps between
\Omega[\mathscr{O}]
-spaces:

(1)

for every tree
[n;t]
, a
spine inclusion

\mathrm{Sp}[n;t]\coloneqq\mathrm{Sp}[n]\boxtimes_{[n]}[n;t]\to[n;t],

(2)

for every root
[0;e]
, a
generating completeness extension

[J;e]\coloneqq J\boxtimes_{[0]}[0;e]\to[0;e].

Definition 4.8
.

A map
f\colon X\to Y
between
\Omega[\mathscr{O}]
-spaces is called a
Segal fibration
if it has the unique right lifting property with respect to the spine inclusions. Additionally,
f
is called a
complete Segal fibration
if it is a Segal fibration and it has the unique right lifting property with respect to the generating completeness extensions.

An
\Omega[\mathscr{O}]
-space
X
is called a
(complete) Segal
\Omega[\mathscr{O}]
-space
if the unique map
X\to\ast
is a (complete) Segal fibration. We will write

\mathrm{CSeg}(\Omega[\mathscr{O}])\subset\mathrm{Seg}(\Omega[\mathscr{O}])\subset\mathrm{PSh}(\Omega[\mathscr{O}])

for the full subcategories spanned by the complete Segal
\Omega[\mathscr{O}]
-spaces and Segal
\Omega[\mathscr{O}]
-spaces, respectively.

A map
A\to B
between
\Omega[\mathscr{O}]
-spaces is called a
(complete) Segal extension
if it has the unique left lifting property with respect to the (complete) Segal fibrations.

On account of
[
24
, Example 5.5.5.6 & Proposition 5.5.5.7]
, the Segal extensions form the left part of a factorization system on
\mathrm{PSh}(\Omega[\mathscr{O}])
, whose right part consists of the Segal fibrations. Moreover, the Segal extensions are the smallest
saturated
class containing the spine inclusions. Analogous statements hold for complete Segal extensions and complete Segal fibrations.

Remark 4.9
.

By
[
24
, Proposition 5.5.4.15]
, the category
\mathrm{CSeg}(\Omega[\mathscr{O}])
is a reflective subcategory of
\mathrm{PSh}(\Omega[\mathscr{O}])
and thus admits a reflection functor
L\colon\mathrm{PSh}(\Omega[\mathscr{O}])\to\mathrm{CSeg}(\Omega[\mathscr{O}])
.
The functor
L
carries all complete Segal extensions to equivalences.
On the other hand, if
i\colon A\to B
is a map of
\Omega[\mathscr{O}]
-spaces such that
Li
is an equivalence, then it is not necessarily true that
i
is a complete Segal extension.
However, if
B
is a complete Segal object, then this
does
hold by the following standard argument:
First, factor
A\to B
as a complete Segal extension
A\to B^{\prime}
followed by a complete Segal fibration
B^{\prime}\to B
. By 2-out-of-3, the map
LB^{\prime}\to LB
is an equivalence. Since
B^{\prime}
is a complete Segal object, it follows that
B^{\prime}\to B
is an equivalence and hence that
A\to B
is a complete Segal extension.

Recall that for a sequence of active morphisms
t=(t_{0}\rightsquigarrow\cdots\rightsquigarrow t_{n})
and
0\leq i<j\leq n
, we write
t_{i,j}
for the subsequence
(t_{i}\rightsquigarrow\cdots\rightsquigarrow t_{j})
.

Remark 4.10
.

By
Section

4.1
, we have an equivalence

\mathrm{Sp}[n;t]\simeq[1;t_{0,1}]\cup_{[0;t_{1}]}\cdots\cup_{[0;t_{n-1}]}[1;t_{n-1,n}].

It therefore follows that a
\Omega[\mathscr{O}]
-space
X
is Segal if and only if its right Kan extension
i_{*}X
has the property that for any tree
[n;t]
, the canonical map

i_{*}X(\langle n;t\rangle)\to i_{*}X(\langle 1;t_{0,1}\rangle)\times_{i_{*}X(\langle 0;t_{1}\rangle)}\cdots\times_{i_{*}X(\langle 0;t_{n-1}\rangle)}i_{*}X(\langle 1;t_{n-1,n}\rangle)

is an equivalence.
It similarly follows that
X
is complete if and only if

X([0;e])\to X([3,e=\cdots=e])\times_{X([1;e=e])\times X([1;e=e])}(X([0;e])\times X([0;e]))

is an equivalence for every
e
in
\mathscr{O}^{\mathrm{el}}
.

Remark 4.11
.

Suppose that
p\colon X\to Y
is a (complete) Segal fibration. If
Y
is a (complete) Segal
\Omega[\mathscr{O}]
-space, then so is
X
.

Proposition 4.12
.

Let
\left<n;t\right>
be a forest. If
X\to Y
is a (complete) Segal extension between simplicial spaces over
[n]
, then
the induced map

X\boxtimes_{[n]}[n;t]\to Y\boxtimes_{[n]}[n;t]

between
\Omega[\mathscr{O}]
-spaces is a (complete) Segal extension as well.

Proof.

First suppose
X\to Y
is a Segal extension.
As
(-)\boxtimes_{[n]}[n;t]
preserves colimits, it suffices to handle the case that
X\to Y
is given by
\mathrm{Sp}[m]\to[m]
. If
\phi\colon[m]\to[n]
denotes the underlying map, then the induced map in question is identified with the canonical map

\mathrm{Sp}[m]\boxtimes_{[m]}[m;\phi^{*}t]\to[m;\phi^{*}t]

on account of
Section

4.1
.
All in all, we may thus reduce to the case that
X\to Y
is given by
\mathrm{Sp}[n]\to[n]
, so that we have to show that the map

\mathrm{Sp}[n]\boxtimes_{[n]}[n;t]\to[n;t]

is a Segal extension for every forest
\left<n;t\right>
.
In light of
Section

4.1
, we see that an induction argument on
n
allows us to further reduce to the case that
[n;t]
is a tree. Then the map is a Segal extension by definition.
For the claim about complete Segal extensions, it suffices to consider the case that
X\to Y
is of the form
J\to[0]
.
This follows by an analogous argument.
∎

Proposition 4.13
.

Let
X\to Y
be an exponentiable map between Segal spaces. If
A\to B
is a Segal extension between
\Omega[\mathscr{O}]
-spaces over
Y
, then the induced map

X\boxtimes_{Y}A\to X\boxtimes_{Y}B

between
\Omega[\mathscr{O}]
-spaces is a Segal extension as well.

Proof.

We may reduce to the case that
A\to B
is given by the spine inclusion
\mathrm{Sp}[n;t]\to[n;t]
. The map of the proposition can be rewritten as the map obtained by applying
(-)\boxtimes_{[n]}[n;t]
to the map
i:X\times_{Y}\mathrm{Sp}[n]\to X\times_{Y}[n]
induced by the underlying map
[n]\to Y
. The assumption implies that
i
is a Segal extension between simplicial spaces, so the desired conclusion follows from
Section

4.1
.
∎

As promised at the beginning of this subsection, we include the following observation for thoroughness:

Proposition 4.14
.

Let
X
be a
\Omega[\mathscr{O}]
-space. Then
X
is a Segal
\Omega[\mathscr{O}]
-space in the sense of
Section

4.1
if and only if it is a Segal space for the algebraic pattern
\Omega[\mathscr{O}]^{\mathrm{op},\natural}
in the sense of
Section

2.1
.

Proof.

Let
[n;t]
be a tree.
Then one may consider the canonical span
of functors

\Omega[\mathscr{O}]^{\mathrm{el}}_{/[n;t]}\xleftarrow{\simeq}\Omega[\mathscr{O}]^{\mathrm{el}}_{/\mathrm{Sp}[n;t]}\xrightarrow{i}\Omega[\mathscr{O}]_{/\mathrm{Sp}[n;t]}.

This induces a natural comparison map

\mathop{colim}\displaylimits_{[m;s]\in\Omega[\mathscr{O}]^{\mathrm{el}}_{/[n;t]}}[m;s]\to\mathrm{Sp}[n;t]

in
\mathrm{PSh}(\Omega[\mathscr{O}])
.
The proposition follows if we show that this map is an equivalence, so we will show that
i
is final.

If
n\geq 2
, then we may write
i
as an iterated pushout

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Omega[\mathscr{O}]^{\mathrm{el}}_{/[1;t_{01}]}\cup_{\Omega[\mathscr{O}]^{\mathrm{el}}_{/[0;t_{1}]}}\dotsb\cup_{\Omega[\mathscr{O}]^{\mathrm{el}}_{/[0;t_{n-1}]}}\Omega[\mathscr{O}]^{\mathrm{el}}_{/[1;t_{n-1,n}]}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Omega[\mathscr{O}]_{/[1;t_{01}]}\cup_{\Omega[\mathscr{O}]_{/[0;t_{1}]}}\dotsb\cup_{\Omega[\mathscr{O}]_{/[0;t_{n-1}]}}\Omega[\mathscr{O}]_{/[1;t_{n-1,n}]}.}}

Thus we can reduce to the cases
n=0,1
.
Note that
\Omega[\mathscr{O}]_{/[m;s]}\simeq\Omega[\mathscr{O}]_{/\langle m;s\rangle}
since we defined
[m;s]
as the restriction
i^{*}\langle m;s\rangle
for any forest
\langle m;s\rangle
.
The case
n=0
now follows directly from
Section

3.3
.

Now suppose that
n=1
. Let
\Omega[\mathscr{O}]^{\bot}_{/[1;t]}
be the full subcategory of
\Omega[\mathscr{O}]_{/[1;t]}^{\mathrm{el}}\subset\Omega[\mathscr{O}]_{/[1;t]}
spanned by the maps
[m;s]\to[1;t]
whose underlying map
\phi\colon[m]\to[1]
preserves
0
, so
\phi
is either the the identity or the inclusion of
0
.
Observe that by the same proof as
Section

3.2
, any map
[m;s]\to[1;t]
uniquely factors as a composite
[m;s]\to[i;s^{\prime}]\to[1;t]
such that
[m]\to[i]
preserves the top element and
s^{\prime}_{i}\rightarrowtail s_{m}
is invertible, and the second map lies in
\Omega[\mathscr{O}]^{\bot}_{/[1;t]}
.
This shows that the inclusions
\Omega[\mathscr{O}]^{\bot}_{/[1;t]}\hookrightarrow\Omega[\mathscr{O}]^{\mathrm{el}}_{/[1;t]}
and
\Omega[\mathscr{O}]^{\bot}_{/[1;t]}\hookrightarrow\Omega[\mathscr{O}]_{/[1;t]}
both admit a left adjoint, hence they are final.
We conclude that the inclusion
\Omega[\mathscr{O}]^{\mathrm{el}}_{/[1;t]}\hookrightarrow\Omega[\mathscr{O}]_{/[1;t]}
is final.
∎

4.2.
Functoriality

Every map
f\colon\mathscr{O}\to\mathscr{P}
between algebraic patterns induces a functor
\Omega[f]\colon\Omega[\mathscr{O}]\to\Omega[\mathscr{P}]
between their tree categories. In turn, we obtain an adjunction

\Omega[f]_{!}:\mathrm{PSh}(\Omega[\mathscr{O}])\rightleftarrows\mathrm{PSh}(\Omega[\mathscr{P}]):\Omega[f]^{*}.

One might ask whether this derives to an adjunction between the categories of complete Segal presheaves. We recall the following notion from
[
12
, §4]
:

Definition 4.15

(Chu–Haugseng)
.

A map
f\colon\mathscr{O}\to\mathscr{P}
between algebraic patterns is called
strong Segal
if for every object
t\in\mathscr{O}
, the induced map

\mathscr{O}^{\mathrm{el}}_{t/}\to\mathscr{P}^{\mathrm{el}}_{f(t)/}

is initial.

Proposition 4.16
.

If
f\colon\mathscr{O}\to\mathscr{P}
is strong Segal, then the canonical map

\Omega[f]_{!}(X\boxtimes_{[n]}[n;t])\to X\boxtimes_{[n]}[n;f(t)]

in
\mathrm{PSh}(\Omega[\mathscr{P}])
is an equivalence for every
\mathscr{O}
-forest
\left<n;t\right>
and every simplicial space
X
over
[n]
.

Proof.

In light of
Section

4.1
, it suffices to show this for
X=[n]
. We may now proceed inductively using the forest decomposition formula of
Section

4.1
, and use that the map
(\mathscr{O}^{\mathrm{el},\mathrm{op}}_{n})_{/t}\to(\mathscr{P}_{n}^{\mathrm{el},\mathrm{op}})_{/f(t)}
is final for every
t\in\mathscr{O}_{n}
and
n\geq 0
.
∎

In particular,
\Omega[f]_{!}
preserves the spine inclusions and generating completeness extensions.
We conclude the following.

Corollary 4.17
.

If
f\colon\mathscr{O}\to\mathscr{P}
is a strong Segal map between algebraic patterns, then the adjunction
\Omega[f]_{!}\dashv\Omega[f]^{*}

derives to an adjunction

f_{!}:\mathrm{CSeg}(\Omega[\mathscr{O}])\rightleftarrows\mathrm{CSeg}(\Omega[\mathscr{P}]):f^{*}.

In particular,
\Omega[f]^{*}
preserves complete Segal objects.∎

Remark 4.18
.

We can now rephrase the completeness condition of
Section

4.1
as follows. Suppose that
e\in\mathscr{O}
is an elementary. Then this corresponds to a map
e\colon\ast\to\mathscr{O}
between algebraic patterns where
\ast
is the terminal algebraic pattern. It is automatic that this is a strong Segal morphism, so that
e
induces a functor

\Omega[e]^{*}\colon\mathrm{Seg}(\Omega[\mathscr{O}])\to\mathrm{Seg}(\Delta)
. A Segal
\Omega[\mathscr{O}]
-space
X
is complete if and only if for every elementary
e\in\mathscr{O}
, the Segal space
\Omega[e]^{*}X
is complete in the sense of Rezk
[
31
]
.

Using the initiality of
\mathscr{O}^{\mathrm{el}}_{t/}\to\mathscr{P}^{\mathrm{el}}_{f(t)/}
, one also obtains the following analogue of
Section

4.2
for algebrads.
We write
\mathrm{Cocart}^{\mathrm{int}}(\mathscr{O})
for the category of functors
\mathscr{C}\to\mathscr{O}
that are cocartesian over
\mathscr{O}^{\mathrm{int}}
(cf.
Section

2.1
).

Lemma 4.19
.

If
f\colon\mathscr{O}\to\mathscr{P}
is a strong Segal morphism between algebraic patterns, then the pullback functor
f^{*}\colon\mathrm{Cocart}^{\mathrm{int}}(\mathscr{P})\to\mathrm{Cocart}^{\mathrm{int}}(\mathscr{O})
preserves algebrads. In particular, it restricts to a functor
f^{*}\colon\mathrm{Algad}(\mathscr{P})\to\mathrm{Algad}(\mathscr{O})
. ∎

4.3.
The equivalence with algebrads

We will now show
D
from the introduction, namely that algebrads coincide with complete Segal
\Omega[\mathscr{O}]
-spaces.

Lemma 4.20
.

Suppose that we have a map

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{C}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

between cocartesian fibrations on
\mathscr{C}
. Then
f
is a left fibration if and only if
f
is a fiberwise left fibration, i.e.
f_{x}\colon E_{x}\to E^{\prime}_{x}
is a left fibration for every
x\in\mathscr{C}
.

Proof.

This is an easy exercise, or, alternatively, the statement is obtained by combining Propositions 2.4.2.11, 2.4.2.8 and 2.4.2.4 of
[
24
]
.
∎

Theorem 4.21
.

There exists an equivalence

\mathrm{Algad}(\mathscr{O})\simeq\mathrm{CSeg}(\Omega[\mathscr{O}]).

that is natural in strong Segal maps between algebraic patterns.

Proof.

We will first prove the equivalence
\mathrm{Algad}(\mathscr{O})\simeq\mathrm{CSeg}(\Omega[\mathscr{O}])
, and afterwards show that it is natural in strong Segal maps.
The straightening equivalence induces an equivalence on slice categories

\mathrm{Fun}(\Delta^{\mathrm{op}},\mathrm{Cat})_{/\mathscr{O}}\to\mathrm{Cocart}(\Delta^{\mathrm{op}})_{/\Phi[\mathscr{O}]^{\mathrm{op}}}
.
Let us write
\mathscr{C}\subset\mathrm{Fun}(\Delta^{\mathrm{op}},\mathrm{Cat})_{/\mathscr{O}}
for the full subcategory spanned by the maps
X\to\mathscr{O}
between simplicial categories such that
X_{n}\to\mathscr{O}_{n}
is a left fibration for every
n
.
On account of
Section

4.3
, the restriction of the straightening equivalence to
\mathscr{C}
factors through the inclusion
\mathrm{LFib}(\Phi[\mathscr{O}]^{\mathrm{op}})\to\mathrm{Cocart}(\Delta^{\mathrm{op}})_{/\Phi[\mathscr{O}]^{\mathrm{op}}}
via an equivalence

\mathscr{C}\to\mathrm{LFib}(\Phi[\mathscr{O}]^{\mathrm{op}})\simeq\mathrm{PSh}(\Phi[\mathscr{O}]).

Unwinding the definitions, we see that the essential image of the algebrads
\mathrm{Algad}(\mathscr{O})\subset\mathscr{C}
is given by the full subcategory

\mathrm{CSeg}(\Phi[\mathscr{O}])\subset\mathrm{PSh}(\Phi[\mathscr{O}])

that is spanned by those presheaves
X
such that

(1)

for any forest
\left<n;t\right>
, the map

X(\left<n;t\right>)\to X(\left<1;t_{0,1}\right>)\times_{X(\left<0;t_{1}\right>)}\cdots\times_{X(\left<0;t_{n-1}\right>}X(\left<1;t_{n-1,n}\right>)

is an equivalence,

(2)

for any
x
in
\mathscr{O}
, the map

X(\left<0;x\right>)\to X(\left<3;x=\cdots=x\right>)\times_{X(\left<1;x=x\right>)\times X(\left<1;x=x\right>)}(X(\left<0;x\right>)\times X(\left<0;x\right>))

is an equivalence,

(3)

for every
x
in
\mathscr{O}
, the map

X(\left<0;x\right>)\to\lim_{e\in\mathscr{O}^{\mathrm{el}}_{x/}}X(\left<0;e\right>)

is an equivalence, and

(4)

for every forest
\left<1;t\right>
of length 1, the map

X(\left<1;t\right>)\to X(\left<0;t_{0}\right>)\times_{\lim_{s\in(\mathscr{O}_{1})^{\mathrm{el}}_{t/}}X(\left<0;s_{0}\right>)}\lim_{s\in(\mathscr{O}_{1})^{\mathrm{el}}_{t/}}X(\left<1;s\right>)

is an equivalence.

Items
Item

1
and
Item

2
correspond precisely to the condition that
X
comes from a complete double category.
Conditions
Item

3
and
Item

4
correspond to the last two conditions of
Section

2.3
.

We will now show that the fully faithful functor
i_{*}\colon\mathrm{PSh}(\Omega[\mathscr{O}])\to\mathrm{PSh}(\Phi[\mathscr{O}])
identifies
\mathrm{CSeg}(\Omega[\mathscr{O}])
with
\mathrm{CSeg}(\Phi[\mathscr{O}])
.
It follows from
Section

3.3
that any object in
\mathrm{CSeg}(\Phi[\mathscr{O}])
is of the form
i_{*}X
for some
\Omega[\mathscr{O}]
-space
X
, and it follows from
Section

4.1
that
X
lies in
\mathrm{CSeg}(\Omega[\mathscr{O}])
.
It therefore remains to show that for any complete Segal
\Omega[\mathscr{O}]
-space
X
, its right Kan extension
i_{*}X
lies in
\mathrm{CSeg}(\Phi[\mathscr{O}])
.
Observe that items
Item

3
and
Item

4
hold by
Section

3.3
.
By
Section

4.1
,
X
is local with respect to
\mathrm{Sp}[n;t]\to[n;t]
for any forest
[n;t]
, hence condition
Item

1
holds.
Finally, it follows from
Section

4.1
that
X
is local with respect to
J\boxtimes_{[0]}[0;x]\to[0;x]
for any
x
in
\mathscr{O}
, hence condition
Item

2
holds for
i_{*}X
.
We conclude that
\mathrm{CSeg}(\Omega[\mathscr{O}])\simeq\mathrm{CSeg}(\Phi[\mathscr{O}])\simeq\mathrm{Algad}(\mathscr{O})
.

To prove that the equivalence is natural, write
\mathrm{AlgPatt}^{\mathrm{Seg}}
for the wide subcategory of
\mathrm{AlgPatt}
spanned by the strong Segal morphisms.
It follows from
Section

4.2
that we have a functor
\mathrm{Algad}(-)\colon\mathrm{AlgPatt}^{\mathrm{Seg,op}}\to\mathrm{Cat}
.
We wish to show that this is equivalent to the functor

\Psi\colon\mathrm{AlgPatt}^{\mathrm{Seg,op}}\to\mathrm{Cat};\quad\mathscr{O}\mapsto\mathrm{CSeg}(\Omega[\mathscr{O}])

that exists by
Section

4.2
as a subfunctor of
\mathrm{PSh}(-)
.
Note that
\Psi
sends
f\colon\mathscr{P}\to\mathscr{O}
to the functor
\mathrm{CSeg}(\Omega[\mathscr{O}])\to\mathrm{CSeg}(\Omega[\mathscr{P}])
that restricts along
\Omega[f]
.
A proof similar to
Section

4.2
, combined with
Section

3.3
, shows that this agrees with restriction along
\Phi[f]
under the equivalence
\mathrm{CSeg}(\Phi[\mathscr{O}])\simeq\mathrm{CSeg}(\Omega[\mathscr{O}])
.
Observe that the cartesian fibration corresponding to the functor
\mathrm{PSh}(\Phi[-])
is given by the pullback

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathscr{E}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{LFib}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{AlgPatt}^{\mathrm{Seg}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Cat},}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{target}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Phi[-]^{\mathrm{op}}}

so the cartesian fibration corresponding to the functor
\Psi
is the subfibration of
p
whose fiber over an algebraic pattern
\mathscr{O}
is
\mathrm{CSeg}(\Phi[\mathscr{O}])\subset\mathrm{PSh}(\Phi[\mathscr{O}])\simeq\mathrm{LFib}(\Phi[\mathscr{O}]^{\mathrm{op}})
.
Let us write
\overline{\Psi}\colon\mathscr{A}\to\mathrm{AlgPatt}^{\mathrm{Seg}}
for this cartesian fibration.
Since
\Phi[\mathscr{O}]^{\mathrm{op}}\to\Delta^{\mathrm{op}}
is constructed as the cocartesian fibration corresponding to the double category
\mathscr{O}^{\mathrm{dbl}}
, we may identify
p
with the pullback

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{E}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{Ar}^{\mathrm{LFib}}(\mathrm{Fun}(\Delta^{\mathrm{op}},\mathrm{Cat}))\times_{\mathrm{Fun}(\Delta^{\mathrm{op}},\mathrm{Cat})}\mathrm{DblCat}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{Ar}^{\mathrm{LFib}}(\mathrm{Cocart}(\Delta^{\mathrm{op}}))}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{AlgPatt}^{\mathrm{Seg}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{DblCat}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{Cocart}(\Delta^{\mathrm{op}})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{target}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{target}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(-)^{\mathrm{dbl}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\smallint_{\Delta^{\mathrm{op}}}(-)}

where
\mathrm{Ar}^{\mathrm{LFib}}(-)
denotes the full subcategory of the arrow category spanned by componentwise or fiberwise left fibrations.
Using the equivalence
\mathrm{AlgPatt}\simeq\mathrm{DblPatt}
from
Section

2.2
and unwinding the equivalence
\mathrm{Algad}(\mathscr{O})\simeq\mathrm{CSeg}(\Phi[\mathscr{O}])
proved above, it follows that the cartesian fibration
\overline{\Psi}\colon\mathscr{A}\to\mathrm{AlgPatt}^{\mathrm{Seg}}
is equivalent to the functor
\mathrm{Algad}\to\mathrm{AlgPatt}^{\mathrm{Seg}}
defined as follows:

•

\mathrm{Algad}
is the subcategory of
\mathrm{Ar}(\mathrm{AlgPatt})

–

whose objects are algebrads
\mathscr{P}\to\mathscr{O}
such that
\mathscr{P}
has the algebraic pattern structure from
Section

2.1
,

–

whose morphisms are squares

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathscr{P}^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{P}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathscr{O}^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{O}}

such that
\mathscr{O}^{\prime}\to\mathscr{O}
is a strong Segal morphism, and

•

the functor
\mathrm{Algad}\to\mathrm{AlgPatt}^{\mathrm{Seg}}
is the target projection.

This cartesian fibration precisely encodes the functor
\mathrm{Algad}(-)\colon\mathrm{AlgPatt}^{\mathrm{Seg}}\to\mathrm{Cat}
.
∎

4.4.
Unraveling the equivalence

We will now give an explicit description of the functor underlying the equivalence
\mathrm{Algad}(\mathscr{O})\simeq\mathrm{CSeg}(\Omega[\mathscr{O}]^{\mathrm{op}})
from
Theorem

4.21
.

Let
\mathscr{O}
be an algebraic pattern,
\mathscr{P}\to\mathscr{O}
an algebrad and
X\colon\Omega[\mathscr{O}]^{\mathrm{op}}\to\mathscr{S}
its corresponding complete Segal
\Omega[\mathscr{O}]
-space under the equivalence
\mathrm{Algad}(\mathscr{O})\simeq\mathrm{CSeg}(\Omega[\mathscr{O}])
.
Then
X
is obtained from
\mathscr{P}
by first considering its associated double category
\mathrm{Sq}_{L,R}(\mathscr{P})\to\mathscr{O}^{\mathrm{dbl}}
, then unstraightening this over
\Delta^{\mathrm{op}}
to obtain a left fibration
\smallint_{\Delta^{\mathrm{op}}}\mathrm{Sq}_{L,R}(\mathscr{P})\to\Phi[\mathscr{O}]^{\mathrm{op}}
, subsequently straightening this to obtain a presheaf on
\Phi[\mathscr{O}]
and finally restricting along
i\colon\Omega[\mathscr{O}]\to\Phi[\mathscr{O}]
.
Let
[n;t]
be a tree and write
\overline{t}\colon[n]\to\mathscr{O}^{\mathrm{act}}
for the corresponding functor.
It follows by construction that the value of
X
at a tree
[n;t]
is given by

X([n;t])\simeq\mathrm{Hom}_{/\mathscr{O}}(\overline{t},\mathscr{P}).

Write
\mathrm{Cocart}^{\mathrm{int}}(\mathscr{O})
for the subcategory of
\mathrm{Cat}_{/\mathscr{O}}
spanned by functors that admit cocartesian lifts of inerts, and whose morphisms preserve cocartesian lifts of inerts.
Let us write
[t]^{\mathrm{int}}=[n]\times_{\mathscr{O}}\mathrm{Ar}^{\mathrm{int}}(\mathscr{O})
.
Here
\mathrm{Ar}^{\mathrm{int}}(\mathscr{O})\subset\mathrm{Ar}(\mathscr{O})
is the full subcategory spanned by the inert morphisms, and the pullback is taken along
\overline{t}\colon[n]\to\mathscr{O}
and
\mathrm{ev}_{0}\colon\mathrm{Ar}^{\mathrm{int}}(\mathscr{O})\to\mathscr{O}
.
By
[
5
, Corollary 2.1.5]
, the target projection
[t]^{\mathrm{int}}\to\mathscr{O}
is the free cocartesian fibration over
\mathscr{O}^{\mathrm{int}}
on
\overline{t}
, hence there is an equivalence

X([n;t])\simeq\mathrm{Hom}_{/\mathscr{O}}(\overline{t},\mathscr{P})\simeq\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{Cocart}^{\mathrm{int}}(\mathscr{O})}([t]^{\mathrm{int}},\mathscr{P}).

We obtain the following explicit description of the equivalence
\mathrm{Algad}(\mathscr{O})\simeq\mathrm{CSeg}(\Omega[\mathscr{O}])
as the “nerve” with respect to the functor
\Omega[\mathscr{O}]\to\mathrm{Cocart}^{\mathrm{int}}(\mathscr{O})
that sends
[n;t]
to
[t]^{\mathrm{int}}
.

Proposition 4.22
.

Let
\mathscr{O}
be an algebraic pattern.
Then the category
\Omega[\mathscr{O}]
is equivalent to the full subcategory of
\mathrm{Cocart}^{\mathrm{int}}(\mathscr{O})
spanned by objects of the form
[t]^{\mathrm{int}}
for
[n;t]
a tree,
and the equivalence
\mathrm{Algad}(\mathscr{O})\xrightarrow{\simeq}\mathrm{CSeg}(\Omega[\mathscr{O}])
is given by the functor

\mathscr{P}\mapsto\left([n;t]\mapsto\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{Cocart}^{\mathrm{int}}(\mathscr{O})}([t]^{\mathrm{int}},\mathscr{P})\right).

Proof.

First consider the case where every object of
\mathscr{O}
is elementary, so
\Omega[\mathscr{O}]=\Phi[\mathscr{O}]
and
\mathrm{Algad}(\mathscr{O})=\mathrm{Cocart}^{\mathrm{int}}(\mathscr{O})
.
In this case it follows from
Section

7.4
and
Section

8.1
that all trees
[n;t]
lie in
\mathrm{CSeg}(\Omega[\mathscr{O}])
. (Alternatively, this is straightforward to show by hand.)
Write
\Psi
for the equivalence
\mathrm{Cocart}^{\mathrm{int}}(\mathscr{O})=\mathrm{Algad}(\mathscr{O})\xrightarrow{\simeq}\mathrm{CSeg}(\Omega[\mathscr{O}])
from
Theorem

4.21
.
By the discussion above, we have natural equivalences

\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{Cocart}^{\mathrm{int}}(\mathscr{O})}({}^{-1}[n;t],\mathscr{P})\simeq\Psi(\mathscr{P})([n;t])\simeq\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{Cocart}^{\mathrm{int}}(\mathscr{O})}([t]^{\mathrm{int}},\mathscr{P}).

This proves the proposition when every object of
\mathscr{O}
is elementary.
For a general algebraic pattern
\mathscr{O}
, write
\mathscr{O}^{\sharp}
for the algebraic pattern structure with the same inert-active factorization system, but where every object is elementary.
The result then follows for
\mathscr{O}
since
Theorem

4.21
identifies
\mathrm{CSeg}(\Omega[\mathscr{O}])
with the full subcategory of
\mathrm{CSeg}(\Omega[\mathscr{O}^{\sharp}])
spanned by those objects that are right Kan extended along
\Omega[\mathscr{O}]\hookrightarrow\Omega[\mathscr{O}^{\sharp}]=\Phi[\mathscr{O}]
.
∎

Remark 4.23
.

The category
\mathrm{Algad}(\mathscr{O})
is a reflective localization of
\mathrm{Cocart}^{\mathrm{int}}(\mathscr{O})
; write
L
for this localization.
Then we obtain a functor
\Omega[\mathscr{O}]\to\mathrm{Algad}(\mathscr{O})
given by
[n;t]\mapsto L([t]^{\mathrm{int}})
, and the equivalence
\mathrm{Algad}(\mathscr{O})\simeq\mathrm{CSeg}(\Omega[\mathscr{O}])
is given by the nerve with respect to this functor.

Remark 4.24
.

Let
\mathscr{P}
be an
\mathscr{O}
-algebrad and
X
its corresponding complete Segal
\Omega[\mathscr{O}]
-space.
Suppose that
t=t_{0}\rightsquigarrow\dotsb\rightsquigarrow t_{n}
is a string of active arrows in
\mathscr{O}
corresponding to the functor
\overline{t}\colon[n]\to\mathscr{O}^{\mathrm{act}}
and let
\phi\colon[m]\to[n]
be a map in
\Delta
.
The equivalence
X([n;t])\simeq\mathrm{Hom}_{/\mathscr{O}}(\overline{t},\mathscr{P})
generalizes to an equivalence

\mathrm{Hom}([m]\boxtimes_{[n]}[n;t],X)\simeq X([m;\phi^{*}(t)])\simeq\mathrm{Hom}_{/\mathscr{O}}(\overline{\phi^{*}(t)},\mathscr{P}),

where
\overline{\phi^{*}(t)}
denotes the composite
[m]\xrightarrow{\phi}[n]\xrightarrow{\overline{t}}\mathscr{O}^{\mathrm{act}}
corresponding to the string
\phi^{*}(t)=t_{\phi(0)}\rightsquigarrow t_{\phi(1)}\rightsquigarrow\dotsb\rightsquigarrow t_{\phi(m)}
.

5.
Exponentiable maps between algebrads

This section is dedicated to the study of
the exponentiable objects and morphisms in the category of
\mathscr{O}
-algebrads. In particular, we establish
A
.

Definition 5.1
.

Let
\mathscr{C}
be a category that admits finite limits.
A morphism
f\colon x\to y
in
\mathscr{C}
is called
exponentiable
if
f^{*}\colon\mathscr{C}_{/y}\to\mathscr{C}_{/x}
is a left adjoint.
An object
x
is
exponentiable
if the unique morphism
x\to\ast
to the terminal object is exponentiable.

Our goal is to show the following theorem:

Theorem 5.2
.

Let
\mathscr{O}
be an algebraic pattern. A map
f\colon X\to Y
in
\mathrm{CSeg}(\Omega[\mathscr{O}])
is exponentiable if the following condition is satisfied:

(1)

For every tree
[2;t]\to Y
of length
2
, the following map is an equivalence:

\mathop{colim}\displaylimits_{[k]\in\Delta^{\mathrm{op}}}\mathrm{Hom}_{/Y}([1+k+1;t_{0}\rightsquigarrow\underbrace{t_{1}=\cdots=t_{1}}_{k\text{-times}}\rightsquigarrow t_{2}],X)\to\mathrm{Hom}_{/Y}([1;t_{0}\rightsquigarrow t_{2}],X).

Remark 5.3
.

The proof of
Theorem

5.2
will show that the same result also holds when replacing the category
\mathrm{CSeg}(\Omega[\mathscr{O}])
with the larger category
\mathrm{Seg}(\Omega[\mathscr{O}])
that also includes non-complete Segal
\Omega[\mathscr{O}]
-spaces.

5.1.
Exponentiability in the category of algebrads

Before embarking on the proof of
Theorem

5.2
, we will use the equivalence
\mathrm{CSeg}(\Omega[\mathscr{O}])\simeq\mathrm{Algad}(\mathscr{O})
from
Theorem

4.21
to describe the exponentiable morphisms in
\mathrm{Algad}(\mathscr{O})
.
In particular, this proves
A
.

Lemma 5.4
.

Let
\mathscr{O}
be an algebraic pattern. Suppose that
\mathscr{P}\to\mathscr{Q}
is a map of
\mathscr{O}
-algebrads, equivalently given by a map
X\to Y
of complete Segal
\Omega[\mathscr{O}]
-spaces. Then the following assertions are equivalent:

(1)

Condition
Item

1
of
Theorem

5.2
holds for
X\to Y
.

(2)

For any composable pair of active morphisms
h\colon x\rightsquigarrow y
and
g\colon y\rightsquigarrow e
with
e
elementary in
\mathscr{Q}
, and any lift
f\colon\bar{x}\to\bar{e}
of
g\circ h
, the category

\mathrm{Fact}(f\mid g\circ h)\coloneqq\{x\overset{h}{\rightsquigarrow}y\overset{g}{\rightsquigarrow}e\}\times_{(\mathscr{Q}_{/{e}})_{{gh}/}}(\mathscr{P}_{/\bar{e}})_{f/}.

is weakly contractible.

Proof.

Let a map
[2;t]\to Y
from a tree be given. Viewing
t
as a map
[2]\to\mathscr{O}^{\mathrm{act}}
, the map
[2;t]\to Y
corresponds to a map
[2]\to\mathscr{Q}^{\mathrm{act}}
over
\mathscr{O}^{\mathrm{act}}
; in other words, a pair of active morphisms
h\colon x\rightsquigarrow y
and
g\colon y\rightsquigarrow e
in
\mathscr{Q}
over
t
. (Note that
e
is elementary by definition.)
It follows from
Section

4.4
that we may identify the map

\mathop{colim}\displaylimits_{[k]\in\Delta^{\mathrm{op}}}\mathrm{Hom}_{/Y}([1+k+1]\boxtimes_{[2]}[2;t],X)\to\mathrm{Hom}_{/Y}([1]\boxtimes_{[2]}[2;t],X)

with the map

\mathop{colim}\displaylimits_{[k]\in\Delta^{\mathrm{op}}}\mathrm{Hom}_{/\mathscr{Q}}([1+k+1],\mathscr{P})\to\mathrm{Hom}_{/\mathscr{Q}}([1],\mathscr{P}).

An argument similar to
[
4
, Lemma 2.2.8]
, using universality of colimits in spaces, shows that the fiber of this map over
f\colon\bar{x}\rightsquigarrow\bar{e}
is given by the geometric realization of the category

\mathrm{Fact}(f\mid g\circ h)
.
We conclude that
\mathscr{P}\to\mathscr{Q}
satisfies condition (2) of this lemma if and only if
X\to Y
satisfies the condition from
Theorem

5.2
.
∎

We obtain the following as an immediate corollary of
Theorem

5.2
and
Section

5.1
:

Theorem 5.5
.

Let
\mathscr{O}
be an algebraic pattern and
\mathscr{P}\to\mathscr{Q}
a map of
\mathscr{O}
-algebrads.
Then
\mathscr{P}\to\mathscr{Q}
is exponentiable if for any composable pair of active morphisms
h\colon x\rightsquigarrow y
and
g\colon y\rightsquigarrow e
with
e
elementary in
\mathscr{Q}
, and any lift
f\colon\bar{x}\to\bar{e}
of
g\circ h
, the category

\mathrm{Fact}(f\mid g\circ h)\coloneqq\{x\overset{h}{\rightsquigarrow}y\overset{g}{\rightsquigarrow}e\}\times_{(\mathscr{Q}_{/{e}})_{{gh}/}}(\mathscr{P}_{/\bar{e}})_{f/}.

is weakly contractible.∎

Remark 5.6
.

In the case that
\mathscr{O}=\ast
is the terminal pattern, this recovers the Conduché criterion for exponentiable functors between categories that was exhibited by Lurie
[
25
, Proposition B.3.14]
and Ayala–Francis–Rozenblyum
[
3
, Lemma 5.16(2)]
.

Remark 5.7
.

The condition from
Theorem

5.5
can be rephrased as follows: for every functor
t\colon[2]\to\mathscr{Q}^{\mathrm{act}}
such that
t(2)
is elementary, the base-change

[2]\times_{\mathscr{Q}}\mathscr{P}\to[2]

is exponentiable in
\mathrm{Cat}
.

Remark 5.8
.

It follows that whenever
\mathscr{P}^{\mathrm{act}}\to\mathscr{Q}^{\mathrm{act}}
is exponentiable in
\mathrm{Cat}
, then
\mathscr{P}\to\mathscr{Q}
is exponentiable in
\mathrm{Algad}(\mathscr{O})
.
However, the condition from
Theorem

5.5
is generally weaker than the condition that
\mathscr{P}^{\mathrm{act}}\to\mathscr{Q}^{\mathrm{act}}
is exponentiable.
An explicit counterexample is described in
Section

9.3
.

5.2.
A first reduction step

We start the proof of
Theorem

5.2
by making a few reduction steps.

Lemma 5.9
.

Suppose that
[0;e]
is a root. If

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[J;e]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[0;e]}.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces v}

is a pullback square in
\mathrm{PSh}(\Omega[\mathscr{O}])
, then
v
is a complete Segal extension.

Proof.

We have to show that the map
v:J\boxtimes_{[0]}X\to X
is a complete Segal extension for every
X\to[0;e]
. Since both sides are cocontinuous in
X\in\mathrm{PSh}(\Omega[\mathscr{O}])_{/[0;e]}
, we may assume that
X=[n;t]
. In light of
Section

3.2
and
Section

4.1
, the map
X\to[0;e]
is then of the form
[n]\boxtimes_{[0]}[0;e^{\prime}]\to[0;e]
, with
e^{\prime}
an elementary. By pasting pullback squares,
v
may now be identified with the map

(J\times[n])\boxtimes_{[0]}[0;e^{\prime}]\to[n]\boxtimes_{[0]}[0;e^{\prime}]

induced by the projection
J\times[n]\to[n]
. But the latter map is a complete Segal extension, so that the result follows from
Section

4.1
.
∎

Notation 5.10
.

There is a canonical inclusion

\Gamma[n]\coloneqq[n-1]\cup_{[0]}[1]\to[n]

of simplicial spaces for every
n\geq 0
. Via
Section

4.1
, this induces an inclusion

\Gamma[n;t]\coloneqq\Gamma[n]\boxtimes_{[n]}[n;t]\to[n;t]

for every tree
[n;t]
.

Remark 5.11
.

Via an easy induction argument, it is readily verified that the Segal extensions are generated by the inclusions
\Gamma[n;t]\to[n;t]
where
[n;t]
ranges over all trees.

Lemma 5.12
.

Let
f\colon X\to Y
be a map between complete Segal
\Omega[\mathscr{O}]
-spaces. Then
f
is exponentiable if for every commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X^{\prime\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Gamma[n;t]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[n;t]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces v}

of pullback squares, with
[n;t]
a tree, the map
v
is a complete Segal extension.

Proof.

As presheaf categories are locally cartesian closed, there is an adjunction

f^{*}:\mathrm{PSh}(\Omega[\mathscr{O}])_{/Y}\rightleftarrows\mathrm{PSh}(\Omega[\mathscr{O}])_{/X}:f_{*}.

The base change functor
\mathrm{CSeg}(\Omega[\mathscr{O}])_{/Y}\to\mathrm{CSeg}(\Omega[\mathscr{O}])_{/X}
induced by
f
is restricted from this left adjoint, and we will also denote it by
f^{*}
.
Suppose that
f
meets the condition considered in the statement. Then this hypothesis and
Section

5.2
, combined with
Section

5.2
, imply that the functor
f^{*}
on presheaf categories preserves all complete Segal extensions over
Y
.
Thus the functor
f^{*}\colon\mathrm{CSeg}(\Omega[\mathscr{O}])_{/Y}\to\mathrm{CSeg}(\Omega[\mathscr{O}])_{/X}
is cocontinuous.
∎

5.3.
An explicit Segal replacement

In light of
Section

5.2
, we may reduce to the case that
Y
is given by a tree
[n;t]
. Our next goal is to exhibit an explicit Segal replacement for the pullback
X\times_{[n;t]}\Gamma[n;t]\to[n;t]
. We will make use of the following terminology:

Definition 5.13
.

A map
\phi\colon[m]\to[n]
is called
n
-concave
if there exists a (necessarily unique) integer
0\leq l<m
such that
\phi(l)<n-1
and
\phi(l+1)=n
. Otherwise,
\phi
is called
n
-convex
. We will write
\Lambda_{/[n]}\subset\Delta_{/[n]}
for the full subcategory spanned by the
n
-convex maps.

Similarly, we will say that a map
f\colon[m;s]\to[n;t]
in
\Omega[\mathscr{O}]
is
n
-convex
or
n
-concave
whenever the underlying simplicial map
\phi\colon[m]\to[n]
is. We will write
\Lambda[\mathscr{O}]_{/[n;t]}\coloneqq\Omega[\mathscr{O}]_{/[n;t]}\times_{\Delta_{/[n]}}\Lambda_{/[n]}
for the full subcategory of
\Omega[\mathscr{O}]_{/[n;t]}
spanned by the
n
-convex maps.

Construction 5.14
.

The inclusion
j\colon\Lambda[\mathscr{O}]_{/[n;t]}\to\Omega[\mathscr{O}]_{/[n;t]}

induces an adjunction

j_{!}:\mathrm{PSh}(\Lambda[\mathscr{O}]_{/[n;t]})\rightleftarrows\mathrm{PSh}(\Omega[\mathscr{O}])_{/[n;t]}:j^{*},

where the left adjoint is computed by left Kan extensions.
We define the
replacement
endofunctor
Q
by setting

Q\coloneqq j_{!}j^{*}\colon\mathrm{PSh}(\Omega[\mathscr{O}])_{/[n;t]}\to\mathrm{PSh}(\Omega[\mathscr{O}])_{/[n;t]}.

The counit gives rise to a natural map

\alpha_{X}\colon QX\to X

for
X\in\mathrm{PSh}(\Omega[\mathscr{O}])_{/[n;t]}
. Since the inclusion
\Omega[\mathscr{O}]_{/\Gamma[n;{t}]}\subset\Omega[\mathscr{O}]_{/[n;t]}
factors through
\Lambda[\mathscr{O}]_{/[n;t]}
, the induced map

QX\times_{[n;t]}\Gamma[n;t]\to X\times_{[n;t]}\Gamma[n;t]

is an equivalence for every
X\in\mathrm{PSh}(\Omega[\mathscr{O}])_{/[n;t]}
. All in all, we obtain a natural factorization

X\times_{[n;t]}\Gamma[n;t]\to QX\to X.

We will show that
Q
can be computed using simplicial resolutions of
n
-concave maps by
n
-convex ones.

Construction 5.15
.

Let
\Xi_{/[n]}\subset\Delta_{/[n]}
denote the full subcategory spanned by the maps that do not hit
n-1
.
Suppose that
k\geq 0
is an integer. Let
\phi\colon[m]\to[n]
be a map in
\Xi_{/[n]}
. If
\phi
is
n
-concave, then there must exist a unique integer
0\leq l<m
so that

\phi(l)<n-1
and
\phi(l+1)=n
. We will then consider the
n
-convex map

T_{k}\phi\colon[m+k+1]\to[n]

described by

(T_{k}\phi)(i)=\begin{cases}\phi(i)&\text{if $i\leq l$,}\\
n-1&\text{if $l+1\leq i\leq l+k+1$},\\
n&\textit{if $l+k+2\leq i$}.\end{cases}

In other words,
T_{k}\phi
coincides with
\phi
on the inclusion
\{0,\dotsc,l,l+k+2,\dots,m+k+1\}\colon[m]\to[m+k+1]
, and it is constant to
n-1
on the inclusion
\{l+1,\dotsc,l+k+1\}\colon[k]\to[m+k+1]
.
If
\phi\colon[m]\to[n]
is
n
-convex, then we set

T_{k}\phi\coloneqq\phi.

One readily verifies that this definition assembles to a functor

T\colon\Delta\times\Xi_{/[n]}\to\Delta_{/[n]};\quad([k],\phi)\mapsto T_{k}\phi.

We note that there is a canonical map

\eta_{k,\phi}\colon\phi\to T_{k}\phi

that is natural in
\phi\in\Xi_{/[n]}
and
[k]\in\Delta
.

Proposition 5.16
.

Let
[n;s]\to[n;t]
be a map of trees above
\mathrm{id}_{[n]}\colon[n]\to[n]
.
Suppose that
X\in\mathrm{PSh}(\Omega[\mathscr{O}])_{/[n;t]}
and
\phi\colon[m]\to[n]\in\Xi_{/[n]}
, then the natural maps in the span

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathop{colim}\displaylimits_{[k]\in\Delta^{\mathrm{op}}}\mathrm{Hom}_{/[n;t]}(T_{k}[m]\boxtimes_{[n]}[n;s],QX)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Hom}_{/[n;t]}([m]\boxtimes_{[n]}[n;s],QX)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathop{colim}\displaylimits_{[k]\in\Delta^{\mathrm{op}}}\mathrm{Hom}_{/[n;t]}(T_{k}[m]\boxtimes_{[n]}[n;s],X)}

are equivalences. Here, the left leg is induced by
\eta
, and the right leg is induced by
\alpha
.

Proof.

We will first handle the right leg. To this end, it will suffice to verify that the comparison map

\mathrm{Hom}_{/[n;t]}([m^{\prime};s^{\prime}],QX)\to\mathrm{Hom}_{/[n;t]}([m^{\prime};s^{\prime}],X)

is an equivalence for every map of
forests

\left<m^{\prime};s^{\prime}\right>\to\left<n;t\right>
for which the underlying map
[m^{\prime}]\to[n]
is
n
-convex; see
Section

4.1
. This holds by construction if
\left<m^{\prime};s^{\prime}\right>
is a tree. The general case follows by applying the forest decomposition formula of
Section

4.1
.

We will now handle the left leg.
If
\phi
is
n
-convex, then
\eta_{\bullet,\phi}
is the identity componentwise. Since
\Delta
is weakly contractible, this then implies that the left leg is an equivalence.

Suppose now that
\phi
is
n
-concave. Then the natural transformation

\eta^{\prime}_{\bullet,\phi}\coloneqq\eta_{\bullet,\phi}\boxtimes_{[n]}[n;s]\colon\Delta\times[1]\to\mathrm{PSh}(\Omega[\mathscr{O}])_{/[n;t]}

induced by
\eta_{\bullet,\phi}
factors through the Yoneda embedding
\Omega[\mathscr{O}]_{/[n;t]}\to\mathrm{PSh}(\Omega[\mathscr{O}])_{/[n;t]}
.
The target of
\eta^{\prime}_{\bullet,\phi}
has image in
\Lambda[\mathscr{O}]_{/[n;t]}
. Consequently, it induces a map

T_{\bullet}f\colon\Delta\to(\Lambda[\mathscr{O}]_{/[n;t]})_{f/}

where
f
denotes the map
[m;\phi^{*}s]=[m]\boxtimes_{[n]}[n;s]\to[n;t]
. One may then identify the left leg of the span from the statement with the map

\mathop{colim}\displaylimits_{[k]\in\Delta^{\mathrm{op}}}X(T_{k}[m]\boxtimes_{[n]}[n;s])\to\mathop{colim}\displaylimits_{[m^{\prime};s^{\prime}]\to[n;t]\in((\Lambda[\mathscr{O}]_{/[n;t]})^{\mathrm{op}})_{/f}}X([m^{\prime};s^{\prime}])

induced by restriction along
(T_{\bullet}f)^{\mathrm{op}}
. We will conclude the proof by demonstrating that
T_{\bullet}f
is initial.

The equivalence of
Section

3.2
induces an identification

(\Lambda[\mathscr{O}]_{/[n;t]})_{f/}\simeq(\Lambda_{/[n]})_{\phi/}\times((\mathscr{O}^{\mathrm{el},\mathrm{op}}_{n})_{/t})_{\psi/},

where
\psi\colon t\to s
is the morphism in
\mathscr{O}^{\mathrm{el}}_{n}
corresponding to the fixed map
[n;s]\to[n;t]
.
Under this equivalence, the functor
T_{\bullet}f
is given by the functor

(T_{\bullet}\phi,\{\psi\})\colon\Delta\to(\Lambda_{/[n]})_{\phi/}\times((\mathscr{O}^{\mathrm{el},\mathrm{op}}_{n})_{/t})_{\psi/};\quad[k]\mapsto((\phi\to T_{k}\phi),(\psi=\psi)).

This functor is initial since it admits a right adjoint.
∎

Proposition 5.17
.

The map
X\times_{[n;t]}\Gamma[n;{t}]\to QX
is a Segal extension for all maps
p\colon X\to[n;t]
, and
QX\to[n;t]
is a (complete) Segal fibration if
p
is a (complete) Segal fibration.

Proof.

We will first show that the comparison map
X\times_{[n;t]}\Gamma[n;t]\to QX
is a Segal extension for all
p\colon X\to[n;t]
.
As the functors that appear on both sides preserve colimits in
f\colon X\to[n;t]
, it suffices to handle the case that
X
is a tree
[m;s]
.
Suppose that the underlying map
\phi\colon[m]\to[n]
of
f
is
n
-convex, then
the comparison map can be identified with the map

([m]\times_{[n]}\Gamma[n])\boxtimes_{[m]}[m;s]\to[m;s]

induced by the inclusion
[m]\times_{[n]}\Gamma[n]\to[m]
.
If
\phi
skips
n-1
, then this inclusion is the identity. Thus we may assume that
\phi
hits
n-1
. If
\phi
skips
n
, then the inclusion map is the identity as well. Otherwise, both
n-1
and
n
are contained in the image of
\phi
, and one readily checks that
[m]\times_{[n]}\Gamma[n]\to[m]
is a Segal extension so that the desired result follows from
Section

4.1
.

If
\phi
is
n
-concave, then we proceed as follows.
Let
l
be the integer such that
\phi(l)<n-1
and
\phi(l+1)=n
. Then one readily verifies that the map

[m]\times_{[n]}\Gamma[n]\to[m]
is given by the canonical inclusion

V\coloneqq[l]\sqcup[m-l-1]\to[m]
. The comparison map is then given by a map

V\boxtimes_{[m]}[m;s]\to Q[m;s],

and we claim that this is an equivalence.
As both sides are contained in the image of
j_{!}\colon\mathrm{PSh}(\Lambda[\mathscr{O}]_{/[n;t]})\to\mathrm{PSh}(\Omega[\mathscr{O}])_{/[n;t]}
, we have to check that the map

(5)

\mathrm{Hom}_{/[n;t]}([m^{\prime};s^{\prime}],V\boxtimes_{[m]}[m;s])\to\mathrm{Hom}_{/[n;t]}([m^{\prime};s^{\prime}],Q[m;s])\simeq\mathrm{Hom}_{/[n;t]}([m^{\prime};s^{\prime}],[m;s])

is an equivalence for every
n
-convex map
[m^{\prime};s^{\prime}]\to[n;t]
. One may readily verify that the map displayed in
Equation

5
comes from the canonical map
V\boxtimes_{[m]}[m;s]\to[m;s]
, and thus fits in the pullback square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Hom}_{/[n;t]}([m^{\prime};s^{\prime}],V\boxtimes_{[m]}[m;s])}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Hom}_{/[n;t]}([m^{\prime};s^{\prime}],[m;s])}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Hom}_{/[n]}([m^{\prime}],V)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Hom}_{/[n]}([m^{\prime}],[m]).}

The bottom arrow is an equivalence as we assumed that the underlying map
[m^{\prime}]\to[n]
is
n
-convex.

For the final assertion, we must demonstrate that
QX\to[n;t]
is local with respect to the maps
i\colon\mathrm{Sp}[m;s^{\prime}]\to[m;s^{\prime}]
over
[n;t]
. Since
X\to[n;t]
is a Segal fibration,
Section

5.3
implies that it will be sufficient to check the case where the underlying map
\phi\colon[m]\to[n]
is
n
-concave. In particular,
\phi
hits
n
, so that by
Section

3.2
, the map
[m;s^{\prime}]\to[n;t]
factors as
[m;s^{\prime}]\to[n;s]\to[n;t]
where the first arrow is cartesian over
\phi
, and the second arrow lies over the identity on
[n]
. Then we must show that the restriction

i^{*}\colon\mathrm{Hom}_{/[n;t]}([m]\boxtimes_{[n]}[n;s],QX)\to\mathrm{Hom}_{/[n;t]}(\mathrm{Sp}[m]\boxtimes_{[n]}[n;s],QX)

is an equivalence.

As
\phi
is
n
-concave, there exists a unique integer
l
such that
\phi(l)<n-1
and
\phi(l+1)=n
. The restriction
i^{*}
is then given by the gap map (cf.
Section

1
) in the canonical commutative square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Hom}_{/[n;t]}([m]\boxtimes_{[n]}[n;s],QX)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Hom}_{/[n;t]}([1]\boxtimes_{[n]}[n;s],QX)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Hom}_{/[n;t]}((\mathrm{Sp}[l]\sqcup\mathrm{Sp}[m-l-1])\boxtimes_{[n]}[n;s],QX)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Hom}_{/[n;t]}(([0]\sqcup[0])\boxtimes_{[n]}[n;s],QX),}

where the top map is induced by the inclusion
\{l,l+1\}\colon[1]\to[m]
.
In turn, we may identify this square with the commutative square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathop{colim}\displaylimits_{[k]\in\Delta^{\mathrm{op}}}\mathrm{Hom}_{/[n;t]}(T_{k}[m]\boxtimes_{[n]}[n;s],X)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathop{colim}\displaylimits_{[k]\in\Delta^{\mathrm{op}}}\mathrm{Hom}_{/[n;t]}(T_{k}[1]\boxtimes_{[n]}[n;s],X)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Hom}_{/[n;t]}((\mathrm{Sp}[l]\sqcup\mathrm{Sp}[m-l-1])\boxtimes_{[n]}[n;s],X)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Hom}_{/[n;t]}(([0]\sqcup[0])\boxtimes_{[n]}[n;s],X)}

using the computation of
Section

5.3
.
By
Section

4.1
, it will be sufficient to verify that the map of simplicial sets

\textstyle\mathrm{Sp}[l]\bigcupop\displaylimits_{[0]}[1+k+1]\bigcupop\displaylimits_{[0]}\mathrm{Sp}[m-l-1]\to[m+k+1]

is a Segal extension. But this is clear.

Finally, we need to show that
QX\to[n;t]
is local with respect to
[J;e]\to[0;e]
for any elementary
e
if
X\to[n;t]
is.
This follows since any map
[0;e]\to[n;t]
lies in
\Lambda[\mathscr{O}]_{/[n;t]}
.
∎

5.4.
The conclusion of the proof

We will be ready to finish the proof of
Theorem

5.2
after the following last observation:

Lemma 5.18
.

Let
p\colon X\to Y
be a (complete) Segal fibration between
\Omega[\mathscr{O}]
-spaces. Then the following are equivalent:

(1)

For every diagram of pullback squares

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{X^{\prime\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{X^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Gamma[n;{t}]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[n;t]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y,}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces v}

v
is a (complete) Segal extension.

(2)

For every pullback square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{X^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[n;t]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y,}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p}

the canonical map
QX^{\prime}\to X^{\prime}
is an equivalence.

(3)

For every map from a tree
[2;t]\to Y
, the induced map

\mathop{colim}\displaylimits_{[k]\in\Delta^{\mathrm{op}}}\mathrm{Hom}_{/Y}(T_{k}[1]\boxtimes_{[2]}[2;t],X)\to\mathrm{Hom}_{/Y}([1]\boxtimes_{[2]}[2;t],X)

is an equivalence.

Proof.

In the situation of (1), we have a factorization

X^{\prime\prime}\xrightarrow{v^{\prime}}QX^{\prime}\xrightarrow{v^{\prime\prime}}X^{\prime}

of
v
.
On account of
Section

5.3
,
v^{\prime}
is a Segal extension and
QX^{\prime}\to[n;t]
is a (complete) Segal fibration. By left cancellation, this implies that
v^{\prime\prime}
is a (complete) Segal fibration as well.
Thus
v
is a (complete) Segal extension if and only if
v^{\prime\prime}
is an equivalence. This shows that (1) and (2) are equivalent.

Suppose that (2) holds.
Let
f\colon[2;t]\to Y
be a map. Then for
X^{\prime}\coloneqq X\times_{Y}[2;t]
, the map
QX^{\prime}\to X^{\prime}
is an equivalence. Thus it follows from
Section

5.3
that the map

\mathop{colim}\displaylimits_{[k]\in\Delta^{\mathrm{op}}}\mathrm{Hom}_{/[2;t]}(T_{k}[1]\boxtimes_{[2]}[2;t],X^{\prime})\to\mathrm{Hom}_{/[2;t]}([1]\boxtimes_{[2]}[2;t],X^{\prime})

is an equivalence. But this can be identified with the map from (3).

For the final step, we suppose that (3) holds. By reasoning as in the proof of
Section

5.3
, assertion (2) holds if and only if the map

\mathrm{Hom}_{/[n;t]}([m]\boxtimes_{[n]}[n;s],QX^{\prime})\to\mathrm{Hom}_{/[n;t]}([m]\boxtimes_{[n]}[n;s],X^{\prime})

is an equivalence for every
n
-concave map
\phi\colon[m]\to[n]
and map
s\to t\in\mathscr{O}^{\mathrm{el}}_{n}
giving rise to a map
[n;s]\to[n;t]
of trees.
In light of
Section

5.3
, this is computed by the map

\mathop{colim}\displaylimits_{[k]\in\Delta^{\mathrm{op}}}\mathrm{Hom}_{/[n;t]}(T_{k}[m]\boxtimes_{[n]}[n;s],X^{\prime})\to\mathrm{Hom}_{/[n;t]}([m]\boxtimes_{[n]}[n;s],X^{\prime}).

Let
0\leq l<m
be the integer so that
\phi(l)<n-1
and
\phi(l)=n
. This determines a map
\{l,l+1\}\colon[1]\to[m]
. We have the following natural commutative square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[l]\cup_{[0]}[1]\cup_{[0]}[m-l-1]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[l]\cup_{[0]}T_{k}[1]\cup_{[0]}[m-l-1]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[m]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{T_{k}[m]}}

of simplicial spaces over
[n]
, so that the vertical arrows are Segal extensions. Consequently,
Section

4.1

implies that it suffices to check that the map

(6)

\mathop{colim}\displaylimits_{[k]\in\Delta^{\mathrm{op}}}\mathrm{Hom}_{/[n;t]}(T_{k}[1]\boxtimes_{[n]}[n;s],X^{\prime})\to\mathrm{Hom}_{/[n;t]}([1]\boxtimes_{[n]}[n;s],X^{\prime})

is an equivalence instead.
There is a commutative square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[1]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[2]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[m]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[n]},}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\{l,l+1\}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi}

where
j
is the map that hits
\phi(l)
,
n-1
and
n
. The map
Equation

6
can then be identified with the map

\mathop{colim}\displaylimits_{[k]\in\Delta^{\mathrm{op}}}\mathrm{Hom}_{/Y}(T_{k}[1]\boxtimes_{[2]}[2;j^{*}s],X)\to\mathrm{Hom}_{/Y}([1]\boxtimes_{[2]}[2;j^{*}s],X).

This is an equivalence by the assumption of (3).
∎

Proof of
Theorem

5.2
.

This now follows from combining
Section

5.2
,
Section

5.2
, and
Section

5.4
.
∎

Remark 5.19
.

Suppose that
\mathscr{O}
has the property that all its trees
[n;t]
are complete Segal
\Omega[\mathscr{O}]
-spaces. Then the converse of
Theorem

5.2
is easily shown.
Namely, suppose that
f:X\to Y
is exponentiable in
\mathrm{CSeg}(\Omega[\mathscr{O}])
. Let
[n;t]\to Y
be a map from a tree.
By the assumption on
\mathscr{O}
, we have a colimit expression

\mathop{colim}\displaylimits_{[m;s]\in\Omega[\mathscr{O}]_{/\Gamma[n;t]}}[m;s]\xrightarrow{\simeq}[n;t]

in the category

\mathrm{CSeg}(\Omega[\mathscr{O}])_{/Y}
. In particular, the left adjoint
L\colon\mathrm{PSh}(\Omega[\mathscr{O}])_{/Y}\to\mathrm{CSeg}(\Omega[\mathscr{O}])_{/Y}
takes the map
X\times_{Y}\Gamma[n;t]\simeq\mathop{colim}\displaylimits_{[m;s]\in\Omega[\mathscr{O}]_{/\Gamma[n;t]}}X\times_{Y}[m;s]\to X\times_{Y}[n;t]
to an equivalence.
By
Section

4.1
, this map is a complete Segal extension. One may then invoke
Section

5.4
.

Unfortunately, the condition on the trees
[n;t]
to be complete Segal is quite restrictive. For instance, this fails if
\mathscr{O}
is
\mathbb{F}_{*}^{\flat}
or
\mathrm{Span}(\mathbb{F}_{G})^{\flat}
. We will remedy this situation in
Section

7
, where we introduce so-called
robust
algebraic patterns for which we can show the converse of
Theorem

5.2
in a more elaborate way. There is a special subclass of robust patterns, called the
atomically
robust patterns, for which the trees
[n;t]
are always complete Segal (see
Section

8.1
).

5.5.
Examples

We will now spell out the conditions of
A
for a range of examples of algebraic patterns
\mathscr{O}
.

Example 5.20
.

Let
\mathscr{O}=\Delta^{\mathrm{op},\natural}
be the pattern from
Section

2.1
describing virtual double categories.
By
Theorem

5.5
, it follows that a map
\mathscr{P}\to\mathscr{Q}
in
\mathrm{VirtDblCat}=\mathrm{Algad}(\Delta^{\mathrm{op},\natural})
is exponentiable if
for every functor
t\colon[2]\to\mathscr{Q}^{\mathrm{act}}
such that
t(2)
lies over
[1]
or
[0]
, the base-change
\mathscr{P}^{\mathrm{act}}\times_{\mathscr{Q}^{\mathrm{act}}}[2]\to[2]
is exponentiable in
\mathrm{Cat}
.
Note that if
t(2)=[0]
, then all of
t
must necessarily land in the fiber
\mathscr{Q}_{0}
over
[0]
.
In particular, this condition can be rephrased as follows:
\mathscr{P}\to\mathscr{Q}
is exponentiable if

(1)

the functor
\mathscr{P}_{0}\to\mathscr{Q}_{0}
in
\mathrm{Cat}
is exponentiable, and

(2)

for any
t\colon[2]\to\mathscr{Q}^{\mathrm{act}}
such that
t(2)
lies over
[1]
, the base-change
\mathscr{P}^{\mathrm{act}}\times_{\mathscr{Q}^{\mathrm{act}}}[2]\to[2]
is exponentiable in
\mathrm{Cat}
.

In
Section

8.5
, we will see that the converse also holds: if
\mathscr{P}\to\mathscr{Q}
is exponentiable, then it satisfies these two conditions.
Note that these conditions are strictly weaker than
\mathscr{P}^{\mathrm{act}}\to\mathscr{Q}^{\mathrm{act}}
being exponentiable: in
Section

9.3
we give an explicit example of an exponentiable morphism
\mathscr{P}\to\mathscr{Q}
in
\mathrm{VirtDblCat}
for which
\mathscr{P}^{\mathrm{act}}\to\mathscr{Q}^{\mathrm{act}}
is not exponentiable.

Example 5.21
.

Recall from
Section

2.1
that generalized operads are algebrads for the pattern
\mathbb{F}_{*}^{\natural}
. It follows as in
Section

5.5
that a map
\mathscr{P}\to\mathscr{Q}
of generalized operads is exponentiable in
\mathrm{Algad}(\mathbb{F}_{*}^{\natural})
if

(1)

the functor
\mathscr{P}_{\langle 0\rangle}\to\mathscr{Q}_{\langle 0\rangle}
is exponentiable in
\mathrm{Cat}
, and

(2)

for any
t\colon[2]\to\mathscr{Q}^{\mathrm{act}}
such that
t(2)
lies over
\langle 1\rangle
, the base-change
\mathscr{P}^{\mathrm{act}}\times_{\mathscr{Q}^{\mathrm{act}}}[2]\to[2]
is exponentiable in
\mathrm{Cat}
.

In
Section

8.5
, we will see that the converse also holds.

In contrast to the case of virtual double categories (cf.
Section

9.3
), the following lemma shows that for various flavors of operads, the conditions from
Theorem

5.2
and
Theorem

5.5
are equivalent to
\mathscr{P}^{\mathrm{act}}\to\mathscr{Q}^{\mathrm{act}}
being exponentiable in
\mathrm{Cat}
.

Proposition 5.22
.

Suppose that
\mathscr{O}
is sound and that the elementary slices
\mathscr{O}^{\mathrm{el}}_{x/}
accept an initial functor from a finite set for every
x\in\mathscr{O}
. If
f\colon X\to Y
in
\mathrm{CSeg}(\Omega[\mathscr{O}])
is a map that meets the condition
Item

1
of
Theorem

5.2
, then the comparison map

\mathop{colim}\displaylimits_{[k]\in\Delta^{\mathrm{op}}}\mathrm{Hom}_{/Y}(T_{k}[1]\boxtimes_{[2]}[2;t],X)\to\mathrm{Hom}_{/Y}([1;t_{0}\rightsquigarrow t_{2}],X).

is an equivalence for any forest
\langle 2;t\rangle
of length 2 and any map
[2;t]\to Y
.

Proof.

Using the forest decomposition formula of
Section

4.1
for sound patterns, one shows that the comparison map for a forest
\left<2;t\right>
is computed by the map

\mathop{colim}\displaylimits_{\Delta^{\mathrm{op}}}\lim_{s\in(\mathscr{O}^{\mathrm{el}}_{2})_{t/}}\mathrm{Hom}_{/Y}(T_{k}[1]\boxtimes_{[2]}[2;s],X)\rightarrow\lim_{s\in(\mathscr{O}^{\mathrm{el}}_{2})_{t/}}\mathrm{Hom}_{/Y}([1]\boxtimes_{[2]}[2;s],X).

We may interchange the limit and colimit by our assumption on the elementary slices of
\mathscr{O}
and the fact that
\Delta^{\mathrm{op}}
is sifted. Then we can use the assumption that
f
meets the conditions of
Theorem

5.2
.
∎

The following can now be easily deduced by arguing as in the proof of
Section

5.1
.

Corollary 5.23
.

Suppose that
\mathscr{O}
is sound and that the elementary slices
\mathscr{O}^{\mathrm{el}}_{x/}
accept an initial functor from a finite set for every
x\in\mathscr{O}
. Let
f\colon\mathscr{P}\to\mathscr{Q}
be a map of
\mathscr{O}
-algebrads in the sense of
Section

2.1
. Then the condition of
Theorem

5.5
for
f
is equivalent to the condition that
f^{\mathrm{act}}\colon\mathscr{P}^{\mathrm{act}}\to\mathscr{Q}^{\mathrm{act}}
is exponentiable in
\mathrm{Cat}
.∎

Example 5.24
.

Let
\mathscr{O}=\mathbb{F}_{*}^{\flat}
be the pattern describing operads.
Suppose that
f\colon\mathscr{P}\to\mathscr{Q}
is a map between operads.
It follows from
Section

5.5
and
Theorem

5.5
that
f
is exponentiable in the category
\mathrm{Op}\simeq\mathrm{Algad}(\mathbb{F}_{*}^{\flat})
of operads if the following two equivalent conditions hold:

(1)

for every functor
t\colon[2]\to\mathscr{Q}^{\mathrm{act}}
so that
t(2)
lies over
\left<1\right>
, the base-change
\mathscr{P}^{\mathrm{act}}\times_{\mathscr{Q}^{\mathrm{act}}}[2]\to[2]
is exponentiable in
\mathrm{Cat}
, and,

(2)

the underlying functor
\mathscr{P}^{\mathrm{act}}\to\mathscr{Q}^{\mathrm{act}}
is exponentiable in
\mathrm{Cat}
.

(The equivalence of these conditions was also proved by Hinich
[
21
, Lemma 2.8.2]
.)
In
Section

8.5
, we will conversely see that any exponentiable map of operads satisfies these two conditions.
By
Section

5.5
, it follows that such a map of operads is also exponentiable in the bigger category
\mathrm{Algad}(\mathbb{F}_{*}^{\natural})
of generalized operads

Example 5.25
.

Let
G
be a finite group and consider the pattern
\mathrm{Span}(\mathbb{F}_{G})
from
Section

2.1
describing
G
-operads.
By
[
5
, Example 3.3.26]
, the conditions of
Section

5.5
are satisfied, so we see that a map
f\colon\mathscr{P}\to\mathscr{Q}
is exponentiable in the category
\mathrm{Algad}(\mathrm{Span}(\mathbb{F}_{G}))
of
G
-operads if the following two equivalent conditions hold:

(1)

for every functor
t\colon[2]\to\mathscr{P}^{\mathrm{act}}
so that
t(2)
lies over a
G
-orbit (i.e. a transitive
G
-set), the base-change
\mathscr{P}^{\mathrm{act}}\times_{\mathscr{Q}^{\mathrm{act}}}[2]\to[2]
is exponentiable in
\mathrm{Cat}
, and,

(2)

the underlying functor
\mathscr{P}^{\mathrm{act}}\to\mathscr{Q}^{\mathrm{act}}
is exponentiable in
\mathrm{Cat}
.

In
[
28
, Definition 2.1.7]
,
G
-operads were defined as algebrads for a different algebraic pattern
\underline{\mathbb{F}}_{G,*}
(see also
[
5
, Observation 5.2.12]
).
By
[
5
, Observation 5.2.9]
, the pattern
\underline{\mathbb{F}}_{G,*}
satisfies the conditions of
Section

5.5
, so we see that a map
\mathscr{P}\to\mathscr{Q}
in
\mathrm{Algad}(\underline{\mathbb{F}}_{G,*})
is exponentiable if
\mathscr{P}^{\mathrm{act}}\to\mathscr{Q}^{\mathrm{act}}
is exponentiable in
\mathrm{Cat}
.
This recovers Corollary 3.1.5 of
[
28
]
for
G
-operads.
We will see in
Section

8.5
that the converse also holds.

6.
Underlying graphs of algebrads

Let
f\colon\mathscr{O}\to\mathscr{P}
be a strong Segal map between algebraic patterns. As explained in
Section

4.2
, there is an induced adjunction

f_{!}:\mathrm{Algad}(\mathscr{O})\rightleftarrows\mathrm{Algad}(\mathscr{P}):f^{*}.

The following is an easy observation:

Proposition 6.1
.

The right adjoint functor
f^{*}
preserves the maps in
\mathrm{Algad}(\mathscr{P})
that satisfy condition
Item

1
from
Theorem

5.2
. ∎

Although
f^{*}
might preserve the exponentiable objects that satisfy
Item

1
, it is not true that
f^{*}
commutes with taking
exponential
objects in general. That is, the canonical comparison map
f^{*}[X,Y]\to[f^{*}X,f^{*}Y]
is not invertible in general for
\mathscr{P}
-algebrads
X
and
Y
, where
X
is exponentiable (see
Section

1
for the notation). We will now discuss an important example of a morphism
f
where this
does
hold.

Construction 6.2
.

There is a canonical map of algebraic patterns
\mathscr{O}^{\mathrm{el}}\to\mathscr{O}
. Here,
\mathscr{O}^{\mathrm{el}}
is considered as an algebraic pattern where all morphisms are inert and all objects are elementary. We note that
\mathrm{Algad}(\mathscr{O}^{\mathrm{el}})\simeq\mathrm{Fun}(\mathscr{O}^{\mathrm{el}},\mathrm{Cat})
. Consequently,
Section

4.2
gives rise to an induced adjunction

\iota:\mathrm{Fun}(\mathscr{O}^{\mathrm{el}},\mathrm{Cat})\rightleftarrows\mathrm{Algad}(\mathscr{O}):\Gamma.

If
X
is an algebrad on
\mathscr{O}
, then
\Gamma X
is called the
underlying graph
of
X
.

Definition 6.3
.

A tree
[n;t]
in
\Omega[\mathscr{O}]
is called
unary
if there exists a cartesian morphism
[n;t]\to[0;t_{0}]
; i.e. if
t_{0}\rightsquigarrow\cdots\rightsquigarrow t_{n}
is a sequence of equivalences.

We will establish the following description of
\iota
:

Proposition 6.4
.

The functor
\iota
is fully faithful, and an algebrad
X
is in the essential image of
\iota
if and only if
X([n;t])=\emptyset
for every non-unary tree
[n;t]
.

To this end, we will need some preparations. We will view
\mathscr{O}
as a double category in what follows.

Remark 6.5
.

The final object of
\Delta
is given by
[0]
, hence there is a unique functor
\Delta\times\mathscr{O}_{0}^{\mathrm{op}}\to\Phi[\mathscr{O}]
over
\Delta
that preserves cartesian edges, and that induces an equivalence on the fiber above
[0]
. Its restriction

i\colon\Delta\times\mathscr{O}_{0}^{\mathrm{el},\mathrm{op}}\to\Omega[\mathscr{O}];\quad([n],e)\mapsto[n;\overline{e}]

is precisely the functor that is induced by the map
\mathscr{O}^{\mathrm{el}}_{0}\to\mathscr{O}
between algebraic patterns. By definition, the functor
\iota
is derived from the left Kan extension functor

i_{!}\colon\mathrm{PSh}(\Delta\times\mathscr{O}_{0}^{\mathrm{el},\mathrm{op}})\to\mathrm{PSh}(\Omega[\mathscr{O}]).

We note that
i_{!}
carries
([n],e)
to the tree
[n;\bar{e}]=[n]\boxtimes_{[0]}[0;e]
.

Lemma 6.6
.

The functor
i\colon\Delta\times\mathscr{O}_{0}^{\mathrm{el},\mathrm{op}}\to\Omega[\mathscr{O}]
is fully faithful.

Proof.

It suffices to check that the functor
\Delta\times\mathscr{O}_{0}^{\mathrm{op}}\to\Phi[\mathscr{O}]
is fully faithful. This may be checked fiberwise, so we have to verify that the functor
\mathscr{O}_{0}\to\mathscr{O}_{n}
is fully faithful for every
n
. Using the Segal condition, we may reduce to the case that
n=1
. As a double category,
\mathscr{O}
is in the image of the
\mathrm{Sq}_{L,R}
-construction. Thus the fully faithfulness in level
n=1
ultimately follows from the fact that the diagonal functor
\mathscr{C}\to\mathrm{Fun}([1],\mathscr{C})
is fully faithful for every category
\mathscr{C}
, since
[1]\times[1]\cup_{\{0,1\}\times[1]}\{0,1\}=[1]
.
∎

Lemma 6.7
.

Let
X
be a presheaf on
\Delta\times\mathscr{O}^{\mathrm{el},\mathrm{op}}_{0}
. Then
(i_{!}X)([n;t])=\emptyset
for every non-unary tree
[n;t]
.

Proof.

The commutative square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\emptyset}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega[\mathscr{O}]_{[n;t]/}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta\times\mathscr{O}^{\mathrm{el},\mathrm{op}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega[\mathscr{O}]}

is a pullback square. Namely, if
[n;t]\to[m;\overline{e}]
were a map, then we could consider the composite
[n;t]\to[m;\overline{e}]\to[0;e]
. But then the description of
Section

3.2
would imply that
[n;t]
was unary.
∎

Proof of
Section

6
.

Recall that
\iota
is derived from
i_{!}
, but in fact, we claim that
\iota
is also restricted from
i_{!}
. Since
i_{!}
is fully faithful by
Section

6
, this will then prove that
\iota
is fully faithful. The claim follows by observing that
i_{!}
carries each complete Segal
(\Delta\times\mathscr{O}^{\mathrm{el},\mathrm{op}})
-space
X
to a complete Segal
\Omega[\mathscr{O}]
-space. Namely, it is clear that
i_{!}X
is local with respect to the completeness extensions by the fully faithfulness of
i
.
To show that
i_{!}X
is local with respect to the Segal extensions, we note that
\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{PSh}(\Omega[\mathscr{O}])}(\mathrm{Sp}[n;t],i_{!}X)=\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{PSh}(\Omega[\mathscr{O}])}([n;t],i_{!}X)=\emptyset
if
[n;t]
is non-unary by
Section

6
. If
[n;t]
is unary, then
i_{!}X
is local with respect to its associated spine inclusion by the fully faithfulness of
i_{!}
.

For the second assertion, assume that
X
is an
\mathscr{O}
-algebrad. Then it follows from the above and
Section

6
that
X([n;t])=\emptyset
for all non-unary trees
[n;t]
if
X
is in the image of
\iota
. Conversely, suppose that
X
satisfies
X([n;t])=\emptyset
for every non-unary tree
[n;t]
. Then one readily deduces that the counit
\iota\Gamma X\to X
must be an equivalence.
∎

It follows directly from
Section

6
that the unary trees are the only trees that are in the essential image of
\iota
. Therefore, we may introduce the following extension of the terminology:

Definition 6.8
.

The algebrads in the essential image of
\iota
are called
unary algebrads
.

Corollary 6.9
.

If
X
is a unary algebrad, then
\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{Algad}(\mathscr{O})}(Y,X)=\emptyset
for every non-unary algebrad
Y
.

Proof.

Since
Y
is non-unary,
Section

6
implies that there exists a map
[n;t]\to Y
where
[n;t]
is non-unary. We then obtain a restriction map
\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{Algad}(\mathscr{O})}(Y,X)\to X([n;t])
, so that
Section

6
implies the desired result.
∎

Corollary 6.10
.

If
X
is a unary algebrad, then
X\times Y
is unary as well for every algebrad
Y
.

Proof.

If
[n;t]
is a non-unary tree, then
(X\times Y)([n;t])=X([n;t])\times Y([n;t])=\emptyset
by
Section

6
. Thus the desired conclusion follows from another application of
Section

6
.
∎

Remark 6.11
.

The functor
\iota\colon\mathrm{Fun}(\mathscr{O}^{\mathrm{el}},\mathrm{Cat})\to\mathrm{Algad}(\mathscr{O})
factors as the composite

\mathrm{Fun}(\mathscr{O}^{\mathrm{el}},\mathrm{Cat})\to\mathrm{Algad}(\mathscr{O})_{/\iota(\ast)}\to\mathrm{Algad}(\mathscr{O}),

where
\ast\colon\mathscr{O}^{\mathrm{el}}\to\mathrm{Cat}
denotes the terminal functor. The second functor is the projection. The resulting first functor is fully faithful on account of
Section

6
. Moreover,
Section

6
also implies that it is essentially surjective. Under this identification
\mathrm{Fun}(\mathscr{O}^{\mathrm{el}},\mathrm{Cat})\simeq\mathrm{Algad}(\mathscr{O})_{/\iota(\ast)}
, the adjunction
\iota\dashv\Gamma
is thus induced by the unary algebrad
\iota(\ast)
via push-forward and base-change.

We can now easily show that the underlying graph functor preserves exponential objects, establishing
C
:

Theorem 6.12
.

The graph functor
\Gamma
preserves exponential objects. Precisely, for each exponentiable algebrad
X
, and every algebrad
Y
, the canonical comparison map

\Gamma[X,Y]\to[\Gamma X,\Gamma Y]

is an equivalence in
\mathrm{Fun}(\mathscr{O}^{\mathrm{el}},\mathrm{Cat})
.

Proof.

Suppose that
Y
is an
\mathscr{O}
-algebrad, and that
X
is an exponentiable
\mathscr{O}
-algebrad. The map
\Gamma[X,Y]\to[\Gamma X,\Gamma Y]
represents the map between presheaves

\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{Algad}(\mathscr{O})}(\iota(-)\times X,Y)\to\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{Algad}(\mathscr{O})}(\iota((-)\times\Gamma X),Y)

that comes from the natural comparison map
\gamma_{A}:\iota(A\times\Gamma X)\to\iota A\times X
defined for each algebrad
A
.
Thus it suffices to show that
\gamma_{A}
is an equivalence. This follows from
Section

6
.
∎

Example 6.13
.

We consider the pattern
\mathscr{O}=\mathbb{F}_{*}^{\flat}
whose category of algebrads is given by the category
\mathrm{Op}=\mathrm{Algad}(\mathbb{F}_{*}^{\flat})
of operads. In this case, we obtain an adjunction

\iota:\mathrm{Cat}\rightleftarrows\mathrm{Op}:\Gamma.

The underlying graph of an operad
\mathscr{P}
is precisely the underlying category
\mathscr{P}_{\langle 1\rangle}
of the operad. The operad
\iota(\ast)
can be identified with the
trivial operad
of
[
25
, Example 2.1.1.20]
.
Suppose that
\mathscr{P}
is an exponentiable operad. Then for every other operad
\mathscr{Q}
, the underlying category of the exponential object
[\mathscr{P},\mathscr{Q}]
is given by
\mathrm{Fun}(\mathscr{P}_{\langle 1\rangle},\mathscr{Q}_{\langle 1\rangle}),
cf.
[
25
, Proposition 2.2.6.4]
.

There is also an equivariant analogue of this example that is obtained when considering the pattern
\mathscr{O}=\mathrm{Span}(\mathbb{F}_{G})^{\flat}
of
Section

2.1
for
G
-operads, cf.
[
28
, Proposition 3.1.9]
. In this case, we obtain an adjunction

\iota:\mathrm{Fun}(\mathrm{Orb}_{G}^{\mathrm{op}},\mathrm{Cat})\rightleftarrows\mathrm{Algad}(\mathrm{Span}(\mathbb{F}_{G})^{\flat}):\Gamma,

where
\mathrm{Orb}_{G}\subset\mathbb{F}_{G}
is the full subcategory spanned by the
G
-orbits. The functor
\Gamma
assigns the underlying
G
-category to a
G
-operad.

Example 6.14
.

We consider the pattern
\mathscr{O}=\Delta^{\mathrm{op},\natural}
whose category of algebrads
\mathrm{Algad}(\Delta^{\mathrm{op},\natural})\simeq\mathrm{VirtDblCat}
is the category of virtual double categories (see
Section

2.1
). In this case,

\mathbb{G}\coloneqq\mathscr{O}^{\mathrm{el}}=\{[1]\rightrightarrows[0]\}

is the category of two parallel arrows. Let
X,Y\in\mathrm{Fun}(\mathbb{G},\mathrm{Cat})
. Thus we obtain an adjunction

\iota:\mathrm{Fun}(\mathbb{G},\mathrm{Cat})\rightleftarrows\mathrm{VirtDblCat}:\Gamma.

For this specific example, one may view
Section

6
as a generalization of
[
1
, Proposition 3.3]
, and
Theorem

6.12
as a generalization of
[
1
, Lemma 3.8]
to the
\infty
-categorical setting.

One can verify that the exponential object
Z\coloneqq[X,Y]
in
\mathrm{Fun}(\mathbb{G},\mathrm{Cat})
is characterized by the pullback diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Fun}(X_{1},Y_{1})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z_{0}^{\times 2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Fun}(X_{1},Y_{0})^{\times 2},}

and where
Z_{0}
is defined to be
\mathrm{Fun}(X_{0},Y_{0})
, and the bottom functor in the square is the obvious one, cf.
[
1
, Proposition 3.6]
. If we combine this with the fact that
\Gamma
preserves exponential objects, we obtain a concrete description of the cells of exponential objects of virtual double categories.

7.
Robust algebraic patterns

In
Section

8
, we will show the converse of
Theorem

5.2
(and hence also
Theorem

5.5
) for so-called
robust
algebraic patterns. This preliminary section is dedicated to introducing this class of patterns. We will discuss a range of examples of robust patterns in
Section

7.4
. The reader may safely skip to
Section

7
and consult the results of this section when needed.

7.1.
The
\pi_{0}
functor

We will start with a general construction that will be necessary to state the definition of the robust patterns.

Let
x
be an object of an algebraic pattern
\mathscr{O}
. Then we will write

\pi_{0}(x):=\pi_{0}|\mathscr{O}^{\mathrm{el}}_{x/}|

for the set of connected components of its elementary slice.
Any inert morphism
f:x\rightarrowtail y
in
\mathscr{O}
induces a function

\pi_{0}(y)\to\pi_{0}(x)
by precomposition. Moreover, if
\mathscr{O}
is
sound
in the sense of
[
5
, Definition 3.3.2]
, then any active map
g:x\rightsquigarrow y
induces a morphism
\pi_{0}(x)\to\pi_{0}(y)
. Indeed, following
[
5
]
, we define the category
\mathscr{O}^{\mathrm{el}}(g)
as the pullback

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathscr{O}^{\mathrm{el}}(g)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{Ar}(\mathscr{O}^{\mathrm{int}}_{x/})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathscr{O}^{\mathrm{el}}_{y/}\times\mathscr{O}^{\mathrm{el}}_{x/}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathscr{O}^{\mathrm{int}}_{x/}\times\mathscr{O}^{\mathrm{int}}_{x/}}.}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}

Recall that
\mathscr{O}
is defined to be sound if for any active morphism
g\colon x\rightsquigarrow y
, the projection
\mathscr{O}^{\mathrm{el}}(g)\to\mathscr{O}^{\mathrm{el}}_{x/}
is initial.
This in particular implies that
\pi_{0}|\mathscr{O}^{\mathrm{el}}(g)|\to\pi_{0}|\mathscr{O}^{\mathrm{el}}_{x/}|=\pi_{0}(x)
is an equivalence when
g
is active, hence we obtain the desired map
\pi_{0}(x)\to\pi_{0}(y)
. We will now show that we can extend this assignment to an
oplax
functor
\pi_{0}:\mathscr{O}\to\mathrm{Span}(\mathrm{Set})
where
\mathrm{Span}(\mathrm{Set})
is the
2
-category of spans of sets.

Construction 7.1
.

Let
\mathrm{Ar}^{\mathrm{int}}(\mathscr{O})\subset\mathrm{Ar}(\mathscr{O})
be the full subcategory spanned by inert morphisms. Then
\mathrm{ev}_{0}\colon\mathrm{Ar}^{\mathrm{int}}(\mathscr{O})\to\mathscr{O}
is the cartesian fibration corresponding to the functor
\mathscr{O}^{\mathrm{int}}_{-/}\colon\mathscr{O}^{\mathrm{op}}\to\mathrm{Cat}
.
We write
\mathrm{Ar}^{\mathrm{int}}(\mathscr{O})^{\vee}\to\mathscr{O}^{\mathrm{op}}
for the
cocartesian
fibration corresponding to this functor.
We will consider the full subcategory
\mathrm{Ar}^{\mathrm{int}}(\mathscr{O})^{\vee,\mathrm{el}}
spanned by the inert arrows
x\rightarrowtail e
whose target is an elementary.

Remark 7.2
.

We note that
\mathrm{Ar}^{\mathrm{int}}(\mathscr{O})^{\vee,\mathrm{el}}\to\mathscr{O}^{\mathrm{op}}
is cocartesian when restricted to the inert maps.

Remark 7.3
.

Using the description of the cartesian lifts of
\mathrm{Ar}^{\mathrm{int}}(\mathscr{O})\to\mathscr{O}
, we can compute the fibers of

\mathrm{Fun}([1],\mathrm{Ar}^{\mathrm{int}}(\mathscr{O})^{\vee,\mathrm{el}})\to\mathrm{Fun}([1],\mathscr{O}^{\mathrm{op}})

above a morphism
f:x\to y
as follows. It is given by the category of commutative diagrams in
\mathscr{O}
of shape

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{x^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces e.}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{e^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

This recovers the category
\mathscr{O}^{\mathrm{el}}(f)
defined before if
f
is active. We will use the same notation for this category if
f
is not necessarily active.

Lemma 7.4
.

Let
\mathscr{O}
be a sound pattern. Then the functor
\mathrm{Ar}^{\mathrm{int}}(\mathscr{O})^{\vee,\mathrm{el}}\to\mathscr{O}^{\mathrm{op}}
is exponentiable.

Proof.

Let
f:x\to y
and
g:y\to z
be two maps in
\mathscr{O}
, and
\alpha
be a morphism in
\mathrm{Ar}^{\mathrm{int}}(\mathscr{O})^{\vee,\mathrm{el}}
over
g\circ f
. By
Section

7.1
, the morphism
\alpha
corresponds to a diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces z}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{x^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces e}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces e^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j}

with
e
and
e^{\prime}
elementary. To apply the Conduché criterion (see
[
25
, Proposition B.3.2]
or
[
4
, Lemma 2.2.8]
), we have to show that the category of diagrams in
\mathscr{O}
of shape

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces z}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{x^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{y^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces e}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{x^{\prime\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{e^{\prime\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces e^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j}

is weakly contractible. However, this category is equivalent to the fiber of
\mathscr{O}^{\mathrm{el}}(f^{\prime})\to\mathscr{O}^{\mathrm{el}}_{e^{\prime}/}
at
j
and is hence weakly contractible by
[
5
, Lemma 3.3.9]
.
∎

Construction 7.5
.

Let
\mathrm{Prof}
be the (flagged) 2-category of categories and profunctors/correspondences/two-sided fibrations.
2
2

2

The underlying (flagged) 1-category is the
opposite
of the category
\mathrm{Corr}
considered by Ayala–Francis
[
4
]
.
A
two-sided fibration
from a category
\mathscr{C}
to a category
\mathscr{D}
is a span
(p,q):E\to\mathscr{C}\times\mathscr{D}
so that its fibers are spaces,
p
is cocartesian with cocartesian morphisms lying above equivalences in
\mathscr{C}
, and
q
is cartesian with cartesian morphisms lying above equivalences in
\mathscr{D}
. These were called
bifibrations
in
[
24
, §2.4.7]
.
We will now construct a unital oplax functor
\mathrm{Prof}\to\mathrm{Span}(\mathrm{Set})
that carries a two-sided fibration
E\to\mathscr{C}\times\mathscr{D}
to the span
\pi_{0}|E|\to\pi_{0}|\mathscr{C}|\times\pi_{0}|\mathscr{D}|
.

The (flagged) 2-category
\mathrm{Prof}
arises as the horizontal fragment (see e.g.
[
32
, Section 3.3]
) of the double category
\mathbb{C}\mathrm{at}
of categories. Similarly, the 2-category
\mathrm{Span}(\mathrm{Set})
is the horizontal fragment of the double category
\mathbb{S}\mathrm{pan}(\mathrm{Set})
of spans.
There is a canonical functor
\mathbb{S}\mathrm{pan}(\mathscr{S})\to\mathbb{C}\mathrm{at}
between double categories that selects the full subdouble category spanned by the spaces.
3
3

3

Alternatively, the canonical inclusion from
\mathbb{S}\mathrm{pan}(\mathscr{S})
to
\mathbb{C}\mathrm{at}
can be obtained by viewing
\mathbb{C}\mathrm{at}
as a subdouble category of the Morita construction applied to
\mathbb{S}\mathrm{pan}(\mathscr{S})
; see
[
8
, Proposition 7.1]
.
Consider the composite inclusion

\mathbb{S}\mathrm{pan}(\mathrm{Set})\to\mathbb{S}\mathrm{pan}(\mathscr{S})\to\mathbb{C}\mathrm{at}.

This functor recovers the inclusion
\mathrm{Set}\to\mathrm{Cat}
