on vertical categories, and interprets each span of sets as a two-sided fibration. We claim that this functor has an oplax left adjoint; i.e. that it has a left adjoint in the ambient 2-category
\mathrm{Fun}^{\mathrm{oplax/int-strong}}(\Delta^{\mathrm{op}},\mathrm{Cat})
of functors and oplax natural transformations that are strong when restricted to inerts. By e.g. the dual of
[
32
, Corollary 6.9]
and the Segal condition, it suffices to check that the horizontal functors in the square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Fun}(\bullet\leftarrow\bullet\rightarrow\bullet,\mathrm{Set})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{TsFib}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Set}\times\mathrm{Set}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Cat}\times\mathrm{Cat}}

admit left adjoints, and that the associated mate is an equivalence. This is readily verified. Here
\mathrm{TsFib}\subset\mathrm{Fun}(\bullet\leftarrow\bullet\rightarrow\bullet,\mathrm{Cat})
is the full subcategory spanned by the two-sided fibrations. The resulting left adjoint functor
\pi_{0}|-|:\mathbb{C}\mathrm{at}\to\mathbb{S}\mathrm{pan}(\mathrm{Set})
is also unital. To see this, we have to verify that the mate of the square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Set}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Cat}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Fun}(\bullet\leftarrow\bullet\rightarrow\bullet,\mathrm{Set})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{TsFib}}

is also invertible. But this follows from the fact that
|\mathrm{Fun}([1],\mathscr{C})|\simeq|\mathscr{C}|
. We can now take horizontal fragments to obtain the desired oplax functor
\mathrm{Prof}\to\mathrm{Span}(\mathrm{Set})
.

Construction 7.6
.

By
[
4
, Theorem 0.8]
or
[
8
, Corollary 6.2]
, and
Section

7.1
, the functor

\mathrm{Ar}^{\mathrm{int}}(\mathscr{O})^{\vee,\mathrm{el}}\to\mathscr{O}^{\mathrm{op}}
is classified by a functor
E:\mathscr{O}\to\mathrm{Prof}
to the (flagged) 2-category of categories and profunctors.
Composing with the (unital) oplax functor

\mathrm{Prof}\to\mathrm{Span}(\mathrm{Set})
of
Section

7.1
, we obtain a unital oplax functor
\pi_{0}:\mathscr{O}\to\mathrm{Span}(\mathrm{Set})
that sends a map
f\colon x\to y
to the span

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\pi_{0}|\mathscr{O}^{\mathrm{el}}(f)|}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\pi_{0}(x)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\pi_{0}(y)},}

see
Section

7.1
.
If
f
is inert, then the right leg is an equivalence since
\mathrm{Ar}^{\mathrm{int}}(\mathscr{O})^{\vee,\mathrm{el}}\to\mathscr{O}^{\mathrm{op}}
is a cocartesian fibration over the inert morphisms, and if
f
is active then the left leg is an equivalence by soundness.
We conclude that
\pi_{0}
is of the desired form.

Remark 7.7
.

Let
f\colon x\to y
and
g\colon y\to z
be morphisms in
\mathscr{O}
.
Since
E\colon\mathscr{O}\to\mathrm{Prof}
is a strong functor, as opposed to (op)lax, the composition formula for two-sided fibrations (see
[
4
]
) implies that the canonical map

|\mathscr{O}^{\mathrm{el}}(f)\times_{\mathscr{O}^{\mathrm{el}}_{y/}}\mathscr{O}^{\mathrm{el}}(g)|\to|\mathscr{O}^{\mathrm{el}}(gf)|

is an equivalence.
In particular, we obtain a diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\pi_{0}|\mathscr{O}^{\mathrm{el}}(gf)|}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\pi_{0}|\mathscr{O}^{\mathrm{el}}(f)|}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\pi_{0}|\mathscr{O}^{\mathrm{el}}(g)|}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\pi_{0}(x)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\pi_{0}(y)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\pi_{0}(z)}}

where the square is obtained by applying
\pi_{0}|-|
to the pullback defining
\mathscr{O}^{\mathrm{el}}(f)\times_{\mathscr{O}^{\mathrm{el}}_{y}/}\mathscr{O}^{\mathrm{el}}(g)

and then identifying
\pi_{0}|\mathscr{O}^{\mathrm{el}}(f)\times_{\mathscr{O}^{\mathrm{el}}_{y/}}\mathscr{O}^{\mathrm{el}}(g)|
with
\mathscr{O}^{\mathrm{el}}(gf)
as above.
The gap map of this square precisely defines the oplax comparison 2-morphism
\pi_{0}(gf)\Rightarrow\pi_{0}(g)\circ\pi_{0}(f)
of
\pi_{0}\colon\mathscr{O}\to\mathrm{Span}(\mathrm{Set})
.

Lemma 7.8
.

Suppose that any square of the form

(7)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces w}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces z}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces y}

is sent to a cartesian square of sets by the functor
\pi_{0}
.
Then the unital oplax functor
\pi_{0}\colon\mathscr{O}\to\mathrm{Span}(\mathrm{Set})
sending
x
to
\pi_{0}(x)
is a strong functor.

Proof.

By
Section

7.1
, it suffices to show that the square

(8)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\pi_{0}|\mathscr{O}^{\mathrm{el}}(f)\times_{\mathscr{O}^{\mathrm{el}}_{y/}}\mathscr{O}^{\mathrm{el}}(g)|}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\pi_{0}|\mathscr{O}^{\mathrm{el}}(g)|}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\pi_{0}|\mathscr{O}^{\mathrm{el}}(f)|}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\pi_{0}|\mathscr{O}^{\mathrm{el}}_{y/}|}}

is cartesian for any
f\colon x\to y
and
g\colon y\to z
in
\mathscr{O}
.
In light of the inert-active factorization system on
\mathscr{O}
, we may assume without loss of generality that
f
is active or inert, and similarly for
g
.
If
f
is inert, then
\mathscr{O}^{\mathrm{el}}(f)\to\mathscr{O}^{\mathrm{el}}_{y/}
admits a fully faithful right adjoint by
[
4
, Lemma 3.2.6]
, hence so does
\mathscr{O}^{\mathrm{el}}(g)\times_{\mathscr{O}^{\mathrm{el}}_{y/}}\mathscr{O}^{\mathrm{el}}(f)\to\mathscr{O}^{\mathrm{el}}(g)
(see e.g.
[
18
, Proposition 2.6]
).
This means that the horizontal maps in the square
Equation

8
are bijections, hence it is cartesian.
If instead
g
is active, then
\mathscr{O}^{\mathrm{el}}(g)\to\mathscr{O}^{\mathrm{el}}_{/y}
is initial.
Since
\mathscr{O}^{\mathrm{el}}(f)\to\mathscr{O}^{\mathrm{el}}_{/y}
is a cartesian fibration, the map
\mathscr{O}^{\mathrm{el}}(g)\times_{\mathscr{O}^{\mathrm{el}}_{y/}}\mathscr{O}^{\mathrm{el}}(f)\to\mathscr{O}^{\mathrm{el}}(f)
is also initial.
We therefore see that the vertical maps in the square
Equation

8
are bijections, hence it is cartesian.
We are therefore left with the case that
f
is active and
g
is inert.
In this case, by factoring the composite
gf
into an inert
x\rightarrowtail w
followed by an active
w\rightsquigarrow z
and considering the previous cases, it follows that the square
Equation

8
agrees with the square obtained by applying
\pi_{0}
to
Equation

7
.
∎

Remark 7.9
.

Let
f:\mathscr{O}\to\mathscr{P}
be a map between algebraic patterns that induces a bijection
\pi_{0}(x)\to\pi_{0}f(x)
for every
x\in\mathscr{O}
. Then we claim that we have a commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{O}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Span}(\mathrm{Set})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{P}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{0}^{\mathscr{O}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{0}^{\mathscr{P}}}

of oplax functors. Namely, we have a commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Ar}^{\mathrm{int}}(\mathscr{O})^{\vee,\mathrm{el}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Ar}^{\mathrm{int}}(\mathscr{P})^{\vee,\mathrm{el}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{O}^{\mathrm{op}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{P}^{\mathrm{op}}.}

The induced map
\mathrm{Ar}^{\mathrm{int}}(\mathscr{O})^{\vee,\mathrm{el}}\to\mathrm{Ar}^{\mathrm{int}}(\mathscr{P})^{\vee,\mathrm{el}}\times_{\mathscr{P}^{\mathrm{op}}}\mathscr{O}^{\mathrm{op}}
is classified by a map
[1]_{v}\times\mathscr{O}_{h}\to\mathbb{C}\mathrm{at}
; see
[
33
, Proposition 3.8.5]
for a proof following Ayala–Francis
[
4
]
, or
[
8
, Theorem 8.1]
. Thus we obtain an oplax functor
[1]_{v}\times\mathscr{O}_{h}\to\mathbb{S}\mathrm{pan}(\mathrm{Set})
from
\pi_{0}^{\mathscr{O}}
to
\pi_{0}^{\mathscr{P}}\circ f
. By assumption, this factors through
[1]_{v}\times\mathscr{O}_{h}\to\mathscr{O}_{h}
.

7.2.
Robust patterns

We introduce the following terminology.

Definition 7.10
.

An algebraic pattern
\mathscr{O}
is called
robust
if the following conditions hold:

(1)

\mathscr{O}
is sound in the sense of
[
5
, Definition 3.3.4]
.

(2)

\mathscr{O}
is saturated in the sense of
[
12
, Definition 14.15]
, i.e. for every
x\in\mathscr{O}
, the presheaf
\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathscr{O}}(x,-)
is a Segal
\mathscr{O}
-space (see
Section

2.1
).

(3)

A map
x\to y
in
\mathscr{O}
is inert if and only if for any inert
y\rightarrowtail e
with
e
elementary, the composite
x\to y\rightarrowtail e
is inert.

(4)

The (unital) oplax functor
\pi_{0}:\mathscr{O}\to\mathrm{Span}(\mathrm{Set})
satisfies the following conditions:

(a)

For any
x
,
\pi_{0}(x)
is a finite set, i.e. each elementary slice
\mathscr{O}^{\mathrm{el}}_{x/}
has a finite number of connected components.

(b)

The functor
\pi_{0}
is strong, or equivalently, it sends any square of shape
Equation

7
to a cartesian square of sets.

(c)

If
x\in\mathscr{O}
lies over
\underline{n}
, there exist
\pi_{0}
-cocartesian inert maps
x\rightarrowtail x^{i}
over the inerts
\underline{n}\hookleftarrow\{i\}\xrightarrow{=}\{i\}

that induce an equivalence

\mathscr{O}^{\mathrm{act}}_{/x}\to\prodop\displaylimits_{i=1}^{n}\mathscr{O}^{\mathrm{act}}_{/x^{i}}.

Definition 7.11
.

An algebraic pattern
\mathscr{O}
is called

atomically robust
if it satisfies conditions (1)-(3) of
Section

7.2
and
\pi_{0}(x)\simeq\underline{1}
for every
x\in\mathscr{O}
, i.e. if each elementary slice
\mathscr{O}^{\mathrm{el}}_{x/}
is connected.

Remark 7.12
.

Every atomically robust pattern is robust, as the functor
\pi_{0}:\mathscr{O}\to\mathrm{Span}(\mathrm{Set})
factors through
\underline{1}:\ast\to\mathrm{Span}(\mathbb{F})
and then automatically meets condition
Item

4
of
Section

7.2
.

A range of examples of robust patterns will be showcased in
Section

7.4
.
We now state some results about robust patterns that will be useful later.

Notation 7.13
.

Given
t\in\mathscr{O}_{n}
, we will write
\pi_{0}(t)
for the set of connected components of
(\mathscr{O}^{\mathrm{el}}_{n})_{t/}
. Note that
\pi_{0}(t)\simeq\pi_{0}(t_{n})
by
Section

2.2
.

We recall the algebraic pattern structure
\mathrm{Span}(\mathbb{F})^{\flat}
on
\mathrm{Span}(\mathbb{F})
from
Section

2.1
where the backward maps are the inert morphisms, the forward maps are the active morphisms and the elementaries are given by
\{\underline{1}\}
. One readily reads off from the construction that the functor
\pi_{0}:\mathscr{O}\to\mathrm{Span}(\mathbb{F})
preserves inerts, actives and elementaries. Consequently, it may be viewed as a map of patterns
\mathscr{O}\to\mathrm{Span}(\mathbb{F})^{\flat}
.
In turn, this gives rise to a functor between factorization double categories via
Section

2.2
. Thus we have functors
\mathscr{O}_{n}\to\mathrm{Span}(\mathbb{F})_{n}
between the degree
n
parts of these double categories.

Proposition 7.14
.

Let
\mathscr{O}
be a robust pattern.
The functor
\mathscr{O}_{n}\to\mathrm{Span}(\mathbb{F})_{n}
induced by
\pi_{0}
admits cocartesian lifts of maps with elementary codomains.

Proof.

We first handle the case that
n=0
. Let
x\in\mathscr{O}
, and suppose that we have a map
i:\pi_{0}(x)\to\underline{1}
in
\mathrm{Span}(\mathbb{F})_{0}=\mathbb{F}^{\mathrm{op}}
. Let
x\rightarrowtail x^{i}
be the associated
\pi_{0}
-cocartesian lift. We may then consider the commutative square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathscr{O}_{0}}(x^{i},y)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathscr{O}_{0}}(x,y)\times_{\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathbb{F}^{\mathrm{op}}}(\pi_{0}(x),\pi_{0}(y))}\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathbb{F}^{\mathrm{op}}}(\underline{1},\pi_{0}(y))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathscr{O}}(x^{i},y)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathscr{O}}(x,y)\times_{\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{Span}(\mathbb{F})}(\pi_{0}(x),\pi_{0}(y))}\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{Span}(\mathbb{F})}(\underline{1},\pi_{0}(y))}

for each
y\in\mathscr{O}
.
The bottom arrow is an equivalence by assumption, and the vertical arrows are monomorphisms. The top arrow is also an equivalence by cancellation of inert morphisms.

For general
n
, suppose that
t\in\mathscr{O}_{n}
and that we have a map
\pi_{0}(t)\to X
in
\mathrm{Span}(\mathbb{F})_{n}
with
X_{n}=\underline{1}
. We have an equivalence
(\mathscr{O}_{n})_{t/}\simeq(\mathscr{O}_{0})_{t_{n}/}
by
Section

2.2
. Let
t\to t^{i}
be the map so that
t_{n}\to t_{n}^{i}
is the
\pi_{0}
-cocartesian lift of
\pi_{0}(t_{n})\to X_{n}=\underline{1}
. It then follows from the above
that the map
(\mathscr{O}_{n})_{t^{i}/}\to(\mathscr{O}_{n})_{t/}\times_{(\mathrm{Span}(\mathbb{F})_{n})_{\pi_{0}(t)/}}(\mathrm{Span}(\mathbb{F})_{n})_{X/}
is an equivalence.
∎

Proposition 7.15
.

Let
\mathscr{O}
be a robust pattern. If
x\in\mathscr{O}
lies over
\underline{n}
, then the
\pi_{0}
-cocartesian inert maps
x\rightarrowtail x^{i}
over
\underline{n}\hookleftarrow\{i\}\xrightarrow{=}\{i\}
induce an equivalence

\coprodop\displaylimits_{i=1}^{n}\mathscr{O}^{\mathrm{el}}_{x^{i}/}\to\mathscr{O}^{\mathrm{el}}_{x/}.

Proof.

We will proceed fiberwise. For
y\in\mathscr{O}^{\mathrm{el}}
, the canonical square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\coprodop\displaylimits_{i=1}^{n}\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathscr{O}_{0}}(x^{i},y)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathscr{O}_{0}}(x,y)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\coprodop\displaylimits_{i=1}^{n}\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathbb{F}^{\mathrm{op}}}(\underline{1},\pi_{0}(y))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathbb{F}^{\mathrm{op}}}(\underline{n},\pi_{0}(y))}

is cartesian by
Section

7.2
. The bottom arrow is an equivalence as
\pi_{0}(y)\simeq\underline{1}
, hence the desired conclusion follows.
∎

Remark 7.16
.

Let
\mathscr{O}
be a robust pattern and
\left<n;t\right>
be a forest. Note that the canonical map

\textstyle\coprodop\displaylimits_{i\in\pi_{0}(t)}(\mathscr{O}_{n}^{\mathrm{el}})_{t^{i}/}\to(\mathscr{O}_{n}^{\mathrm{el}})_{t/}
is an equivalence by
Section

2.2
and
Section

7.2
,
where
t\to t^{i}
is the cocartesian lift of the map
\pi_{0}(t_{n})\hookleftarrow\{i\}\xrightarrow{=}\{i\}
.
We may then use the decomposition formula of
Section

4.1
to conclude that the canonical map

\coprodop\displaylimits_{i\in\pi_{0}(t)}X\boxtimes_{[n]}[n;t^{i}]\to X\boxtimes_{[n]}[n;t]

is an equivalence in
\mathrm{PSh}(\Omega[\mathscr{O}])
for every simplicial space
X
over
[n]
.

Proposition 7.17
.

Let
\mathscr{O}
be a robust pattern.
Then the tuple
(\mathscr{O}_{n},\mathscr{O}_{n}^{\mathrm{el}})
is saturated; that is, for any
t
and
s
in
\mathscr{O}_{n}
, the map

\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathscr{O}_{n}}(s,t)\to\operatorname{lim}_{u\in(\mathscr{O}^{\mathrm{el}}_{n})_{t/}}\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathscr{O}_{n}}(s,u)

is an equivalence.

Proof.

For
n=0
, this follows directly from assumptions (2) and (3). Suppose that
n=1
. Since
\mathscr{O}_{1}
is a subcategory of
\mathrm{Ar}(\mathscr{O})
, the map of the proposition is a map between pullbacks:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\lim\limits_{(t\rightarrowtail u)\in(\mathscr{O}^{\mathrm{el}}_{1})_{t/}}\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathscr{O}_{1}}(s,u)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\lim\limits_{(t\rightarrowtail u)\in(\mathscr{O}^{\mathrm{el}}_{1})_{t/}}\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathscr{O}_{0}}(s_{0},u_{0})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathscr{O}_{1}}(s,t)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathscr{O}_{0}}(s_{0},t_{0})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\lim\limits_{(t\rightarrowtail u)\in(\mathscr{O}^{\mathrm{el}}_{1})_{t/}}\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathscr{O}_{0}}(s_{1},u_{1})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\lim\limits_{(t\rightarrowtail u)\in(\mathscr{O}^{\mathrm{el}}_{1})_{t/}}\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathscr{O}}(s_{0},u_{1})}.}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathscr{O}_{0}}(s_{1},t_{1})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathscr{O}}(s_{0},t_{1})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g_{3}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g_{2}}

We see that
g_{1}
is an equivalence since
(\mathscr{O}_{0},\mathscr{O}_{0}^{\mathrm{el}})
is saturated and
(\mathscr{O}^{\mathrm{el}}_{1})_{t/}\simeq\mathscr{O}^{\mathrm{el}}_{t_{1}/}
, while
g_{2}
is an equivalence since
\mathscr{O}
is saturated.
To see that
g_{3}
is an equivalence, note that since
\mathscr{O}_{0}
is saturated, we can rewrite it as the map

\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathscr{O}_{0}}(s_{0},t_{0})\to\lim_{u\in(\mathscr{O}^{\mathrm{el}}_{1})_{t/}}\operatorname{lim}_{e\in(\mathscr{O}^{\mathrm{el}})_{u_{0}/}}\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathscr{O}_{0}}(s_{0},e).

By
[
5
, Observation 3.3.6]
, the soundness of
\mathscr{O}
and the saturatedness of
\mathscr{O}_{0}
, this map is an equivalence.

To handle the general case, we can use that
\mathscr{O}_{\bullet}
is a double category and reason as in
Section

3.3
.
∎

7.3.
Verifying the robustness condition in practice

We will now describe a few methods for checking whether a pattern
\mathscr{O}
is robust.

Proposition 7.18
.

Suppose that
\mathscr{O}
is a soundly extendable pattern. Then conditions (2) and (3) of
Section

7.2
can be replaced by the single condition:

(2&3’)

(\mathscr{O}^{\mathrm{int}},\mathscr{O}^{\mathrm{el}})
is saturated.

Moreover, condition (4c) can be replaced by:

(4c’)

If
x\in\mathscr{O}
lies over
\underline{n}
, then there exist cocartesian inert maps
x\rightarrowtail x^{i}
over the inerts
\underline{n}\hookleftarrow\{i\}\xrightarrow{=}\{i\}
for
1\leq i\leq n
.

Proof.

For the first assertion, we need to show that
\mathscr{O}
is saturated if and only if
\mathscr{O}^{\mathrm{int}}
is.
Since
\mathscr{O}
is extendable, left Kan extension along
\mathscr{O}^{\mathrm{int}}\hookrightarrow\mathscr{O}
preserves Segal objects by
[
12
, Proposition 8.8]
.
Applying this to representables, we see that
\mathscr{O}
is saturated if
\mathscr{O}^{\mathrm{int}}
is.
For the converse, note that by
[
5
, Remark 4.1.4]
,
\mathscr{O}_{x/}^{\mathrm{int}}\to\mathscr{O}
is a fibrous pattern if the representable
\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathscr{O}}(x,-)
satisfies the Segal condition.
Being a fibrous pattern in particular implies the Segal condition (cf.
[
5
, Remark 4.1.8]
), so since
\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathscr{O}^{\mathrm{int}}}(x,-)
is the straightening of
\mathscr{O}_{x/}^{\mathrm{int}}\to\mathscr{O}
, we conclude that it satisfies the Segal condition.

For the second assertion, suppose that
x\in\mathscr{O}
.
We note that there is a commutative square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{O}^{\mathrm{act}}_{/x}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lim_{e\in\mathscr{O}^{\mathrm{el}}_{x/}}\mathscr{O}^{\mathrm{act}}_{/e}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\prodop\displaylimits_{i\in\pi_{0}(x)}\mathscr{O}^{\mathrm{act}}_{/x^{i}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\prodop\displaylimits_{i\in\pi_{0}(x)}\lim_{e^{\prime}\in\mathscr{O}^{\mathrm{el}}_{x^{i}/}}\mathscr{O}^{\mathrm{act}}_{/e^{\prime}},}

so that the right vertical functor is an equivalence.
As
\mathscr{O}
is extendable, the top and bottom functors are equivalences. So the left vertical functor is an equivalence as well.
∎

Corollary 7.19
.

Suppose that
\mathscr{O}
is a soundly extendable pattern. Then
\mathscr{O}
is atomically robust if and only if
(\mathscr{O}^{\mathrm{int}},\mathscr{O}^{\mathrm{el}})
is saturated and its elementary slices are connected. ∎

Under an extra assumption, atomically robust patterns are stable under products:

Proposition 7.20
.

Let
\mathscr{O}
and
\mathscr{P}
be robust algebraic patterns with weakly contractible elementary slices. Then
\mathscr{O}\times\mathscr{P}
is atomically robust.

Proof.

Observe that
(\mathscr{O}\times\mathscr{P})^{\mathrm{el}}_{(x,y)/}\simeq\mathscr{O}^{\mathrm{el}}_{x/}\times\mathscr{P}^{\mathrm{el}}_{y/}
is weakly contractible for any
(x,y)\in\mathscr{O}\times\mathscr{P}
and that
\mathscr{O}\times\mathscr{P}
is sound by
[
5
, Lemma 3.3.13]
.
To see that
\mathscr{O}\times\mathscr{P}
and
(\mathscr{O}\times\mathscr{P})^{\mathrm{int}}
are saturated, observe that

\lim_{(e,e^{\prime})\in(\mathscr{O}\times\mathscr{P})^{\mathrm{el}}_{(x,y)/}}\mathrm{Hom}((z,w),(e,e^{\prime}))\simeq\lim_{e\in\mathscr{O}^{\mathrm{el}}_{x/}}\mathrm{Hom}(z,e)\times\lim_{e^{\prime}\in\mathscr{P}^{\mathrm{el}}_{y/}}\mathrm{Hom}(w,e^{\prime})

since
\mathscr{O}^{\mathrm{el}}_{x/}
and
\mathscr{P}^{\mathrm{el}}_{y/}
are weakly contractible.
∎

Finally, when
\mathscr{O}
is robust, any
\mathscr{O}
-algebrad is robust as well.

Proposition 7.21
.

Let
\mathscr{O}
be a robust algebraic pattern. Then any algebrad
p\colon\mathscr{P}\to\mathscr{O}
is also robust, when given the pattern structure from
Section

2.1
.

Proof.

We first note that
p
induces an equivalence on elementary slices since
\mathscr{P}^{\mathrm{int}}\to\mathscr{O}^{\mathrm{int}}
is a left fibration, and thus
\mathscr{P}^{\mathrm{int}}_{x/}\simeq\mathscr{O}^{\mathrm{int}}_{p(x)/}
.
By
[
5
, Lemma 4.1.15]
,
\mathscr{P}
is again sound.
To see that
\mathscr{P}
is saturated, we note that the saturatedness of
\mathscr{O}
implies that the bottom map in the pullback square
Item

3
of
Section

2.1
is an equivalence.
Since
\mathscr{P}^{\mathrm{el}}_{y/}\simeq\mathscr{O}^{\mathrm{el}}_{p(y)/}
, this implies saturatedness of
\mathscr{P}
.
Finally, saturatedness of
\mathscr{P}^{\mathrm{int}}
follows by the same argument since
\mathscr{P}^{\mathrm{int}}\to\mathscr{O}^{\mathrm{int}}
is an algebrad.
This shows that (1)-(3) hold for
\mathscr{P}
as well.

To verify condition (4), we note that conditions (4a) and (4b) directly follow from
Section

7.1
(note that the remark simplifies in this case, as the displayed square is already a pullback square).
Suppose that
x\in\mathscr{P}
. For every
i\in\pi_{0}(x)=\pi_{0}p(x)
, we take a
p
-cocartesian lift
x\to x^{i}
of
p(x)\rightarrowtail p(x)^{i}
. By
[
5
, Proposition 4.1.7]
, we have a pullback square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{P}\times_{\mathscr{O}}\mathscr{O}^{\mathrm{act}}_{/p(x)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\prodop\displaylimits_{i\in\pi_{0}(x)}\mathscr{P}\times_{\mathscr{O}}\mathscr{O}^{\mathrm{act}}_{/p(x)^{i}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{O}^{\mathrm{act}}_{/p(x)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\prodop\displaylimits_{i\in\pi_{0}(x)}\mathscr{O}^{\mathrm{act}}_{/p(x)^{i}},}

where the top functor is described in
[
5
, Observation 4.1.1]
.
The bottom functor is an equivalence by assumption, so that the top functor is an equivalence as well. Similarly as in the proof of
[
5
, Lemma 4.1.15]
, the top functor recovers the functor of (4c) when passing to the slices over
(x,p(x)=p(x))
and the
(x^{i},p(x)^{i}=p(x)^{i})
’s.
∎

7.4.
Examples of robust patterns

We now discuss some examples and non-examples of robust patterns.

Example 7.22
.

Let
\mathbb{F}_{*}^{\flat}
be the pattern from
Section

2.1
whose algebrads are operads.
Note that
\mathbb{F}_{*}^{\flat,\mathrm{int}}=(\mathbb{F}_{\mathrm{inj}})^{\mathrm{op}}
, the opposite of the category of finite sets and injections.
The two-element set
\underline{2}
is
not
the coproduct
\underline{1}\sqcup\underline{1}
in
\mathbb{F}_{\mathrm{inj}}
, since the fold map
\underline{2}\to\underline{1}
is not injective.
This means that
\langle 2\rangle
is not the product of
\langle 1\rangle
with itself in
\mathbb{F}_{*}^{\flat,\mathrm{int}}
, so
\mathbb{F}_{*}^{\flat,\mathrm{int}}
is not saturated and hence
\mathbb{F}_{*}^{\flat}
is not robust.
One similarly sees that the algebraic pattern
\Delta^{\mathrm{op},\flat}
whose algebrads are non-symmetric operads is not robust.

Example 7.23
.

Let
G
be a finite group. Then the algebraic pattern
\mathrm{Span}(\mathbb{F}_{G})^{\flat}
is robust. It is soundly extendable by
[
5
, Example 3.3.26]
, and we see that
\mathrm{Span}(\mathbb{F}_{G})^{\mathrm{int}}=\mathbb{F}_{G}^{\mathrm{op}}
is saturated since any object in
\mathbb{F}_{G}
is the coproduct of its orbits, i.e. its subsets on which
G
acts transitively. Thus condition (2&3’) of
Section

7.3
is met. It remains to check condition (4a), (4b) of
Section

7.2
and condition (4c’) of
Section

7.3
.

If
X
is a finite
G
-set, then we can consider the (finite) set
O(X)
of orbits of
X
. This defines a functor
O:\mathbb{F}_{G}\to\mathbb{F}
. One readily verifies that this commutes with pullbacks and that
O(G/H)\simeq\underline{1}
for each subgroup
H\leq G
. Hence, we get a functor
\mathrm{Span}(\mathbb{F}_{G})^{\flat}\to\mathrm{Span}(\mathbb{F})^{\flat}
of patterns. By
Section

7.1
, this is precisely the functor
\pi_{0}
. Thus conditions (4a) and (4b) of
Section

7.2
are met. Suppose that
X
is a finite
G
-set. Then we can consider its decomposition
X=\coprodop\displaylimits_{i=1}^{n}X^{i}
into orbits. Now,
\pi_{0}(X)\simeq\underline{n}
and one readily checks that the inert maps
X\to X^{i}
in
\mathrm{Span}(\mathbb{F}_{G})
are the desired lifts of (4c’).

Example 7.24
.

Consider the algebraic pattern
\Delta^{\mathrm{op},\natural}
for virtual double categories from
Section

2.1
.
In contrast to
Section

7.4
, this algebraic pattern
is
atomically robust: it is soundly extendable by
[
5
, Example 3.3.18]
, and it is saturated since the representables
[n]\in\mathrm{Fun}(\Delta^{\mathrm{op}},\mathscr{S})
are Segal spaces.
The elementary slice
(\Delta^{\mathrm{op},\natural})^{\mathrm{el}}_{[n]/}
is an iterated span

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{n-1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces n}

hence weakly contractible.

Warning 7.25
.

One can also endow
\mathbb{F}_{*}
with the pattern structure
\mathbb{F}_{*}^{\natural}
where the elementaries are
\langle 0\rangle
and
\langle 1\rangle
.
The algebrads for this pattern are
generalized operads
in the sense of
[
25
, Definition 2.3.2.1]
.
In light of the previous example, one might be inclined to believe that this algebraic pattern is atomically robust.
However, this is
not
the case, for the same reason as
Section

7.4
.

Example 7.26
.

While
\mathbb{F}_{*}^{\natural}
is not robust, we can replace it with an (atomically) robust pattern as follows.
Namely, we will see in
Section

8.5
that the inclusion
\mathbb{F}_{*}\hookrightarrow\mathrm{Span}(\mathbb{F})
induces an equivalence

\mathrm{Algad}(\mathbb{F}^{\natural}_{*})\simeq\mathrm{Algad}(\mathrm{Span}(\mathbb{F})^{\natural}).

The pattern
\mathrm{Span}(\mathbb{F})^{\natural}
is atomically robust: its elementary slices are clearly contractible, it is soundly extendable by
[
5
, Proposition 3.3.23]
and
\mathrm{Span}(\mathbb{F})^{\mathrm{int}}\simeq\mathbb{F}^{\mathrm{op}}
is easily seen to be saturated.

Example 7.27
.

The category
\Theta_{n}
has a pattern structure whose elementary objects are the free
k
-cells
C_{k}
with
k\leq n
(see e.g.
[
5
, Example 3.2.2]
).
The Segal spaces for this pattern are (flagged)
n
-categories and its algebrads can be thought of as virtual versions of
(1,n)
-double categories.
This pattern is saturated by
[
12
, Examples 14.21]
, while it is soundly extendable by
[
5
, Example 3.3.18]
.
Finally, the elementary slices of
\Theta_{n}
are weakly contractible by
[
20
, Corollary 2.9]
.
By
Section

7.3
, this algebraic pattern structure on
\Theta_{n}
is atomically robust.

Example 7.28
.

The
n
-fold product
\Delta^{\times n,\mathrm{op},\natural}
of
\Delta^{\mathrm{op},\natural}
with itself can be used to describe (virtual versions of)
n
-uple categories.
By
Section

7.4
and
Section

7.3
, the algebraic pattern
\Delta^{\times n,\mathrm{op},\natural}
is atomically robust.

Example 7.29
.

Let
\mathscr{O}
be an algebraic pattern in which every object is elementary.
Then the inclusion of
t
in
\mathscr{O}^{\mathrm{el}}_{t/}
is initial for every
t
, hence the conditions of
Section

7.2
become vacuous.
In particular,
\mathscr{O}
is atomically robust.

Example 7.30
.

Recall the algebraic pattern structure on the tree category
\Omega[\mathscr{O}]^{\mathrm{op},\natural}
from
Section

3.1
.
Even if
\mathscr{O}
is robust, the pattern
\Omega[\mathscr{O}]^{\mathrm{op},\natural}
need not be robust.
For example, if
\mathscr{O}=\mathrm{Span}(\mathbb{F})^{\flat}
, then
\Omega[\mathscr{O}]^{\mathrm{op},\natural}
is not saturated and hence not robust.
However, if
\mathscr{O}
is atomically robust, then the next lemma shows that the same is true for
\Omega[\mathscr{O}]^{\mathrm{op},\natural}
.
In particular, the iterated tree construction
{}^{n}[\mathscr{O}]^{\mathrm{op},\natural}
is atomically robust whenever
\mathscr{O}
is.

Lemma 7.31
.

Let
\mathscr{O}
be an atomically robust pattern.
Then the pattern
\Omega[\mathscr{O}]^{\mathrm{op},\natural}
from
Section

3.1
is also atomically robust.

Proof.

We will check the conditions from
Section

7.2
. We start by showing that
\mathscr{O}
is sound.
Let
\phi:[m]\rightsquigarrow[n]
be an active map in
\Delta
. Suppose that
[n;t]
is a tree. We will consider the cartesian map
f:[m;\phi^{*}t]\rightsquigarrow[n;t]
in
\Omega[\mathscr{O}]
. This is an active map, and
Section

3.2
implies that every active map is of this form. Suppose that
g:[k;u]\rightarrowtail[n;t]
is an inert map with
[k;u]
elementary, i.e.
k\leq 1
.
By
[
5
, Lemma 3.3.9]
, we have to show that the category

(9)

\Omega[\mathscr{O}]^{\mathrm{el}}_{/[m;\phi^{*}t]}\times_{\Omega[\mathscr{O}]_{/[n;t]}^{\mathrm{int}}}(\Omega[\mathscr{O}]_{/[n;t]}^{\mathrm{int}})_{g/}

is weakly contractible. Recall from
Section

4.1
that the canonical map

\mathop{colim}\displaylimits_{\alpha:[j]\rightarrowtail[m]\in\Delta^{\mathrm{el}}_{/[m]}}\Omega[\mathscr{O}]^{\mathrm{el}}_{/\left<j;\alpha^{*}\phi^{*}t\right>}\to\Omega[\mathscr{O}]^{\mathrm{el}}_{/[m;\phi^{*}t]}

is an equivalence. As pulling back along the left fibration
(\Omega[\mathscr{O}]^{\mathrm{int}}_{/[n;t]})_{g/}\to\Omega[\mathscr{O}]^{\mathrm{int}}_{/[n;t]}
preserves colimits,
Equation

9
can be written as an iterated pushout of categories of the form

(10)

\Omega[\mathscr{O}]^{\mathrm{el}}_{/\left<j;\alpha^{*}\phi^{*}t\right>}\times_{\Omega[\mathscr{O}]^{\mathrm{int}}_{/[n;t]}}(\Omega[\mathscr{O}]_{/[n;t]}^{\mathrm{int}})_{g/}.

for
\alpha\colon[j]\rightarrowtail[m]
with
j\leq 1
.
To study these categories, consider the factorization

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[j]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[l]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[m]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[n]}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi}

and let us write
\psi\colon[k]\to[n]
for the underlying map of
g
.
The pullback
Equation

10
is non-empty if and only if
\psi
factors through
\beta
, which is the case if and only if
\phi(\alpha(0))\leq\psi(0)\leq\psi(k)\leq\phi(\alpha(j))
.
Observe that there exists at least one inert map
\alpha\colon[1]\rightarrowtail[m]
for which this happens.
We will show that the pullback
Equation

10
is weakly contractible in this case.
This then implies that the classifying space of the category
Equation

9
is an iterated pushout of the form

(\varnothing\cup_{\varnothing}\cdots\cup_{\varnothing}\varnothing)\cup_{\varnothing}(*\cup_{*}\cdots\cup_{*}*)\cup_{\varnothing}(\varnothing\cup_{\varnothing}\cdots\cup_{\varnothing}\varnothing),

hence weakly contractible.
So suppose that
\psi
factors through
\beta
.
In this case
g
factors uniquely as
[k;u]\xrightarrow{g^{\prime}}\langle l;\beta^{*}t\rangle\rightarrowtail[n;t]
and we obtain an equivalence

(11)

\Omega[\mathscr{O}]^{\mathrm{el}}_{/\left<j;\alpha^{*}\phi^{*}t\right>}\times_{\Omega[\mathscr{O}]^{\mathrm{int}}_{/[n;t]}}(\Omega[\mathscr{O}]_{/[n;t]}^{\mathrm{int}})_{g/}\simeq\Omega[\mathscr{O}]^{\mathrm{el}}_{/\left<j;\gamma^{*}\beta^{*}t\right>}\times_{\Omega[\mathscr{O}]^{\mathrm{int}}_{/\left<l;\beta^{*}t\right>}}(\Omega[\mathscr{O}]_{/\left<l,\beta^{*}t\right>}^{\mathrm{int}})_{g^{\prime}/}

To see that this is weakly contractible,
consider the factorization

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\mathscr{O}_{j}^{\mathrm{el},\mathrm{op}})_{/\gamma^{*}\beta^{*}t}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega[\mathscr{O}]^{\mathrm{el}}_{/\left<j;\gamma^{*}\beta^{*}t\right>}.}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega[\mathscr{O}]_{/\left<j;\gamma^{*}\beta^{*}t\right>}}

The left slanted arrow is final since
\mathscr{O}
is sound (see
Section

4.1
) and the right slanted arrow is final on account of
Section

4.1
. Pulling back along a left fibration preserves final functors by
[
24
, Remark 4.1.2.10 & Proposition 4.1.2.15]
. Since final functors induce equivalences on classifying spaces, 2-out-of-3 for weak equivalences implies that the induced functor

(\mathscr{O}_{j}^{\mathrm{el},\mathrm{op}})_{/\gamma^{*}\beta^{*}t}\times_{\Omega[\mathscr{O}]^{\mathrm{int}}_{/\left<l;\beta^{*}t\right>}}(\Omega[\mathscr{O}]_{/\left<l,\beta^{*}t\right>}^{\mathrm{int}})_{g^{\prime}/}\to\Omega[\mathscr{O}]^{\mathrm{el}}_{/\left<j;\gamma^{*}\beta^{*}t\right>}\times_{\Omega[\mathscr{O}]^{\mathrm{int}}_{/\left<l;\beta^{*}t\right>}}(\Omega[\mathscr{O}]_{/\left<l,\beta^{*}t\right>}^{\mathrm{int}})_{g^{\prime}/}

is a weak equivalence on classifying spaces.
Now observe that the left-hand side fits in a diagram of pullback squares

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\mathscr{O}_{j}^{\mathrm{el},\mathrm{op}})_{/\gamma^{*}\beta^{*}t}\times_{\Omega[\mathscr{O}]^{\mathrm{int}}_{/\left<l;\beta^{*}t\right>}}(\Omega[\mathscr{O}]_{/\left<l,\beta^{*}t\right>}^{\mathrm{int}})_{g^{\prime}/}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces((\mathscr{O}_{l}^{\mathrm{el},\mathrm{op}})_{/\beta^{*}t})_{g^{\prime}/}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\Omega[\mathscr{O}]_{/\left<l,\beta^{*}t\right>}^{\mathrm{int}})_{g^{\prime}/}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\mathscr{O}_{j}^{\mathrm{el},\mathrm{op}})_{/\gamma^{*}\beta^{*}t}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\mathscr{O}_{l}^{\mathrm{el},\mathrm{op}})_{/\beta^{*}t}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega[\mathscr{O}]^{\mathrm{int}}_{/\left<l;\beta^{*}t\right>},}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}

Thus we must show that
((\mathscr{O}_{l}^{\mathrm{el},\mathrm{op}})_{/\beta^{*}t})_{g^{\prime}/}
is weakly contractible.
The equivalence
(\mathscr{O}_{l}^{\mathrm{el}})_{\beta^{*}t/}\simeq\mathscr{O}^{\mathrm{el}}_{t_{\beta(l)}/}
from
Section

2.2
induces an equivalence
((\mathscr{O}_{l}^{\mathrm{el},\mathrm{op}})_{/\beta^{*}t})_{g^{\prime}/}\simeq((\mathscr{O}^{\mathrm{el}}_{t_{\beta(l)}/})_{/u_{k}})^{\mathrm{op}}
.
This category has an initial object since
u_{k}
is elementary, hence it is weakly contractible.
We conclude that
Equation

9
is weakly contractible and hence that
\Omega[\mathscr{O}]
is sound.

We will now show that the elementary slices of
\Omega[\mathscr{O}]
are connected.
We can write any elementary slice as an iterated pushout as above, so it suffices to show that for every forest
\left<k;t\right>
with
k\leq 1
, the elementary slice
\Omega[\mathscr{O}]^{\mathrm{el}}_{/\left<k;t\right>}
is connected.
By the same reasoning as
Equation

11
, the map

(\mathscr{O}^{\mathrm{el},\mathrm{op}}_{k})_{/t}\to\Omega[\mathscr{O}]^{\mathrm{el}}_{/\left<k;t\right>}

induces a homotopy equivalence on classifying spaces. Since
\mathscr{O}
has connected elementary slices by assumption, the result follows.

Finally, observe that
Section

8.1
exactly says that the pattern
\Omega[\mathscr{O}]^{\mathrm{op},\natural}
is saturated. We are therefore reduced to showing that a map
[m;s]\to[n;t]
is inert if for any inert map from an elementary
[i;u]\rightarrowtail[m;s]
, the composite
[i;u]\rightarrowtail[m;s]\to[n;t]
is inert.
Since
\Delta
satisfies this property and a map in
\Omega[\mathscr{O}]
is inert precisely if it lies over an inert in
\Delta
, it suffices to show that any inert
\phi\colon[i]\rightarrowtail[m]
admits a lift with target
[m;s]
.
This follows from
Section

3.2
since
(\mathscr{O}_{0}^{\mathrm{op}})_{/s_{\phi(i)}}\simeq\mathscr{O}^{\mathrm{el}}_{s_{\phi(i)}/}
connected (hence non-empty) by assumption.
∎

Remark 7.32
.

Let
\mathscr{O}
be a robust pattern. Instead of considering simplices decorated by elements of
\mathscr{O}
as we do when working with the category
\Omega[\mathscr{O}]
, we could consider non-layered trees, i.e., elements of the dendroidal category
\Omega
, decorated appropriately by atomic objects of
\mathscr{O}
. This would lead to a category
{}_{nl}[\mathscr{O}]
of non-layered trees that we conjecture is a robust pattern whenever
\mathscr{O}
is.

8.
Necessity of the Conduché criterion

This section is dedicated to the proof of
B
, the converse of
A
, for so-called
robust
algebraic patterns:

Theorem 8.1
.

Let
\mathscr{O}
be a robust algebraic pattern. Then a map
p:X\to Y
in
\mathrm{CSeg}(\Omega[\mathscr{O}])
satisfies
Item

1
of
Theorem

5.2
if
p
is exponentiable.

The converse of
Theorem

5.5
then automatically follows from the dictionary of
Section

5.1
.

Throughout this section, we will fix a robust algebraic pattern
\mathscr{O}
.

8.1.
Relative Segality

By
Section

4.2
,
\pi_{0}
induces a functor

q:=\Omega[\pi_{0}]:\Omega[\mathscr{O}]\to\Omega[\mathrm{Span}(\mathbb{F})^{\flat}]

between tree categories. If
\left<n;t\right>
is a forest, then we have a projection

[n;t]\to q^{*}[n;\pi_{0}(t)]

obtained by applying
i^{*}:\mathrm{PSh}(\Phi[\mathscr{O}])\to\mathrm{PSh}(\Omega[\mathscr{O}])
to the canonical map
\left<n;t\right>\to\Phi[\pi_{0}]^{*}\left<n;\pi_{0}(t)\right>
. The goal of this subsection is to show the following:

Proposition 8.2
.

The projection
[n;t]\to q^{*}[n;\pi_{0}(t)]
is a complete Segal fibration for every forest
\left<n;t\right>
.

Recall from
Section

4.2
that we have a natural map
q_{!}(X\boxtimes_{[n]}[n;t])\to X\boxtimes_{[n]}[n;\pi_{0}(t)]
which is an equivalence if
q
is strong Segal. In general,
q
fails to be strong Segal. Still, the following holds:

Lemma 8.3
.

If
Y
is a discrete
\Omega[\mathrm{Span}(\mathbb{F})]
-space, then the induced map

\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{PSh}(\Omega[\mathrm{Span}(\mathbb{F})])}(X\boxtimes_{[n]}[n;\pi_{0}(t)],Y)\to\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{PSh}(\Omega[\mathscr{O}])}(X\boxtimes_{[n]}[n;t],q^{*}Y)

is an equivalence for every forest
\left<n;t\right>
and every simplicial space
X\to[n]
.

Proof.

By
Section

4.1
, it suffices to handle the case that
X=[n]
. By the decomposition formula of
Section

7.2
, we may moreover assume that
(\mathscr{O}^{\mathrm{el}}_{n})_{t/}
is connected. The map
q_{!}[n;t]\to[n;\pi_{0}(t)]
is computed by
\mathop{colim}\displaylimits_{s\in(\mathscr{O}^{\mathrm{el}}_{n})_{t/}}[n;\pi_{0}(s)]\to[n;\pi_{0}(t)]
, as
\mathscr{O}
is sound.
Now, each map
[n;\pi_{0}(s)]\to[n;\pi_{0}(t)]
is an equivalence as it lies over
\mathrm{id}_{[n]}
and
\pi_{0}(s)
and
\pi_{0}(t)
are equivalent to
\underline{1}
.
Thus
q_{!}[n;t]\to[n;\pi_{0}(t)]
is equivalent to the projection
|(\mathscr{O}^{\mathrm{el}}_{n})_{t/}|\times[n;\pi_{0}(t)]\to[n;\pi_{0}(t)]
. The map induced on hom-spaces from the statement of the lemma is thus given by

\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{PSh}(\Omega[\mathrm{Span}(\mathbb{F})])}([n;\pi_{0}(t)],Y)\to\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathscr{S}}(|\mathscr{O}^{\mathrm{el}}_{t/}|,\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{PSh}(\Omega[\mathrm{Span}(\mathbb{F})])}([n;\pi_{0}(t)],Y)).

This is an equivalence since
\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{PSh}(\Omega[\mathrm{Span}(\mathbb{F})])}([n;\pi_{0}(t)],Y)
is a set and
|(\mathscr{O}^{\mathrm{el}}_{n})_{t/}|
is connected.
∎

Lemma 8.4
.

Let
\langle n;t\rangle
and
\langle m;s\rangle
be forests. Suppose that we have a map
f:\left<m;\pi_{0}(s)\right>\to\left<n;\pi_{0}(t)\right>
of forests over
\phi:[m]\to[n]
determined by a commutative diagram of pullback squares

cf.
Section

3.1
.
Then there is a pullback square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\prodop\displaylimits_{i\in\pi_{0}(s)}\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathscr{O}_{m}}((\phi^{*}t)^{\alpha_{m}(i)},s^{i})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{PSh}(\Omega[\mathscr{O}])}([m;s],[n;t])}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\{\bar{f}\}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{PSh}(\Omega[\mathrm{Span}(\mathbb{F})])}([m;\pi_{0}(s)],[n;\pi_{0}(t)])},}

where the right vertical arrow is induced by
[n;t]\to q^{*}[n;\pi_{0}(t)]
(see
Section

8.1
), and
\bar{f}
is the map
[m;\pi_{0}(s)]\to[n;\pi_{0}(t)]
induced by
f
.

Proof.

Suppose first that
[m;s]
is a tree. The right vertical map of the square above may be identified with the top right vertical map induced by
\Phi[\pi_{0}]
in the commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathscr{O}_{m}}(\phi^{*}t,s)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{Hom}_{\Phi[\mathscr{O}]}(\left<m;s\right>,\left<n;t\right>)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{Span}(\mathbb{F})_{m}}(\phi^{*}(\pi_{0}(t)),\pi_{0}(s))}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{Hom}_{\Phi[\mathrm{Span}(\mathbb{F})]}(\left<m;\pi_{0}(s)\right>,\left<n;\pi_{0}(t)\right>)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\{\phi\}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{Hom}_{\Delta}([m],[n])}.}

The total and lower squares are pullback squares, so the top one is a pullback square as well by right cancellation. Pulling back this upper square along the morphism
\alpha_{m}:\pi_{0}(t_{\phi(m)})\rightarrowtail\pi_{0}(s_{m})\simeq\underline{1}
leads to the result by
Section

7.2
.

Suppose now that
\langle m;s\rangle
is a forest. Then the canonical horizontal arrows in the square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{PSh}(\Omega[\mathscr{O}])}([m;s],[n;t])}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\prodop\displaylimits_{i\in\pi_{0}(s)}\lim_{e\in(\mathscr{O}^{\mathrm{el}}_{m})_{s^{i}/}}{\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{PSh}(\Omega[\mathscr{O}])}([m;e],[n;t])}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{PSh}(\Omega[\mathscr{O}])}([m;s],q^{*}[n;\pi_{0}(t)])}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\prodop\displaylimits_{i\in\pi_{0}(s)}\lim_{e\in(\mathscr{O}^{\mathrm{el}}_{m})_{s^{i}/}}{\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{PSh}(\Omega[\mathscr{O}])}([m;e],q^{*}[n;\pi_{0}(t)])}}

are equivalences by
Section

4.1
and
Section

7.2
applied to
X=[n]
. It follows from the above case that the fiber of the left vertical arrow above
f
is computed by the product

\prodop\displaylimits_{i\in\pi_{0}(s)}\lim_{e\in(\mathscr{O}^{\mathrm{el}}_{m})_{s^{i}/}}\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathscr{O}_{m}}((\phi^{*}t)^{\alpha_{m}(i)},e).

As
\mathscr{O}_{m}
is saturated by
Section

7.2
, the canonical map

\textstyle\prodop\displaylimits_{i\in\pi_{0}(s)}\mathrm{Hom}((\phi^{*}t)^{\alpha_{m}(i)},s^{i})\to\prodop\displaylimits_{i\in\pi_{0}(s)}\lim_{e\in(\mathscr{O}^{\mathrm{el}}_{m})_{s^{i}/}}\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathscr{O}_{m}}((\phi^{*}t)^{\alpha_{m}(i)},e)

is an equivalence.
∎

Moreover, we will need the following observation to finish the proof of the main result
Section

8.1
of this subsection.

Lemma 8.5
.

Let
f_{i}:A_{i}\to B_{i}
be a cartesian family of complete Segal fibrations between
\Omega[\mathscr{O}]
-spaces indexed by a category
I
.
Then the induced morphism
\mathop{colim}\displaylimits_{I}f_{i}:\mathop{colim}\displaylimits_{I}A_{i}\to\mathop{colim}\displaylimits_{I}B_{i}
is a complete Segal fibration.

Proof.

As all generating complete Segal extensions have a representable codomain, it is sufficient to check that for every morphism
g:[n;t]\to\mathop{colim}\displaylimits_{I}B_{i}
, the pullback
g^{*}\mathop{colim}\displaylimits_{I}f_{i}\to[n;t]
is a complete Segal fibration. Let
g
be such a morphism. Remark that there necessarily exists a (non-unique)
j
in
I
such that
g
factors through
B_{j}\to\mathop{colim}\displaylimits_{I}B_{i}
. As
\mathrm{PSh}(\Omega[\mathscr{O}])
is a topos, and as the family of diagrams is cartesian, by
[
24
, Theorem 6.1.0.6]
we have a diagram of cartesian squares:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{g^{*}\mathop{colim}\displaylimits_{I}f_{i}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A_{j}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathop{colim}\displaylimits_{I}A_{i}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[n;t]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{B_{j}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathop{colim}\displaylimits_{I}B_{i}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathop{colim}\displaylimits_{I}f_{i}}

As the assumption implies that the middle map is a complete Segal fibration, so is the left one, which concludes the proof.
∎

Proof of
Section

8.1
.

We need to show that the map

(12)

\mathrm{Hom}_{/q^{*}[n;\pi_{0}(t)]}([m;s],[n;t])\to\mathrm{Hom}_{/q^{*}[n;\pi_{0}(t)]}(\Gamma[m;s],[n;t])

is an equivalence
for every map
f:[m;s]\to q^{*}[n;\pi_{0}(t)]
so that
[m;s]
is a tree. As
[m;s]
is a tree, this comes from a map
\left<m;\pi_{0}(s)\right>\to\left<n;\pi_{0}(t)\right>
between forests by adjunction, that we will denote by
f
as well. Let
\phi:[m]\to[n]
be its underlying map in
\Delta
and
\alpha\colon\pi_{0}(\phi^{*}(t))\to\pi_{0}(s)
the underlying map in
\mathrm{Span}(\mathbb{F})_{m}
.
Note that the commutative square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[m;\phi^{*}t]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[n;t]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q^{*}{[m;\pi_{0}(\phi^{*}t)]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q^{*}{[n;\pi_{0}(t)]}}

is cartesian by pullback pasting. Therefore, we may reduce to the case that
[n]=[m]
and
\phi=\mathrm{id}_{[m]}
in what follows. Now, by the decomposition formula of
Section

7.2
(applied to both patterns
\mathscr{O}
and
\mathrm{Span}(\mathbb{F})
), we can identify the projection
[m;t]\to q^{*}[m;\pi_{0}(t)]
with the coproduct of projections
\coprodop\displaylimits_{i\in\pi_{0}(t_{m})}[m;t^{i}]\to\coprodop\displaylimits_{i\in\pi_{0}(t_{m})}q^{*}[m;\pi_{0}(t)^{i}]
. Thus by
Section

8.1
, we may further reduce to the case that
[n]=[m]
,
\phi=\mathrm{id}_{[m]}
and
\pi_{0}(t_{m})\simeq\underline{1}
. In particular, the component
\alpha_{m}
is an equivalence.

By
Section

8.1
, the map
Equation

12
is then computed as the gap map in the top square of the commutative diagram

(13)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathscr{O}_{m}}(t,s)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathscr{O}_{1}}(t_{\leq m-1},s_{\leq m-1})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\prodop\displaylimits_{i\in\pi_{0}(s_{m-1})}\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathscr{O}_{m-1}}(t_{\leq m-1}^{\alpha_{m-1}(i)},s_{\leq m-1}^{i})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\prodop\displaylimits_{i\in\pi_{0}(s_{m-1})}\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathscr{O}_{0}}(t_{m-1}^{\alpha_{m-1}(i)},s_{m-1}^{i})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\prodop\displaylimits_{i\in\pi_{0}(s_{m-1})}\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathscr{O}_{m-1}}(t_{\leq m-1},s_{\leq m-1}^{i})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\prodop\displaylimits_{i\in\pi_{0}(s_{m-1})}\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathscr{O}_{0}}(t_{m-1},s_{m-1}^{i}).}}

As
\mathscr{O}_{m-1}
is saturated by
Section

7.2
, the bottom map is equivalent to

\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathscr{O}_{m-1}}(t_{\leq m-1},s_{\leq m-1})\to\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathscr{O}_{0}}(t_{m-1},s_{m-1})

and so the outer rectangle is cartesian as
\mathscr{O}_{\bullet}
is a double category.
By pullback pasting, it therefore suffices to show that the bottom square of
Equation

13
is cartesian.
We will fix
i\in\pi_{0}(s_{m-1})
and show this for the
i
-th factor.
Note that we have a cube

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(\mathscr{O}_{m-1})_{t^{\alpha_{m-1}(i)}_{\leq m-1}/}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(\mathscr{O}_{0})_{t^{\alpha_{m-1}(i)}_{m-1}/}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(\mathscr{O}_{m-1})_{t_{\leq m-1}/}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(\mathscr{O}_{0})_{t_{m-1}/}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathscr{O}_{m-1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathscr{O}_{0}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathscr{O}_{m-1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathscr{O}_{0}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}

Both the bottom and top square are cartesian. Taking fibers of the vertical maps over
s^{i}_{\leq m-1}
, it follows that the
i
-th factor of the bottom square of
Equation

13
is cartesian.
We conclude that the top square of
Equation

13
is cartesian.

The proof of the unique left lifting property against generating completeness extension is shown in a similar way using the fact that the double category
\mathscr{O}_{\bullet}
is complete.
∎

Corollary 8.6
.

Let
\mathscr{O}
be an atomically robust pattern. Then the
\Omega[\mathscr{O}]
-Segal space
[n;t]
is complete Segal for every forest
\langle n;t\rangle
.

Proof.

As
\mathscr{O}
is atomically robust,
[n;\pi_{0}(t)]
corresponds to the tree
\coprodop\displaylimits_{\pi_{0}(t_{n})}L_{n}
where
L_{n}
is the tree
[n;\underline{1}=\underline{1}=\ldots=\underline{1}]
. The unary tree
L_{n}
is precisely the image of
[n]
under the functor
\iota:\mathrm{CSeg}(\Delta)\to\mathrm{CSeg}(\Omega[\mathrm{Span}(\mathbb{F})])
of
Section

6
. Thus
[n;\pi_{0}(t)]
is complete Segal. As
[n;\pi_{0}(t)]
is also discrete,
q^{*}[n;\pi_{0}(t)]
is a complete Segal
\Omega[\mathscr{O}]
-space by
Section

8.1
. The desired conclusion now follows from
Section

8.1
.
∎

8.2.
The grafting construction

Recall that we are working with a robust pattern
\mathscr{O}
. The key ingredient for our proof of
B
is the so-called
grafting construction
:

Construction 8.7
.

Let
u:u_{0}\rightsquigarrow u_{1}
and
t:t_{0}\rightsquigarrow u_{0}^{i}
be two active morphisms with
i\in\pi_{0}(u_{0})
. Then there exists a unique active morphism
\underline{t}\rightsquigarrow u_{0}
whose projection by the functor
\mathscr{O}^{\mathrm{act}}_{/u_{0}}\to\mathscr{O}^{\mathrm{act}}_{/u_{0}^{j}}
is given by

\begin{cases}t:t_{0}\rightsquigarrow u_{0}^{i}&\text{if $i=j$},\\
\mathrm{id}:u_{0}^{j}\to u_{0}^{j}&\text{otherwise}.\end{cases}

We then obtain a forest

\textstyle\left<2;u\star^{i}t\right>:=\left<2;\underline{t}\rightsquigarrow u_{0}\rightsquigarrow u_{1}\right>.

For
[k]\in\Delta
, we will consider the
\Omega[\mathscr{O}]
-space defined by the pushout square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\coprodop\displaylimits_{j\neq i\in\pi_{0}(u_{0})}(T_{k}[1]\times_{[2]}[1])\boxtimes_{[1]}[1;u_{0}^{j}=u_{0}^{j}]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{T_{k}[1]\boxtimes_{[2]}[2;u\star^{i}t]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\coprodop\displaylimits_{j\neq i\in\pi_{0}(u_{0})}[0;u_{0}^{j}]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{T_{k}[u\circ^{i}t]}.}

We then set

[u\circ^{i}t]:=T_{0}[u\circ^{i}t],\quad\quad\Gamma[u\circ^{i}t]:=[1;t^{i}]\cup_{[1;u_{0}^{i}]}[1;u].

The goal of this subsection is to show the following:

Proposition 8.8
.

Let
u,t
and
\gamma
be as in
Section

8.2
.
Then the map
\Gamma[u\circ^{i}t]\to[u\circ^{i}t]
is a Segal extension and
[u\circ^{i}t]
is a complete Segal space.
Moreover, for any forest
\langle n;s\rangle
with
n\leq 1
, the
\Omega[\mathscr{O}]
-space
[n;s]
is complete Segal.

The name of the grafting construction comes from the fact that it recovers the grafting construction for the prototypical case of operads. This is explained during the demonstration of the following lemma:

Lemma 8.9
.

If
\mathscr{O}
is the pattern
\mathrm{Span}(\mathbb{F})^{\flat}
, then the
\Omega[\mathrm{Span}(\mathbb{F})^{\flat}]
-space
[u\circ^{i}t]
is complete Segal, or equivalently, is an operad.
Moreover, for any forest
\langle n;t\rangle
with
n\leq 1
, the
\Omega[\mathrm{Span}(\mathbb{F})^{\flat}]
-space
[n;t]
is complete Segal.

Proof.

Note that we have a composite
\Psi\colon\Omega[\mathrm{Span}(\mathbb{F})^{\flat}]\to\Omega\hookrightarrow\mathrm{Op}
sending a tree to the free operad generated by this tree; here
\Omega
is the dendroidal category of Moerdijk–Weiss
[
26
]
.
Given an operad
P
, its
nerve

N(P)\coloneqq\mathrm{Hom}(\Psi(-),P)
defines a complete Segal
\Omega[\mathrm{Span}(\mathbb{F})^{\flat}]
-space by
[
11
, Theorem 1.1]
.
We will prove the first part of the lemma by showing that
[u\circ^{i}t]
is of the form
N(P)
for some operad
P
.
Suppose that
|u_{0}|=m
and
|t_{0}|=n
; then we will show that
[u\circ^{i}t]=N(P)
where
P
is the operad freely generated by the tree

p
q
\cdots
\cdots
n\text{-times}
(m-1)\text{-times}

where
p
has
m
ingoing edges and
q
has
n
ingoing edges. To be precise, the edges of this tree are the colors of
P
, while the (non-identity) operations of
P
are the vertices
p
and
q
and their composite
p\circ^{i}q
.
Note that we have a canonical comparison map
[2;u\star^{i}t]\to N(P)
given by the operad map

\scalebox{0.8}{\hbox to111.37pt{\vbox to114.32pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 65.64142pt\lower-0.4pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin}

{}{{}{}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{}}{{}}{{}{}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}{}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{{{}{}{{}}

}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}
{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -15.75 86.61 M -15.75 86.61 C -14.71 88.69 -12.29 90.07 -8.83 90.07 L 16.7 90.07 C 20.16 90.07 22.58 91.46 23.62 93.53 C 24.66 91.46 27.08 90.07 30.54 90.07 L 56.07 90.07 C 59.53 90.07 61.95 88.69 62.99 86.61}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{

{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{2.95956pt}{74.62898pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}

\lxSVG@closescope {{
{}{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}

that sends
p
and
q
to themselves, and all other vertices to the identity operation of the corresponding color.
This map factors through the pushout
[u\circ^{i}t]
by construction.
We will show that this map
\chi\colon[u\circ^{i}t]\to N(P)
is an equivalence.
This comes down to showing that for any layered tree
[n;s]\in\Omega[\mathscr{O}]
and any operad map
f\colon\Psi([n;t])\to P
, there is a unique map
\tilde{f}\colon[n;s]\to[u\circ^{i}t]
such that the composite
\chi\circ\tilde{f}\colon[n;s]\to N(P)
is the adjunct of
f
.

First consider the case
m=1
.
In this case
[u\circ^{i}t]=[2;u_{0}\rightsquigarrow\underline{1}\rightsquigarrow\underline{1}]
, so the claim reduces to showing that any map from (the operad associated with) a layered tree to
P
automatically respects the layering. This is clear.
Now assume
n=1
and
m\neq 1
.
If a map
f\colon[n;s]\to N(P)
exists, then either
[n;s]
is a linear tree, meaning that
s_{0}=s_{1}=\cdots=s_{n}=\underline{1}
, or the tree
[n;s]
contains precisely one vertex with
m
ingoing edges and all other vertices have exactly one ingoing edge.
In the first case, there can potentially be multiple lifts of
f
to a map
[n;s]\to[2;u\star^{i}t]
, but the pushout defining
[u\circ^{i}t]
will always identify these with each other.
In the second case, the vertex with
m
ingoing edges needs to be sent to
p
. Such a map always lifts uniquely to a map of layered trees
[n;s]\to[2;u\star^{i}t]
.
Now suppose
m,n\neq 1
and let
f\colon[n;s]\to N(P)
be given.
If
[n;s]
is a linear tree, then as in the previous case a lift of
f
to
[2;u\star^{i}t]
exists, but need not be unique if
[n;s]
hits one of the leaves attached to
p
.
However, the pushout defining
[2;u\star^{i}t]
identifies all these lifts.
On the other hand, if
[n;s]
is not a linear tree, then any lift of
f
to
[2;u\star^{i}t]
and hence
[u\circ^{i}t]
is necessarily unique.
We conclude that
[u\circ^{i}t]\to N(P)
is an equivalence.

For the second part of the lemma, a similar (but simpler) argument shows that
[n;t]
is complete Segal for any tree of length
n\leq 1
.
If
\langle n;t\rangle
is a forest instead, then the claim follows since
[n;t]
is a coproduct of trees of length
n
in
\mathrm{PSh}(\Omega[\mathscr{O}])
, and the operadic nerve
\mathrm{Op}\to\mathrm{PSh}(\Omega)\to\mathrm{PSh}(\Omega[\mathscr{O}])
preserves coproducts.
∎

Proof of
Section

8.2
.

By construction, we have a pushout square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Gamma[2;u\star^{i}t]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[2;u\star^{i}t]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Gamma[u\circ^{i}t]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[u\circ^{i}t]}}

so that the bottom arrow is a complete Segal extension as well.

To show that
[u\circ^{i}t]
is complete Segal, we first note that
[u\circ^{i}t]\to q^{*}[\pi_{0}(u)\circ^{i}\pi_{0}(t)]
is a complete Segal fibration.
Namely, we have pullback squares

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\coprodop\displaylimits_{j\neq i\in\pi_{0}(u_{0})}[0;u_{0}^{j}]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\coprodop\displaylimits_{j\neq i\in\pi_{0}(u_{0})}[1;u_{0}^{j}=u_{0}^{j}]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[2;u\star^{i}t]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\coprodop\displaylimits_{j\neq i\in\pi_{0}(u_{0})}q^{*}[0;\pi_{0}(u)_{0}^{j}]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\coprodop\displaylimits_{j\neq i\in\pi_{0}(u_{0})}q^{*}[1;\pi_{0}(u)_{0}^{j}=\pi_{0}(u)_{0}^{j}]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{q^{*}[2;\pi_{0}(u)\star^{i}\pi_{0}(t)]}.}

By construction, the map
[u\circ^{i}t]\to q^{*}[\pi_{0}(u)\circ^{i}\pi_{0}(t)]
is obtained by taking pushouts horizontally in this diagram. Thus it is a complete Segal fibration by

Section

8.1
and
Section

8.1
.
As
q^{*}
preserves discrete complete Segal objects by
Section

8.1
, the prototypical case of
Section

8.2
implies that
q^{*}[\pi_{0}(u)\circ^{i}\pi_{0}(t)]
is complete Segal. We conclude that
[u\circ^{i}t]
is complete Segal.

The second part of the proposition follows immediately by combining
Sections

8.1
and
8.2
.
∎

8.3.
Another explicit replacement

Suppose that
X\to[u\circ^{i}t]
is a complete Segal fibration. Then we will prove
Theorem

8.1
by constructing an explicit replacement for
X\times_{[u\circ^{i}t]}\Gamma[u\circ^{i}t]\to[u\circ^{i}t]
using the
Q
functor of
Section

5.3
.

Construction 8.10
.

Let
X\in\mathrm{PSh}(\Omega[\mathscr{O}])_{/[u\circ^{i}t]}
. We will write
X_{0}:=X\times_{[u\circ^{i}t]}[2;u\star^{i}t]
. Using
Section

5.3
, we can construct the following commutative diagram:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{3}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{QX_{0}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{4}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces RX}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\coprodop\displaylimits_{j\neq i\in\pi_{0}(u_{0})}[1;u_{0}^{j}=u_{0}^{j}]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Gamma[2;u\star^{i}t]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[2;u\star^{i}t]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\coprodop\displaylimits_{j\neq i\in\pi_{0}(u_{0})}[0;u_{0}^{j}]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Gamma[u\circ^{i}t]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[u\circ^{i}t]}}

where the
X_{i}
’s are pullbacks of
X
, and
RX\coloneqq X_{2}\cup_{X_{1}}QX_{0}
.
The back square involving the map
X_{1}\to QX_{0}
is cartesian as remarked in
Section

5.3
.
We note that there is a canonical factorization

X_{2}=X\times_{[u\circ^{i}t]}\Gamma[u\circ^{i}t]\to RX\to X.

Proposition 8.11
.

The map
X\times_{[u\circ^{i}t]}\Gamma[u\circ^{i}t]\to RX
is a Segal extension for all maps
p:X\to[u\circ^{i}t]
, and
RX\to[u\circ^{i}t]
is a complete Segal fibration if
p
is a complete Segal fibration.

Proof.

The first assertion follows directly from
Section

5.3
. If
p
is a complete Segal fibration, then
Section

5.3
implies that the morphism
QX_{0}\to[2;u\star^{i}t]
is a complete Segal fibration. The result then follows from
Section

8.1
as
RX\to[u\circ^{i}t]
is the colimit of a cartesian diagram of complete Segal fibrations.
∎

The next goal is to provide a computation of
R
that is similar to the computation of
Q
established in
Section

5.3
.

Lemma 8.12
.

Let
X\to[2;u\star^{i}t]
be a complete Segal fibration. Then the induced map

\mathrm{Hom}_{/[2;u\star^{i}t]}(T_{\bullet}[1]\boxtimes_{[2]}[2;u\star^{i}t],X)\to\prodop\displaylimits_{i\neq j}\mathrm{Hom}_{/[2;u\star^{i}t]}([1+\bullet]\boxtimes_{[1]}[1;u_{0}^{j}=u_{0}^{j}],X)

is map of complete Segal spaces, and can thus be viewed as a functor of categories. As such, it is a cartesian fibration.

Proof.

It follows from
Section

4.1
that this is indeed a map between complete Segal spaces.
Note that
[1+\bullet]\simeq T_{\bullet}[1]\times_{[2]}[1]
, where the pullback is taken along
d_{2}\colon[1]\to[2]
.
We get an induced map

\mathrm{Hom}_{/[2;u\star^{i}t]}(T_{\bullet}[1]\boxtimes_{[2]}[2;u\star^{i}t],X)\xrightarrow{\alpha}\mathrm{Hom}_{/[2;u\star^{i}t]}((T_{\bullet}[1]\times_{[2]}[1])\boxtimes_{[2]}[2;u\star^{i}t],X).

We claim that this is a right fibration.
To this end, we have to show that the square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Hom}_{/[2;u\star^{i}t]}({T_{1}[1]\boxtimes_{[2]}[2;u\star^{i}t]},X)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Hom}_{/[2;u\star^{i}t]}({T_{0}[1]\boxtimes_{[2]}[2;u\star^{i}t]},X)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Hom}_{/[2;u\star^{i}t]}({(T_{1}[1]\times_{[2]}[1])\boxtimes_{[2]}[2;u\star^{i}t]},X)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Hom}_{/[2;u\star^{i}t]}({(T_{0}[1]\times_{[2]}[1])\boxtimes_{[2]}[2;u\star^{i}t]},X)}

induced by
\{1\}\to[1]
is a pullback square.
But this follows from
Section

4.1
and the observation that the following square is a pushout in the category of complete Segal spaces over
[2]
:

Now, using the decomposition formula of
Section

7.2
, the following square is a pullback

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{Hom}_{/[2;u\star^{i}t]}([1+\bullet]\boxtimes_{[2]}[2;u\star^{i}t],X)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{Hom}_{/[2;u\star^{i}t]}([1+\bullet]\boxtimes_{[1]}[1;t],X)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\prodop\displaylimits_{i\neq j}\mathrm{Hom}_{/[2;u\star^{i}t]}([1+\bullet]\boxtimes_{[1]}[1;u_{0}^{j}=u_{0}^{j}],X)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ast.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta}

As the right vertical morphism is a cartesian fibration, so is the left vertical one. This implies that
\beta\alpha
is a cartesian fibration, as desired.
∎

Lemma 8.13
.

Suppose that
X\to[u\circ^{i}t]
is a complete Segal fibration.
Then the morphism

\mathop{colim}\displaylimits_{[k]\in\Delta^{\mathrm{op}}}\mathrm{Hom}_{/[u\circ^{i}t]}(T_{k}[u\circ^{i}t],X)\to\mathop{colim}\displaylimits_{[k]\in\Delta^{\mathrm{op}}}\mathrm{Hom}_{/[u\circ^{i}t]}(T_{k}[1]\boxtimes_{[2]}[2;u\star^{i}t],X)

is an equivalence.

Proof.

By construction, we have a pullback square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{Hom}_{/[u\circ^{i}t]}(T_{\bullet}[u\circ^{i}t],X)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{Hom}_{/[u\circ^{i}t]}(T_{\bullet}[1]\boxtimes_{[2]}[2;u\star^{i}t],X)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\prodop\displaylimits_{i\neq j}\mathrm{Hom}_{/[u\circ^{i}t]}([0;u_{0}^{j}],X)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\prodop\displaylimits_{i\neq j}\mathrm{Hom}_{/[u\circ^{i}t]}([1+\bullet]\boxtimes_{[1]}[1;u_{0}^{j}=u_{0}^{j}],X)}}

in
\mathrm{PSh}(\Delta)
. All vertices of this square are complete Segal spaces, so we can view this as a pullback square in
\mathrm{Cat}
. We have to show that the top functor induces an equivalence on classifying spaces. To this end, we will show that it is initial. Note that the right vertical map is a cartesian fibration by
Section

8.3
. So it suffices to check that the bottom horizontal map is initial.
The inclusions
[0;u_{0}^{j}]\to[1+k]\boxtimes_{[1]}[1;u_{0}^{j}=u_{0}^{j}]
induced by
\{0\}\to[1+k]
provide a left deformation retract for the bottom horizontal morphism, and that implies that it is initial.
∎

Proposition 8.14
.

Let
X\to[u\circ^{i}t]
be a complete Segal fibration.
Then the maps in the canonical span

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathop{colim}\displaylimits_{[k]\in\Delta^{\mathrm{op}}}\mathrm{Hom}_{/[u\circ^{i}t]}(T_{k}[u\circ^{i}t],RX)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Hom}_{/[u\circ^{i}t]}([1;\underline{t}\rightsquigarrow u_{1}],RX)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathop{colim}\displaylimits_{[k]\in\Delta^{\mathrm{op}}}\mathrm{Hom}_{/[u\circ^{i}t]}(T_{k}[u\circ^{i}t],X)}

are equivalences. Here
\underline{t}\rightsquigarrow u_{1}
is the active arrow defined in
Section

8.2
.

Proof.

This now readily follows from
Section

5.3
and
Section

8.3
.
∎

8.4.
The proof of
B

The proof of
Theorem

8.1
proceeds by inductively grafting. To formulate this procedure, we need a more elaborate version of
Section

8.2
:

Construction 8.15

(The multigrafting construction)
.

Let
u:u_{0}\rightsquigarrow u_{1}
be an active arrow. Suppose that
E
is a subset of
\pi_{0}(u_{0})
, and that we have an active arrow
t^{i}:t_{0}^{i}\rightsquigarrow u_{0}^{i}
for every
i\in E
.
We consider the unique active morphism
\underline{t}\rightsquigarrow u_{0}
whose projection by the functor
\mathscr{O}^{\mathrm{act}}_{/u_{0}}\to\mathscr{O}^{\mathrm{act}}_{/u_{0}^{i}}
is given by

\begin{cases}t^{i}:t_{0}^{i}\rightsquigarrow u_{0}^{i}&\text{if $i\in E$},\\
\mathrm{id}:u_{0}^{i}\to u_{0}^{i}&\text{otherwise}.\end{cases}

We then obtain a forest

\textstyle\left<2;u\star^{E}(t^{i})^{i\in E}\right>:=\left<2;\underline{t}\rightsquigarrow u_{0}\rightsquigarrow u_{1}\right>.

For
[k]\in\Delta
, we will consider the
\Omega[\mathscr{O}]
-space defined by the pushout square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\coprodop\displaylimits_{i\notin E}(T_{k}[1]\times_{[2]}[1])\boxtimes_{[1]}[1;u_{0}^{i}=u_{0}^{i}]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{T_{k}[1]\boxtimes_{[2]}[2;u\star^{E}(t^{i})^{i\in E}]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\coprodop\displaylimits_{i\notin E}[0;u_{0}^{i}]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{T_{k}[u\circ^{E}(t^{i})^{i\in E}],}}

and set
[u\circ^{E}(t^{i})^{i\in E}]:=T_{0}[u\circ^{E}(t^{i})^{i\in E}]
.

Example 8.16
.

If
|E|=1
, then
Section

8.4
specializes to the previous grafting construction of
Section

8.2
. We now consider the maximal case
E=\pi_{0}(u_{0})
.
Suppose that
u:u_{0}\rightsquigarrow u_{1}
is an active arrow. If
t:t_{0}\rightsquigarrow u_{0}
is an active arrow, then we can consider the collection of actives
(t^{i}:t_{0}^{i}\rightsquigarrow u_{0}^{i})^{i\in E}
. It readily follows from the formula that

[u\circ^{E}(t^{i})^{i\in E})]\simeq[2;t_{0}\rightsquigarrow u_{0}\rightsquigarrow u_{1}]
.

We construct an auxiliary object that encodes coherences concerning the associativity of multigrafting:

Construction 8.17
.

Let
E
,
u
,
(t^{i})^{i\in E}
as in
Section

8.4
. Suppose then that
|E|>1
, and let
x\in E
. We will write
F:=E\setminus\{x\}
.
We consider the unique commutative diagram of active arrows

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces v_{00}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces v_{10}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces v_{01}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces v_{11}\simeq u_{0}}

over
u_{1}
so that for
i\in\pi_{0}(u_{0})
its projection by the functor
\mathscr{O}^{\mathrm{act}}_{/u_{0}}\to\mathscr{O}^{\mathrm{act}}_{/u_{0}^{i}}
is given by the square
