We define an auxiliary
\Omega[\mathscr{O}]
-space
A
by the pushout square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[2;v_{00}\rightsquigarrow v_{11}\rightsquigarrow u_{1}]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[3;v_{00}\rightsquigarrow v_{10}\rightsquigarrow v_{11}\rightsquigarrow u_{1}]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[3;v_{00}\rightsquigarrow v_{01}\rightsquigarrow v_{11}\rightsquigarrow u_{1}]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A.}

As
([1]\times[1])*[0]\simeq[3]\cup_{[2]}[3]
,
the pushout
A
is canonically fibered over
p^{*}(([1]\times[1])*[0])
. We then define

A_{k,l}:=(([1+k]\times[1+l])*[0])\boxtimes_{([1]\times[1])*[0]}A

for
[k],[l]\in\Delta
. Here
[1+k],[1+l]\to[1]
are the maps that carry
0
to
0
and all other elements to
1
. Moreover, for
i\in\pi_{0}(u_{0})
, we consider the projection

(B^{i}_{k,l}\to C^{i}_{k,l}):=\begin{cases}\penalty\ {([1+k]\times[1+l])\boxtimes_{[0]}[0;u^{i}_{0}]}\rightarrow{[0;u^{i}_{0}]}&\text{if $i\notin E$},\\
\penalty\ {([1+k]\times[1+l])\boxtimes_{[1]}[1;t^{i}]}\rightarrow{[1+k]\boxtimes_{[1]}[1;t^{i}]}&\text{if $i\in F$, and}\\
\penalty\ {([1+k]\times[1+l])\boxtimes_{[1]}[1;t^{x}]}\rightarrow{[1+l]\boxtimes_{[1]}[1;t^{x}]}&\text{if $i=x$}.\end{cases}

We now define
\Upsilon_{k,l}
by the pushout square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\coprodop\displaylimits_{i\in\pi_{0}(u_{0})}B^{i}_{k,l}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{k,l}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\coprodop\displaylimits_{i\in\pi_{0}(u_{0})}C^{i}_{k,l}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Upsilon_{k,l},}

where the top map is the canonical map

\coprodop\displaylimits_{i\in\pi_{0}(u_{0})}B^{i}_{k,l}\simeq([1+k]\times[1+l])\boxtimes_{([1+k]\times[1+l])\ast[0]}A_{k,l}\to A_{k,l}.

Lemma 8.18
.

Let
k,l\geq 0
.
The canonical inclusion

\left(\coprodop\displaylimits_{i\in F}[1+k]\boxtimes_{[1]}[1;t^{i}]\sqcup[1+l]\boxtimes_{[1]}[1;t^{x}]\right)\bigcupop\displaylimits_{\coprodop\displaylimits_{i\in E}[0;u_{0}^{i}]}[1;u]\to\Upsilon_{k,l}

is a Segal extension.

Proof.

If we define
A^{\prime}
by the pushout square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\coprodop\displaylimits_{i\in\pi_{0}(u_{0})}{[1;v_{00}^{i}\rightsquigarrow v_{11}^{i}]})\cup_{[0;u_{0}]}[1;u]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\coprodop\displaylimits_{i\in\pi_{0}(u_{0})}{[2;v_{00}^{i}\rightsquigarrow v_{10}^{i}\rightsquigarrow v_{11}^{i}]})\cup_{[0;u_{0}]}[1;u]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\coprodop\displaylimits_{i\in\pi_{0}(u_{0})}{[2;v_{00}^{i}\rightsquigarrow v_{01}^{i}\rightsquigarrow v_{11}^{i}]})\cup_{[0;u_{0}]}[1;u]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A^{\prime},}

then the canonical inclusion
A^{\prime}\to A
is a Segal extension by the decomposition formula of
Section

7.2
. Note that the surjective map
([1+k]\times[1+l])*[0]\to([1]\times[1])*[0]
is exponentiable in
\mathrm{Cat}
. Thus it follows from
Section

4.1
that

A^{\prime}_{k,l}:=(([1+k]\times[1+l])*[0])\boxtimes_{([1]\times[1])*[0]}A^{\prime}\to A_{k,l}

is a Segal extension as well. The map from the lemma is obtained after pushing out along
\coprodop\displaylimits_{i\in\pi_{0}(u_{0})}B^{i}_{k,l}\to\coprodop\displaylimits_{i\in\pi_{0}(u_{0})}C^{i}_{k,l}
.
∎

Lemma 8.19
.

There is a Segal extension

T_{k}[u\circ^{E}(t^{i})^{i\in E}]\to\Upsilon_{k,k}

that is natural in
[k]\in\Delta
.

Proof.

There is an obvious commutative square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T_{k}[1]={[1+k]*[0]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[1]}*[0]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{([1+k]\times[1+k])*[0]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces([1]\times[1])*[0],}

where the vertical maps are induced by the diagonals.
This gives rise to a natural map
T_{k}[1]\boxtimes_{[2]}[2;u\star^{E}(t^{i})^{i\in E}]\to(([1+k]\times[1+k])*[0])\boxtimes_{([1]\times[1])*[0]}[2;v_{00}\rightsquigarrow v_{11}\rightsquigarrow u_{1}]
, where we use the notation from
Section

8.4
. This map factors through the defining pushout of
T_{k}[u\circ^{E}(t^{i})^{i\in E}]
to give the natural map
T_{k}[u\circ^{E}(t^{i})^{i\in E}]\to\Upsilon_{k,k}
from the statement.
The Segal extension of
Section

8.4
factors through this natural map via the inclusion
(\coprodop\displaylimits_{i\in E}[1+k]\boxtimes_{[1]}[1;t^{i}])\cup_{\coprodop\displaylimits_{i\in E}[0;u_{0}^{i}]}[1;u]\to T_{k}[u\circ^{E}(t^{i})^{i\in E}]
, which is also a Segal extension for similar reasons.
∎

Lemma 8.20
.

Let
w
denote the composite active arrow

v_{01}=(u\star^{F}(t^{i})^{i\in F})_{0}\rightsquigarrow u_{0}\rightsquigarrow u_{1}.

There is a commutative square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[1;w]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{T_{k}[u\circ^{F}(t^{i})^{i\in F}]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{T_{l}[w\circ^{x}t^{x}]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Upsilon_{k,l}}}

that is natural in
[k],[l]\in\Delta
. The associated cogap map of this square is a Segal extension.

Proof.

Let
A^{\prime\prime}
be the
\Omega[\mathscr{O}]
-space defined by the pushout square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[1;w]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[1;v_{01}\rightsquigarrow u_{1}]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[2;v_{01}\rightsquigarrow v_{11}\rightsquigarrow u_{1}]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[2;u\star^{F}(t^{i})^{i\in F}]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[2;w\star^{x}t^{x}]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[2;v_{00}\rightsquigarrow v_{01}\rightsquigarrow u_{1}]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A^{\prime\prime}.}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces=}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces=}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces=}

There is a canonical inclusion
A^{\prime\prime}\to[3;v_{00}\rightsquigarrow v_{01}\rightsquigarrow v_{11}\rightsquigarrow u_{1}]\to A
. This map induces the commutative square of the lemma. The latter assertion can be deduced from
Section

8.4
again.
∎

Lemma 8.21
.

Let
p:X\to Y
be a map between complete Segal
\Omega[\mathscr{O}]
-spaces. Suppose that for every active arrow
u:u_{0}\rightsquigarrow u_{1}
ending in an elementary, and every active arrow
t:t_{0}\rightsquigarrow t_{1}=u_{0}^{i}
, with
i\in\pi_{0}(u_{0})
, the morphism

\mathop{colim}\displaylimits_{[k]\in\Delta^{\mathrm{op}}}\mathrm{Hom}_{/Y}(T_{k}[u\circ^{i}t],X)\to\mathrm{Hom}_{/Y}([1;\underline{t}\rightsquigarrow u_{1}],X)

is an equivalence. Then
p
satisfies
Item

1
.

Proof.

Let
u:u_{0}\rightsquigarrow u_{1}
be an active arrow ending with an elementary. Suppose that
t:t_{0}\rightsquigarrow u_{0}
is an active arrow. Then we must show that the map

(14)

\mathop{colim}\displaylimits_{[k]\in\Delta^{\mathrm{op}}}\mathrm{Hom}_{/Y}(T_{k}[1]\boxtimes_{[2]}[2;t_{0}\rightsquigarrow u_{0}\rightsquigarrow u_{1}],X)\to\mathrm{Hom}_{/Y}([1;t_{0}\rightsquigarrow u_{1}],X)

is an equivalence.

Suppose first that
\pi_{0}(u_{0})
is empty. On account of condition (4c) of
Section

7.2
, the map
t_{0}\rightsquigarrow u_{0}
is an equivalence. Thus the above map is then induced by applying
(-)\boxtimes_{[1]}[1;u]
to the natural sections
[1]\to[1+k+1]
in
\Delta_{/[1]}
, where
[1+k+1]\to[1]
carries only the maximal element to
1
. This (augmented) cosimplicial object admits a splitting, so
Equation

14
is an equivalence.

Henceforth, we may assume that
\pi_{0}(u_{0})
is non-empty. Suppose that
E
is a non-empty subset of
\pi_{0}(u_{0})
and consider the collection of actives
(t^{i}:t_{0}^{i}\rightsquigarrow u_{0}^{i})^{i\in E}
. We will show that for every
f:[u\circ^{E}(t^{i})^{i\in E}]\to Y
, the map

\mathop{colim}\displaylimits_{[k]\in\Delta^{\mathrm{op}}}\mathrm{Hom}_{/Y}(T_{k}[u\circ^{E}(t^{i})^{i\in E}],X)\to\mathrm{Hom}_{/Y}([1;\underline{t}\rightsquigarrow u_{1}],X)

is an equivalence,
where
\underline{t}
is defined as in
Section

8.4
. The maximal case that
E=\pi_{0}(u_{0})
recovers precisely
Equation

14
(see
Section

8.4
).
Note that the case
|E|=1
corresponds to the hypothesis. We now proceed by induction on the cardinality of
E
.

Suppose now that
|E|>1
, and let
x\in E
. We define
F:=E\setminus\{x\}
and we suppose that the claim holds for
F
. Consider the bisimplicial
\Omega[\mathscr{O}]
-space
\Upsilon_{k,l}
of
Section

8.4
. Note that we can view
f
as a map
\Upsilon_{0,0}\to Y
by
Section

8.4
. We have a natural map
[1;v_{00}\rightsquigarrow u_{1}]=[1;\underline{t}\rightsquigarrow u_{1}]\to\Upsilon_{k,l}
. By
Section

8.4
, we must show that the induced map

\mathop{colim}\displaylimits_{[k]\in\Delta^{\mathrm{op}}}\mathrm{Hom}_{/Y}(\Upsilon_{k,k},Y)\to\mathrm{Hom}_{/Y}([1;v_{00}\rightsquigarrow u_{1}],Y)

is an equivalence.

We will now use the presentation of
Section

8.4
to obtain a cartesian square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{Hom}_{/Y}(\Upsilon_{k,l},X)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{Hom}_{/Y}(T_{k}[w\circ^{x}t^{x}],X)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{Hom}_{/Y}(T_{l}[u\circ^{F}(t^{i})^{i\in F}],X)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{Hom}_{/Y}([1;w],X)}}

where
w:v_{01}\rightsquigarrow u_{1}
is the composite of the sequence
u\star^{F}(t^{i})^{i\in F}
.
Using the assumption and the induction hypothesis, we then deduce that the natural maps
[1;v_{00}\rightsquigarrow u_{1}]\to\Upsilon_{k,l}

induce an equivalence

\displaystyle\mathop{colim}\displaylimits_{([k],[l])\in\Delta^{\mathrm{op}}\times\Delta^{\mathrm{op}}}\mathrm{Hom}_{/Y}(\Upsilon_{k,l},X)

\displaystyle\xrightarrow{\simeq}\mathop{colim}\displaylimits_{[k]\in\Delta^{\mathrm{op}}}\mathrm{Hom}_{/Y}(T_{k}[w\circ^{x}t^{x}],X)

\displaystyle\xrightarrow{\simeq}\mathrm{Hom}_{/Y}([1;v_{00}\rightsquigarrow u_{1}],Y).

As
\Delta^{\mathrm{op}}
is sifted, the desired conclusion follows.
∎

Proof of
Theorem

8.1
.

Suppose that
p:X\to Y
is an exponentiable map between complete
\Omega[\mathscr{O}]
-spaces. Let
u_{0}\rightsquigarrow u_{1}
be an active arrow ending in an elementary. Suppose that
t:t_{0}\rightsquigarrow u_{0}^{i}
is an active map with
i\in\pi_{0}(u_{0})
. Then we consider the following diagram of pullback squares:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{X^{\prime\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{X^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Gamma[u\circ^{i}t]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[u\circ^{i}t]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces v}

We claim that
v
is a Segal extension. To this end, let
L:\mathrm{PSh}(\Omega[\mathscr{O}])\to\mathrm{CSeg}(\Omega[\mathscr{O}])
denote the reflection.
The map
[1;t_{0}^{i}\rightsquigarrow u_{0}^{i}]\cup_{[0;u_{0}^{i}]}[1;u]\simeq\Gamma[u\circ^{i}t]\to[u\circ^{i}t]
is a Segal extension and
[u\circ^{i}t]
is complete Segal; see
Section

8.2
.
Applying
L
, we obtain the colimit expression
[1;t_{0}^{i}]\cup_{[0;u_{0}^{i}]}[1;u]\simeq[u\circ^{i}t]
in
\mathrm{CSeg}(\Omega[\mathscr{O}])
. Note that all objects in this colimit expression lie in
\mathrm{CSeg}(\Omega[\mathscr{O}])
by
Section

8.2
. As
p^{*}:\mathrm{CSeg}(\Omega[\mathscr{O}])_{/Y}\to\mathrm{CSeg}(\Omega[\mathscr{O}])_{/X}
is cocontinuous, we thus deduce that
v
is carried to an equivalence by
L
. Thus
Section

4.1
implies that
v
is a complete Segal extension.

Now, using
Section

8.3
and reasoning similarly as in
Section

5.4
, we have a factorization
X^{\prime\prime}\to RX^{\prime}\to X^{\prime}
of
v
into a complete Segal extension followed by a complete Segal fibration. Thus
RX^{\prime}\to X^{\prime}
must be an equivalence. By
Section

8.3
, the map
p
fulfills the hypothesis of
Section

8.4
and thus verifies
Item

1
.
∎

8.5.
Examples

We now highlight some sample applications of
B
.

Example 8.22
.

By
Theorem

8.1
(or
Section

5.4
) the conditions of
Section

5.5
for a map in
\mathrm{Algad}(\Delta^{\mathrm{op},\natural})
to be exponentiable are also necessary.
That is, a map
\mathscr{P}\to\mathscr{Q}
of virtual double categories is exponentiable if and only if the following conditions hold:

(1)

the functor
\mathscr{P}_{0}\to\mathscr{Q}_{0}
is exponentiable in
\mathrm{Cat}
, and

(2)

for any
t\colon[2]\to\mathscr{Q}^{\mathrm{act}}
such that
t(2)
lies over
[1]
, the base-change
\mathscr{P}^{\mathrm{act}}\times_{\mathscr{Q}^{\mathrm{act}}}[2]\to[2]
is exponentiable in
\mathrm{Cat}
.

Example 8.23
.

Let
\mathscr{O}=\mathbb{F}_{*}^{\flat}
be the pattern describing operads. Suppose that
f\colon\mathscr{P}\to\mathscr{Q}
is a map between operads.
Then we claim that
f
is exponentiable if and only if the equivalent conditions (1) and (2) of
Section

5.5
hold. As explained in
Section

7.4
,
\mathbb{F}_{*}^{\flat}
is not robust, so we cannot directly apply
Theorem

8.1
.

However, note that there is an inclusion
\phi\colon\mathbb{F}_{*}\to\mathrm{Span}(\mathbb{F})
as the wide subcategory of
\mathrm{Span}(\mathbb{F})
containing the spans whose backward arrows are injective; concretely, a morphism
f\colon X\sqcup\{*\}\to Y\sqcup\{*\}
is sent by
f
to the span

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{f^{-1}(Y)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

By
[
5
, Corollary B]
, pullback along
\phi
induces an equivalence

\phi^{*}\colon\mathrm{Algad}(\mathrm{Span}(\mathbb{F})^{\flat})\xrightarrow{\simeq}\mathrm{Algad}(\mathbb{F}_{*}^{\flat})=\mathrm{Op},

where
\mathrm{Span}(\mathbb{F})^{\flat}
has the pattern structure from
Section

2.1
.
Alternatively, this equivalence follows from the fact that the tree categories of
\mathrm{Span}(\mathbb{F})^{\flat}
and
\mathbb{F}_{*}^{\flat}
agree, see
Section

3.1
.
Since
\mathrm{Span}(\mathbb{F})^{\flat}
is robust by
Section

7.4
, it follows that items (1) and (2) are necessary when
\mathbb{F}_{*}
is replaced by
\mathrm{Span}(\mathbb{F})
.
Because the functor
\phi\colon\mathbb{F}_{*}\to\mathrm{Span}(\mathbb{F})
is an equivalence on active morphisms, it follows that (1) and (2) are also necessary for
\mathbb{F}_{*}^{\flat}
.

Example 8.24
.

The inclusion
\phi\colon\mathbb{F}_{*}\hookrightarrow\mathrm{Span}(\mathbb{F})
from the previous example also induces an equivalence

\mathrm{Algad}(\mathrm{Span}(\mathbb{F})^{\natural})\simeq\mathrm{Algad}(\mathbb{F}^{\natural}_{*}).

This follows by checking the conditions of
[
5
, Theorem A]
, but can also be deduced as follows:
By
Section

3.1
, the inclusion
\phi
yields an equivalence
\Omega[\mathbb{F}_{*}^{\natural}]\simeq\Omega[\mathrm{Span}(\mathbb{F})^{\natural}]
.
This is an equivalence of algebraic patterns for the pattern structure of
Section

3.1
, hence we obtain an equivalence

\mathrm{CSeg}(\Omega[\mathrm{Span}(\mathbb{F})^{\natural}])\simeq\mathrm{CSeg}(\Omega[\mathbb{F}^{\natural}_{*}])

by
Section

4.1
.

Theorem

4.21
then shows that pulling back along
\phi
gives an equivalence between categories of algebrads.
Because the functor
\phi\colon\mathbb{F}_{*}\to\mathrm{Span}(\mathbb{F})
is an equivalence on active morphisms, it follows that conditions (1) and (2) of
Section

5.5
are both necessary and sufficient for a map in
\mathrm{Algad}(\mathbb{F}_{*}^{\natural})
to be exponentiable.
In particular, combining this example with the previous one, it follows that a map of operads is exponentiable in the category of operads if and only if it is exponentiable in the category of generalized operads.

Example 8.25
.

Let
G
be a finite group and consider the pattern
\mathrm{Span}(\mathbb{F}_{G})^{\flat}
from
Section

2.1
describing
G
-operads. This is robust by
Section

7.4
. Thus conditions (1) and (2) of
Section

5.5
are also necessary for a map of
G
-operads to be exponentiable.

As explained in
Section

5.5
, Nardin–Shah
[
28
]
modelled
G
-operads as algebrad for a different pattern
\underline{\mathbb{F}}_{G,*}
and showed that a map
\mathscr{P}\to\mathscr{Q}
between such algebrads is exponentiable if
\mathscr{P}^{\mathrm{act}}\to\mathscr{Q}^{\mathrm{act}}
is exponentiable in
\mathrm{Cat}
.
While
\underline{\mathbb{F}}_{G,*}
is not robust, we can show that their condition is necessary by comparing the pattern to
\underline{\mathbb{F}}_{G,*}
to
\mathrm{Span}(\mathbb{F}_{G})^{\flat}
.
We first briefly recall the equivalence of
\mathrm{Algad}(\underline{\mathbb{F}}_{G,*})
with
\mathrm{Algad}(\mathrm{Span}(\mathbb{F}_{G})^{\flat})
proved in
[
5
, Corollary B]
.

The category
\underline{\mathbb{F}}_{G,*}
is defined as the cocartesian unstraightening
p\colon\underline{\mathbb{F}}_{G,*}\to\mathrm{Orb}_{G}^{\mathrm{op}}
of the functor

\mathrm{Orb}_{G}^{\mathrm{op}}\to\mathrm{Cat};\quad G/H\mapsto\mathbb{F}_{H,*},

where
\mathrm{Orb}_{G}^{\mathrm{op}}
is the category of transitive
G
-sets and
\mathbb{F}_{H,*}
the category of pointed finite
H
-sets.
A map in
\underline{\mathbb{F}}_{G,*}
is inert if it is
p
-cocartesian and active if it lies over an equivalence in
\mathrm{\mathrm{Orb}}_{G}
.
4
4

4

In
[
28
]
, a slightly larger class of actives is used, but their inerts and actives don’t form a factorization system. Moreover, their exponentiability criterion
[
28
, Definition 3.1.1]
uses the actives we define here.

There is a functor
\phi\colon\underline{\mathbb{F}}_{G,*}\to\mathrm{Span}(\mathbb{F}_{G})
that sends a pointed
H
-set
X\sqcup\{*\}
to the
G
-set
\mathrm{ind}_{H}^{G}X
. By
[
5
, Proposition 5.2.14]
, pullback along
\phi
induces an equivalence

\phi^{*}\colon\mathrm{Algad}(\mathrm{Span}(\mathbb{F}_{G}))\to\mathrm{Algad}(\underline{\mathbb{F}}_{G,*}).

The inclusion
\{G/G\}\hookrightarrow\mathrm{Orb}^{\mathrm{op}}_{G}
induces an inclusion
\mathbb{F}_{G}\hookrightarrow\underline{\mathbb{F}}_{G,*}^{\mathrm{act}}
whose composition with
\phi^{\mathrm{act}}\colon\underline{\mathbb{F}}_{G,*}^{\mathrm{act}}\to\mathrm{Span}(\mathbb{F}_{G})^{\mathrm{act}}=\mathbb{F}_{G}
is the identity.
We therefore see that given a map
\mathscr{Q}\to\mathscr{P}
in
\mathrm{Algad}(\mathrm{Span}(\mathbb{F}_{G}))
, the functor
\mathscr{Q}^{\mathrm{act}}\to\mathscr{P}^{\mathrm{act}}
is exponentiable in
\mathrm{Cat}
if and only if
(\phi^{*}\mathscr{P})^{\mathrm{act}}\to(\phi^{*}\mathscr{Q})^{\mathrm{act}}
is exponentiable in
\mathrm{Cat}
.
This is precisely the exponentiability condition of
[
28
, Corollary 3.1.5]
.

Example 8.26
.

The algebraic patterns
\Theta_{n}
and
\Delta^{\times n,\mathrm{op},\natural}
from
Section

7.4
and
Section

7.4
are (atomically) robust, so
Theorem

5.5
gives a complete characterization of the exponentiable morphisms in
\mathrm{Algad}(\Theta_{n})
and
\mathrm{Algad}(\Delta^{\times n,\mathrm{op},\natural})
.

9.
Examples of exponentiable maps

We will now give concrete examples of exponentiable maps between algebrads using
A
.
We start with observing that any Segal
\mathscr{O}
-category
\mathscr{O}\to\mathrm{Cat}
is exponentiable when viewed as an
\mathscr{O}
-algebrad.
Secondly, we consider the case where every object of
\mathscr{O}
is elementary.
In this case
\mathrm{Algad}(\mathscr{O})\simeq\mathrm{Cocart}^{\mathrm{int}}(\mathscr{O})
, and we obtain a complete characterization of the exponentiable morphisms in
\mathrm{Cocart}^{\mathrm{int}}(\mathscr{O})
.
We then give a very small example of an exponentiable morphism between virtual double categories that does not fall in the previous classes.
This is also the counterexample mentioned in
Section

1.4
.
Finally, we will consider the example of the (virtual) cospan double category
\mathbb{C}\mathrm{ospan}(\mathscr{C})
of a category
\mathscr{C}
.
If
\mathscr{C}
admits pushouts, then this is a double category and hence it is exponentiable within the category of virtual double categories.
We will show that if
\mathscr{C}
does not admit pushouts, then
\mathbb{C}\mathrm{ospan}(\mathscr{C})
still exists as a virtual double category, and in that it is always exponentiable.

9.1.
Segal
\mathscr{O}
-categories

Recall that Segal
\mathscr{O}
-categories (
Section

2.1
) can be viewed as particular examples of
\mathscr{O}
-algebrads via unstraightening. It turns out that these are always exponentiable in
\mathrm{Algad}(\mathscr{O})
:

Proposition 9.1
.

Let
\mathscr{O}
be an algebraic pattern and
X\colon\mathscr{O}\to\mathrm{Cat}
a Segal
\mathscr{O}
-category.
Then its unstraightening
\smallint X\to\mathscr{O}
is an exponentiable object in
\mathrm{Algad}(\mathscr{O})
.

Proof.

By
[
4
, Lemma 3.2.1]
,
\smallint X\to\mathscr{O}
is exponentiable in
\mathrm{Cat}
.
In particular, it satisfies the criterion
Item

1
from
A
.
∎

Example 9.2
.

The category of double categories is equivalent to
\mathrm{DblCat}=\mathrm{Seg}(\Delta^{\mathrm{op},\natural},\mathrm{Cat})
.
In particular, any double category, when viewed as an object in the category
\mathrm{VirtDblCat}=\mathrm{Algad}(\Delta^{\mathrm{op},\natural})
of virtual double categories, is exponentiable.
This is an
\infty
-categorical version of
[
1
, Theorem 3.9]
.

Warning 9.3
.

It follows from
Section

9.1
that any two Segal
\mathscr{O}
-categories
X
and
Y
admit an exponential object
[X,Y]
in
\mathrm{Algad}(\mathscr{O})
.
However,
[X,Y]\to\mathscr{O}
is generally not a cocartesian fibration and hence not a Segal
\mathscr{O}
-category.
Even if it is a Segal
\mathscr{O}
-category, it is usually not an exponential object in
\mathrm{Seg}(\mathscr{O},\mathrm{Cat})
since the morphisms in
\mathrm{Algad}(\mathscr{O})
and
\mathrm{Seg}(\mathscr{O},\mathrm{Cat})
don’t agree.
For example, any double category
X
is exponentiable in both
\mathrm{DblCat}
and
\mathrm{VirtDblCat}
, but the exponential objects generally don’t agree:
Given a double category
Y
, the objects of the exponential object
[[1]_{h},Y]_{\mathrm{DblCat}}
in
\mathrm{DblCat}
are horizontal arrows in
Y
, while the objects of the exponential object
[[1]_{h},Y]_{\mathrm{VirtDblCat}}
are pairs of objects in
Y
(cf.
Section

6
). Here
[1]_{h}
denotes the free-living double category containing a horizontal arrow.

Example 9.4
.

Let
\mathscr{O}
be an algebraic pattern. On account of
Section

4.1
there is a composite inclusion of (non-full) subcategories

\displaystyle\mathrm{Algad}(\mathscr{O})

\displaystyle\simeq\mathrm{CSeg}(\Omega[\mathscr{O}])\hookrightarrow\mathrm{Seg}(\Omega[\mathscr{O}])=\mathrm{Seg}(\Omega[\mathscr{O}]^{\mathrm{op},\natural},\mathscr{S})

\displaystyle\hookrightarrow\mathrm{Seg}(\Omega[\mathscr{O}]^{\mathrm{op},\natural},\mathrm{Cat})\hookrightarrow\mathrm{Algad}(\Omega[\mathscr{O}]^{\mathrm{op},\natural}).

On account of
Section

9.1
, the inclusion carries each
\mathscr{O}
-algebrad to an exponentiable
\Omega[\mathscr{O}]^{\mathrm{op},\natural}
-algebrad (but beware of
Section

9.1
).

By iterating the tree construction (see
Section

3.1
), we now produce a sequence of inclusions

\mathrm{Algad}(\mathscr{O})\to\mathrm{Algad}(\Omega[\mathscr{O}]^{\mathrm{op},\natural})\to\mathrm{Algad}({}^{2}[\mathscr{O}]^{\mathrm{op},\natural})\to\dotsb,

whose images consist of exponentiable objects.

9.2.
Exponentiable morphisms in
\mathrm{Cocart}^{\mathrm{int}}(\mathscr{O})

Let
\mathscr{O}
be a category with a factorization system
(\mathscr{O}^{\mathrm{int}},\mathscr{O}^{\mathrm{act}})
.
Then we can make
\mathscr{O}
into an algebraic pattern by declaring that every object is elementary.
By
Section

2.1
, it follows that
\mathrm{Cocart}^{\mathrm{int}}(\mathscr{O})\simeq\mathrm{Algad}(\mathscr{O})
, while
\mathscr{O}
is robust by
Section

7.4
.
In particular, we obtain the following complete characterization of the exponentiable morphisms in
\mathrm{Cocart}^{\mathrm{int}}(\mathscr{O})
.

Proposition 9.5
.

Let
\mathscr{O}
be a category with a factorization system
(\mathscr{O}^{\mathrm{int}},\mathscr{O}^{\mathrm{act}})
.
Then a morphism
\mathscr{P}\to\mathscr{Q}
is exponentiable in
\mathrm{Cocart}^{\mathrm{int}}(\mathscr{O})
precisely if the functor

\mathscr{P}^{\mathrm{act}}\coloneqq\mathscr{P}\times_{\mathscr{O}}\mathscr{O}^{\mathrm{act}}\to\mathscr{Q}\times_{\mathscr{O}}\mathscr{O}^{\mathrm{act}}\eqqcolon\mathscr{Q}^{\mathrm{act}}

is exponentiable in
\mathrm{Cat}
.

Proof.

Since every object in
\mathscr{Q}
lies over an elementary object in
\mathscr{O}
, the condition from
Theorem

5.5
is equivalent to the Conduché criterion for the functor
\mathscr{P}^{\mathrm{act}}\to\mathscr{Q}^{\mathrm{act}}
from
[
4
, Lemma 2.2.8]
.
∎

Example 9.6
.

Suppose that
\mathscr{C}
is a category. We may then apply
Section

9.2
to the factorization system whose left class is given by all maps in
\mathscr{C}
. Then it follows from
Section

9.2
that a map
F\to G
in
\mathrm{Fun}(\mathscr{C},\mathrm{Cat})
is exponentiable if and only if each component
Fc\to Gc
is exponentiable.

9.3.
A non-trivial exponentiable map between virtual double categories

We will discuss a toy example of an exponentiable map in
\mathrm{VirtDblCat}\simeq\mathrm{Algad}(\Delta^{\mathrm{op},\natural})
, where
\Delta^{\mathrm{op},\natural}
is the algebraic pattern defined in
Section

2.1
. This is also the counterexample mentioned in
Section

1.4
.

Construction 9.7
.

We will write
(s,t\colon\{01,12\}\rightrightarrows\{0,1,2\})\in\mathrm{Fun}(\mathbb{G},\mathrm{Cat})
for the underlying graph of
[0;[2]]
(see
Section

6
). We define

f\colon A\to B\coloneqq[2;[2]=[2]=[2]]

to be the map of unary virtual double categories that is classified by the map of graphs

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[2]\times\{01,12\}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[2]\times\{01,12\}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[3]\times\{0,1,2\}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[2]\times\{0,1,2\}}.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{id}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d_{1}\times s}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d_{2}\times t}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{id}\times s}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{id}\times t}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s_{1}\times\mathrm{id}}

The map
f
is unique in the sense that the
total vertical pasting
of
A
, given by the unique inclusion
[1;[2]=[2]]\to A
, is carried by
f
to the
total vertical pasting

[1;[2]=[2]]\to B
of
B
, which is classified by the tuple of maps
(d_{1}\colon[1]\to[2],\mathrm{id}_{[2]}\colon[2]\to[2])
in
\Delta
.

The following shows that it is necessary in
Theorem

5.2
to only restrict to trees
[2;t]
and that moving to
forests

\left<2;t\right>
would be too broad.

Proposition 9.8
.

The map
f\colon A\to B
is exponentiable in
\mathrm{VirtDblCat}
. However, for the identity map
[2;[2]=[2]=[2]]\to B
, the similarly defined comparison map of
Theorem

5.2
is not an equivalence.

Proof.

For the first assertion, we note that there is an equivalence

\iota\colon\mathrm{Fun}(\mathbb{G},\mathrm{Cat})_{/\Gamma B}\xrightarrow{\simeq}\mathrm{VirtDblCat}_{/B}

on account of
Section

6
and
Section

6
(cf.
Section

6
). Hence, it suffices to show that
\Gamma f\colon\Gamma A\to\Gamma B
is an exponentiable map in
\mathrm{Fun}(\mathbb{G},\mathrm{Cat})
. This can be checked pointwise, and follows from the fact that
s_{1}\colon[3]\to[2]
and
\mathrm{id}_{[2]}\colon[2]\to[2]
are exponentiable in
\mathrm{Cat}
.

For the second assertion, we note that the total vertical pasting of
B
extends to the identity
[2;[2]=[2]=[2]]\to B
. By construction of
f
, the total vertical pasting of
A
lifts the total vertical pasting of
B
, yet the total vertical pasting of
A
cannot be extended along
[1;[2]=[2]]\to[2;[2]=[2]=[2]]
in a compatible way.
∎

Remark 9.9
.

When viewing
A
and
B
as
\Delta^{\mathrm{op},\natural}
-algebrads in the sense of
Section

2.1
,
Section

9.3
says that while
f\colon A\to B
satisfies the criterion of
Theorem

5.5
, the functor on actives
A^{\mathrm{act}}\to B^{\mathrm{act}}
is not exponentiable in
\mathrm{Cat}
.
Namely, it shows that there exists a map
\alpha
in
A^{\mathrm{act}}
together with a factorization
f(\alpha)=\beta\circ\gamma
in
B^{\mathrm{act}}
such that
\beta
and
\gamma
do not lift to
A^{\mathrm{act}}
.

9.4.
The virtual cospan double category

We will now construct, for any category
\mathscr{C}
, its
virtual double category of cospans

\mathbb{C}\mathrm{ospan}^{\mathrm{virt}}(\mathscr{C})
and show that it is exponentiable in
\mathrm{VirtDblCat}
.

Recall that for a category
\mathscr{C}
that admits pushouts, its cospan double category
\mathbb{C}\mathrm{ospan}(\mathscr{C})
is constructed by first constructing a bigger simplicial category

\overline{\mathbb{C}\mathrm{ospan}}(\mathscr{C})\colon\Delta^{\mathrm{op}}\to\mathrm{Cat};\quad\overline{\mathbb{C}\mathrm{ospan}}(\mathscr{C})_{n}=\mathrm{Fun}(\Delta^{\mathrm{int}}_{/[n]},\mathscr{C})

and then restricting, for every
n
, to the full subcategory
\mathbb{C}\mathrm{ospan}(\mathscr{C})_{n}\subset\overline{\mathbb{C}\mathrm{ospan}}(\mathscr{C})_{n}
spanned by those functors
\Delta^{\mathrm{int}}_{/[n]}\to\mathscr{C}
that are left Kan extended from
\Delta^{\mathrm{el}}_{/[n]}
. For details, including the proof that this defines a double category, we refer the reader to
[
19
, §5]
where the dual case of spans is discussed.
Since
\Delta^{\mathrm{int}}_{/[1]}=\Delta^{\mathrm{el}}_{/[1]}\simeq(\bullet\rightarrow\bullet\leftarrow\bullet)
, we see that the horizontal morphisms of
\mathbb{C}\mathrm{ospan}(\mathscr{C})
are indeed cospans.

If
\mathscr{C}
does not admit pushouts, then we embed
\mathscr{C}
into
\mathrm{PSh}(\mathscr{C}^{\mathrm{op}})^{\mathrm{op}}
via the Yoneda embedding.
5
5

5

We may always assume
\mathscr{C}
is small by considering presheaves in a larger universe.

Then
\mathrm{PSh}(\mathscr{C}^{\mathrm{op}})^{\mathrm{op}}
admits pushouts and the inclusion
\mathscr{C}\hookrightarrow\mathrm{PSh}(\mathscr{C}^{\mathrm{op}})^{\mathrm{op}}
preserves all pushouts that exist in
\mathscr{C}
.
We will define the virtual double category
\mathbb{C}\mathrm{ospan}^{\mathrm{virt}}(\mathscr{C})
as a subobject of the double category
\mathbb{C}\mathrm{ospan}(\mathrm{PSh}(\mathscr{C}^{\mathrm{op}})^{\mathrm{op}})
.
In what follows, we write
\widehat{\mathscr{C}}
for
\mathrm{PSh}(\mathscr{C}^{\mathrm{op}})^{\mathrm{op}}

Construction 9.10
.

Let
\mathscr{C}
be a category and write
\mathscr{E}\coloneqq\smallint\mathbb{C}\mathrm{ospan}(\widehat{\mathscr{C}})\to\Delta^{\mathrm{op}}
for the unstraightening of the double category
\mathbb{C}\mathrm{ospan}(\widehat{\mathscr{C}})
.
Then we may identify the fiber of
\mathscr{E}
over
[0]
with
\widehat{\mathscr{C}}
and over
[1]
with the category of cospans
\mathrm{Fun}(\bullet\rightarrow\bullet\leftarrow\bullet,\widehat{\mathscr{C}})
.
Using the Segal condition, we may identify the objects in the fiber
\mathbb{C}\mathrm{ospan}(\widehat{\mathscr{C}})_{n}
over
[n]
with iterated cospans

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{c^{\prime}_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{c^{\prime}_{n}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{c_{0}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{c_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{c_{n-1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{c_{n}}}

in
\widehat{\mathscr{C}}
.
We define
\mathscr{E}^{\prime}
as the full subcategory of
\mathscr{E}
spanned by those iterated cospans for which the objects
c_{0},\ldots,c_{n},c^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,c^{\prime}_{n}
all lie in the image of
\mathscr{C}\hookrightarrow\widehat{\mathscr{C}}
.
We leave it to the reader to verify that
\mathscr{E}^{\prime}\to\Delta^{\mathrm{op}}
defines a
\Delta^{\mathrm{op},\natural}
-algebrad, i.e. a virtual double category.
We will denote this virtual double category by
\mathbb{C}\mathrm{ospan}^{\mathrm{virt}}(\mathscr{C})
.

Remark 9.11
.

Suppose that
\mathscr{C}
admits pushouts. Since the inclusion
\mathscr{C}\hookrightarrow\widehat{\mathscr{C}}
preserves all pushouts that exist in
\mathscr{C}
, it follows that
\mathbb{C}\mathrm{ospan}^{\mathrm{virt}}(\mathscr{C})\simeq\smallint\mathbb{C}\mathrm{ospan}(\mathscr{C})
in this case.

Remark 9.12
.

One can in fact show that the construction of
\mathbb{C}\mathrm{ospan}^{\mathrm{virt}}(\mathscr{C})
is independent of the choice of embedding of
\mathscr{C}
into a category that admits pushouts.

We will now show that
\mathbb{C}\mathrm{ospan}^{\mathrm{virt}}(\mathscr{C})
is always exponentiable in the category of virtual double categories, even if
\mathscr{C}
does not admit pushouts.
The main ingredient is the following lemma.

Lemma 9.13
.

Let
\mathscr{O}
be an algebraic pattern and
p\colon\mathscr{P}\to\mathscr{O}
an algebrad.
Suppose that
\mathscr{P}
admits
p
-cartesian lifts for every active morphism
x\rightsquigarrow e
in
\mathscr{O}
whose target is elementary.
Then
\mathscr{P}
is exponentiable in
\mathrm{Algad}(\mathscr{O})
.

Proof.

We will check the criterion
Item

1
from
A
, so let
h\colon x\rightsquigarrow y
and
g\colon y\rightsquigarrow e
be active morphisms in
\mathscr{O}
with
e
elementary, and let
f\colon\overline{x}\to\overline{e}
be a lift of
g\circ h
in
\mathscr{P}
.
Choose a
p
-cartesian lift
\overline{g}\colon\overline{y}\to\overline{e}
of
g
; then there exists a unique lift
\overline{h}\colon\overline{x}\to\overline{y}
such that
\overline{g}\circ\overline{h}=f
.
An argument dual to
[
4
, Lemma 3.2.1]
shows that
\overline{x}\overset{\overline{h}}{\rightsquigarrow}\overline{y}\overset{\overline{g}}{\rightsquigarrow}\overline{e}

is a terminal object of
\mathrm{Fact}(f\mid g\circ h)
, hence this category is weakly contractible.
∎

Proposition 9.14
.

Let
\mathscr{C}
be any category.
Then
\mathbb{C}\mathrm{ospan}^{\mathrm{virt}}(\mathscr{C})
is an exponentiable virtual double category.

Proof.

We need to show that
\mathbb{C}\mathrm{ospan}^{\mathrm{virt}}(\mathscr{C})\to\Delta^{\mathrm{op}}
is exponentiable in
\mathrm{Algad}(\Delta^{\mathrm{op},\natural})
.
By construction,
\mathbb{C}\mathrm{ospan}^{\mathrm{virt}}(\mathscr{C})
is a subobject of
\smallint\mathbb{C}\mathrm{ospan}(\widehat{\mathscr{C}})
.
Let
\phi\colon[1]\rightsquigarrow[n]
be an active morphism in
\Delta
.
Then the cocartesian transport functor
\mathbb{C}\mathrm{ospan}(\widehat{\mathscr{C}})_{n}\to\mathbb{C}\mathrm{ospan}(\widehat{\mathscr{C}})_{1}
may be identified with the left Kan extension functor

\mathrm{LKan}_{\phi^{*}}\colon\mathrm{Fun}(\Delta^{\mathrm{el}}_{/[n]},\widehat{\mathscr{C}})\to\mathrm{Fun}(\Delta^{\mathrm{el}}_{/[1]},\widehat{\mathscr{C}})

along
\phi^{*}\colon\Delta^{\mathrm{el}}_{/[n]}\to\Delta^{\mathrm{el}}_{/[1]}
.
In particular, it admits a right adjoint which is given by the restriction functor

\mathrm{res}_{\phi^{*}}\colon\mathrm{Fun}(\Delta^{\mathrm{el}}_{/[1]},\widehat{\mathscr{C}})\to\mathrm{Fun}(\Delta^{\mathrm{el}}_{/[n]},\widehat{\mathscr{C}})

It follows as in the proof of (the dual of)
[
24
, Corollary 5.2.2.5]
that
\mathbb{C}\mathrm{ospan}(\widehat{\mathscr{C}})\to\Delta^{\mathrm{op}}
admits cartesian lifts of
\phi
.
Since
\mathrm{res}_{\phi^{*}}
takes the full subcategory
\mathbb{C}\mathrm{ospan}^{\mathrm{virt}}(\mathscr{C})_{1}
to
\mathbb{C}\mathrm{ospan}^{\mathrm{virt}}(\mathscr{C})_{n}
, it follows that
\mathbb{C}\mathrm{ospan}^{\mathrm{virt}}(\mathscr{C})\to\Delta^{\mathrm{op}}
admits cartesian lifts of
\phi
as well.
Since the only active morphism in
\Delta^{\mathrm{op}}
with target
[0]
is the identity, it follows that
\mathbb{C}\mathrm{ospan}^{\mathrm{virt}}(\mathscr{C})\to\Delta^{\mathrm{op}}
admits cartesian lifts of every active morphism whose target is elementary.
The result now follows from
Section

9.4
.
∎

Remark 9.15
.

The cartesian transport functor
\mathbb{C}\mathrm{ospan}^{\mathrm{virt}}(\mathscr{C})_{1}\to\mathbb{C}\mathrm{ospan}^{\mathrm{virt}}(\mathscr{C})_{n}
can explicitly be described as follows: it takes a cospan
c_{0}\rightarrow c_{1}\leftarrow c_{2}
to the iterated cospan

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{c_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{c_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{c_{0}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{c_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{c_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{c_{2}.}}
